# Milestone 3 — Dart-inspired ergonomics without Dart's OOP model

Dart has excellent expression and collection ergonomics. Flux should adopt or adapt the useful parts while rejecting class-centric semantics. Exact syntax is subject to Flux consistency and static-safety review.

Reference set (current Dart language documentation):

- https://dart.dev/language/operators
- https://dart.dev/language/collections
- https://dart.dev/language/patterns
- https://dart.dev/language/records
- https://dart.dev/null-safety/understanding-null-safety

## Null/optional-value ergonomics

Flux currently reserves `nil` specifically for `error`; a general optional-value design must therefore be explicit and must not weaken type safety.

- [x] Optional value types, using explicit `T?` syntax with non-optional types remaining the default. Bootstrap Copy optionals and ownership-tracked list optionals are implemented with typed `none`, injection, safe access/indexing, coalescing, binding, and flow promotion; unsupported non-Copy resource optionals remain explicitly rejected until owned-value transfer and borrow lifetimes are complete.
  - [x] Bootstrap explicit Copy optionals: `i64?`, `bool?`, `str?`, `error?`, recursively Copy struct/enum values, closed interface values, and first-class function values through aliases such as `Mapper?` are distinct static types; `none` is separate from error-only `nil`; ordinary inner values inject at typed optional bindings/assignments/calls/returns; aggregate/interface/function optionals lower by value through allocation-free tagged structs in dependency-safe native type order. Non-Copy `T[]?` values are admitted only as local/parameter ownership-tracked list descriptors for safe optional indexing: direct local injection moves the list descriptor, list optionals remain non-Copy/non-Send, cannot escape through returns or aggregate storage, and cannot be implicitly unwrapped/coalesced/cascaded until first-class borrow lifetimes exist. Other non-Copy resource-shaped optionals remain rejected; formatter/IR/semantic traversal preserve the syntax; remaining optional-aware operators stay tracked below.
- [x] Null/optional-aware access analogous to Dart `?.`: optional Copy-struct receivers support safe field access with single receiver evaluation, absence propagation, chained access, and flattened optional field results; non-optional receivers and untyped bare `none` are rejected. Struct literal/update fields now also lower ordinary Copy values into optional fields through the same explicit tagged representation instead of relying on invalid implicit C conversion. Non-Copy optional receivers remain sequenced behind owned-value transfer/borrow semantics.
- [x] Optional-aware indexing analogous to Dart `?[]`: `value?[index]` accepts ownership-tracked `T[]?` receivers with Copy elements, evaluates the receiver once, skips index evaluation when absent, preserves checked list indexing when present, flattens optional element results, and lowers allocation-free through a tagged optional list descriptor. Non-optional receivers, optional slices, and nested-list results are rejected; the latter remains behind first-class borrow lifetimes rather than manufacturing an untracked zero-copy view.
- [x] Coalescing analogous to Dart `??`: the optional left operand is evaluated exactly once, present values bypass the fallback, absent values lazily evaluate a type-compatible fallback, optional fallback chains preserve optionality, formatter/IR tooling preserve the operator, and native lowering remains allocation-free.
- [x] Coalescing assignment analogous to Dart `??=` where mutation is valid: mutable optional locals accept `value ??= fallback`, preserve the canonical ergonomic spelling through formatting, reuse the allocation-free optional/coalescing lowering, evaluate the fallback lazily only when the current value is absent, and retain ordinary static assignment/mutability checks.
- [x] Safe optional unwrapping via binding patterns: `if let value = optionalValue:` and `elif let ...` evaluate a Copy optional exactly once, enter the branch only when present, infer and bind the concrete inner type only in that branch, support `_` ignores, reject bare `none`, non-optional values, and binding shadowing, preserve formatter/semantic/CFG/typed-IR branch facts (including interface capability reachability after unwrapping), and lower directly through the optional tag without assertions or allocation. Direct presence-check flow promotion is completed separately below.
  - [x] Explicitly reborrowed optional list descriptors (`if let value = borrow optionalList:`) may be unwrapped into a branch-scoped immutable list view. The binding remains non-copy, preserves the normalized owner definition, rejects owner moves while the branch view is live, and lowers through the existing allocation-free optional-list representation; implicit unwraps and arbitrary non-copy optional resources remain gated by first-class ownership lifetimes.
- [x] Avoid routine runtime null assertions; bootstrap Flux intentionally has no postfix force-unwrap/null-assertion syntax. Attempting `value!` produces a targeted diagnostic that directs developers to safe `if let` unwrapping or lazy `??` fallback instead, so ordinary optional handling cannot silently introduce a runtime null assertion.
- [x] Flow-sensitive promotion after optional/type checks: stable immutable Copy-optionals checked directly with `value != none` / `none != value` promote to their concrete inner type only in the proven-present branch, while `value == none` / `none == value` promote in the corresponding `else` branch. Promotion is branch-local, lowers through an allocation-free snapshot/tag test plus native value shadow, and intentionally does not promote mutable `var` bindings whose value may change after the check.

## Indexing and collection conveniences

- [x] `value[index]` indexing with strict index typing for bootstrap lists, including Python-style negative indices with checked bounds.
- [x] Optional indexing (`value?[index]`) for optional list receivers, sharing the ownership-safe, lazy, flattened lowering above while retaining negative-index/bounds behavior from ordinary list indexing.
- [x] Checked `first` and `last` conveniences for compiler-known lists; empty access fails with an explicit Flux bounds diagnostic.
- [x] Checked `only` / exactly-one validation for compiler-known lists; any length other than one fails explicitly at runtime.
- [x] `count` and `empty` / `nonempty` conveniences for compiler-known lists, exposed as zero-cost properties rather than methods.
- [x] Full Python-style list slices/ranges: `[start:end]` and `[start:end:step]`, omitted/negative bounds, positive or negative steps, `[::-1]` reversal, clipping, and explicit zero-step rejection. Native slices remain zero-copy through strided list views.
- [x] Typed list iteration with `for value in list:` and indexed `for index, value in list:` forms; the source is evaluated once and the index is `i64`.
- [x] Typed `map(list, callback)` and `where(list, predicate)` with exact concrete callback signatures, strided-source support, pipeline composition, and stack-backed bootstrap results; chained transforms fuse into one source loop and one final result buffer.
- [x] Related collection-producing functional sequence operations beyond map/where.
  - [x] Typed `merge(left, right)` with exact element-type matching, strided-input support, pipeline composition, overflow checking, and a stack-backed local result; `merge` avoids clashing with `worker.join`.
  - [x] Stable `unique(list)` for scalar-equality lists (`i64`, `bool`, `str`, `error`), preserving first-occurrence order across strided inputs and composing through typed pipelines.
  - [x] One-level `flat(nested)` for concrete nested lists, preserving logical order across strided outer/inner lists, checking total length overflow, and producing a stack-backed local result.
  - [x] Immutable stable `sort(list)` for ordered scalar lists (`i64`, `bool`, `str`), preserving the source, consuming strided inputs in logical order, and producing a stack-backed ascending result that composes through pipelines.
  - [x] Zero-copy `chunk(list, size)` producing `T[][]` from strided inputs, with a positive `i64` size contract, shorter final chunk, empty-input identity, and stack-backed chunk descriptors that reference the original list storage.
- [x] Typed `fold(list, initial, reducer)` and `reduce(list, reducer)` scalar reductions with concrete callback signatures, strided-view support, pipeline composition, and explicit empty-list rejection for `reduce`.
- [x] `any(bool[])` / `all(bool[])` boolean aggregation with Python/Dart-style empty-sequence identities (`false` / `true`), designed to combine with boolean comprehensions and typed pipelines.
- [x] Zero-copy `take(list, count)` / `skip(list, count)` sequence views, designed to compose naturally through typed pipelines such as `values | skip 1 | take 3`; counts are `i64`, oversized counts clamp, and negative counts fail explicitly at runtime.
- [x] Lazy pipeline fusion where it avoids needless allocations: chained `map`/`where` stages share one loop and terminal `fold`/`reduce` consumes the transformed stream directly without an intermediate list.

`first`, `last`, etc. may use property-like syntax on compiler-known collection types while lowering to ordinary functions. This is syntax ergonomics, not an object model.

## Cascades / fluent mutation without builder objects

Dart's `..` cascade is useful because it avoids repeating a target. Flux should keep the ergonomic idea but apply it to values/functions rather than encouraging mutable object builders.

- [x] Functional cascade syntax analogous to `..` for repeated transformations on one target: `value .. transform args .. finish` is typed as the equivalent value pipeline, with parenthesized or shell-style function stages supported and canonical formatting normalized to `|`.
- [x] Optional-aware cascades use `?..` to transform a present Copy optional value through ordinary typed functions while propagating absence, evaluating the target once, skipping stage arguments/functions when absent, flattening optional-returning stages, preserving guarded evaluation in typed IR, and lowering allocation-free to native control flow.
- [x] Cascades produce successive transformed values rather than introducing hidden mutation: each stage receives the previous stage result as its first argument and ordinary Flux call typing applies.
- [x] Cascades use the existing functional pipeline lowering, adding no builder object, allocation, dynamic dispatch, or distinct runtime abstraction.
- [x] Cascade expressions evaluate the original target exactly once; lowering nests the same single-evaluation typed call flow used by pipelines.

## Collection literals and control flow

- [x] List literals with homogeneous static element typing and concrete `T[]` source spelling (no source generics).
- [x] Set literals with typed `set<T>` syntax for homogeneous compile-time primitive values, duplicate elimination, deterministic insertion-order iteration, formatting, and native lowering.
- [x] Map literals with typed `map<K, V>` syntax for homogeneous compile-time primitive key/value pairs, duplicate-key diagnostics, deterministic source order, and native paired key/value storage. Runtime map operations and map patterns remain separate follow-up items.
- [x] Spread elements in list literals with `...source`; spread sources must have the exact surrounding `T[]` type, are evaluated once in source order, preserve strided-view semantics, and lower into one overflow-checked stack-backed result buffer.
- [x] Optional-aware spread analogous to Dart `...?`: optional list sources are evaluated once, contribute zero elements when absent, preserve ordinary spread ordering/overflow checks when present, format canonically, and lower allocation-free through the existing stack-backed list builder.
- [x] Null/optional-aware collection elements use `?element` inside list literals: the expression must be a concrete Copy optional, is evaluated exactly once, appends its payload only when present, rejects bare `none`/non-optionals/type mismatches statically, formats canonically, participates in semantic/typed-IR traversal, and lowers through the existing overflow-checked stack-backed list builder with no allocation or runtime null assertion.
- [x] List-only `if condition: value` / `if condition: first else: second` construction items with strict bool conditions, exact element typing, selected-branch evaluation, and the same overflow-checked stack-backed builder used by spreads.
- [x] Python-style list comprehensions with optional boolean filters: `[expr for item in source if condition]`.
- [x] Pattern guards inside collection construction where useful: list construction supports safe optional-binding guards with `if let value = optionalValue: element`, including optional single evaluation, branch-local binding scope, optional absence skipping, typed `else` fallback elements, formatter/semantic/typed-IR traversal, and allocation-free native lowering through the existing list builder.
- [x] Bootstrap list literals/slices/comprehensions lower directly to native list descriptors; slices are zero-copy and comprehensions use stack-backed output sized from the source list without intermediate collections.

## Records, patterns, and destructuring

Flux intentionally keeps function multi-return values narrow today. General records should be added only when they do not blur explicit multi-return/error semantics.

- [x] Anonymous immutable records for lightweight aggregate data, with structural static types, native by-value lowering, formatter/semantic/IR support, and an ownership-safe bootstrap restriction to primitive or nested-record `Copy` fields until aggregate layout and owned move semantics expand.
- [x] Named record fields with `(name: value, ...)` literals/types and typed dot access.
- [x] Positional record fields with `(value, ...)` literals/types, `.0`/`.1` access, and single-field `(value,)` syntax that remains distinct from grouping through formatting.
- [x] Record destructuring patterns: `(first, second)` patterns destructure positional or named anonymous records by field order in `let`/`var` bindings and assignment, infer exact field types, evaluate the record expression once, preserve formatter/semantic/CFG typing, and lower to direct native field projection without allocation.
- [x] List patterns with exact/rest/wildcard shapes in local bindings plus exhaustive statement/value `match`, including zero-copy rest views over strided lists.
- [x] Map patterns for statement `match` arms: compile-time primitive keys bind map values from native paired storage, duplicate keys are rejected, and an unguarded `_` fallback is required for exhaustiveness. Expression-producing map matches and dynamic key patterns remain follow-up work.
- [x] Struct patterns for bindings and enum match payloads; map pattern forms remain pending with map values.
- [x] Enum/tagged-union variant patterns with typed positional payload bindings, ignored positions, nested struct payload patterns, and exhaustive statement/value matching.
- [x] Relational/logical scalar enum-payload patterns where readable: `==`, `!=`, `<`, `<=`, `>`, and `>=` compare against compile-time primitive constants, `&&`/`||` compose relational tests, statement/value matches share native lowering and formatter support, and refutable pattern arms remain conservatively non-exhaustive until a later irrefutable arm covers the variant.
- [x] Boolean pattern guards on enum/list statement and value-producing `match` arms, with pattern bindings in scope, formatter/semantic support, native lowering, guarded-arm fallthrough, conservative exhaustiveness, and unreachable-arm diagnostics after an unguarded covering arm.
- [x] Exhaustiveness checking for the implemented closed enum and list-length pattern domains, including unreachable/duplicate-arm rejection.
- [x] Pattern-based mutable declarations and assignments beyond `let` destructuring: typed multi-value, inferred multi-value, exact/rest list, and nested struct patterns may declare copyable targets with `var`, while corresponding multi-value/list/struct assignment patterns update existing mutable bindings after exact static type checks. Pattern RHS values are evaluated once, list assignments preserve exact/rest cardinality checks and strided order, struct assignments project nested fields directly, `_` remains discard-only, non-copy mutable captures remain rejected until full move/borrow semantics land, and formatter/semantic/CFG/native lowering share the same forms.

## Expression ergonomics

- [x] Shell-inspired typed call flow without subprocess semantics:
  - [x] Parentheses-free function calls such as `print "hello"` and `scale value 2`.
  - [x] Typed value pipelines such as `source | transform | consume`, passing each return value as the next call's first argument.
  - [x] `>` / `>>` scalar-result redirection to a typed `str` path, truncating or appending respectively.
  - [x] Trailing `&` detached background execution for call/pipeline statements; the current native bootstrap lowers this through POSIX process primitives only when a program actually uses background execution.
- [x] String interpolation. Allocation-free compile-time interpolation now supports `$NAME` / `${NAME}` references to primitive compile-time constants inside ordinary strings, folds the result to one native string literal, preserves literal dollars through `\$`, and formats canonically without introducing runtime storage. The identity form `${localStr}` is also allocation-free and lowers directly to the existing borrowed local string pointer; concatenating runtime/local interpolation remains sequenced after owned-string/buffer semantics because the bootstrap `str` currently lowers to borrowed native string pointers and Flux must not introduce hidden scratch-buffer allocation/lifetime behavior merely for interpolation syntax.
- [x] Multiline strings with triple-quoted `"""..."""` syntax, shared-indent stripping for normal indented source, ordinary escape semantics, formatter canonicalization, and LSP string-token/signature scanning support.
- [x] Raw strings with Python-like `r"..."` syntax: backslashes are literal, the value remains ordinary `str`, semantic highlighting treats the whole literal as a string, and canonical formatting normalizes it to equivalent escaped ordinary-string syntax.
- [x] No ternary expression form: Python-style `a if condition else b` and C-style `?:` are intentionally absent; use statement control flow or `match` values.
- [x] No separate Dart-style `~/`: Flux `i64` `/` already truncates toward zero and is checked for division by zero and `i64::MIN / -1`, so a second operator would duplicate semantics.
- [x] Concise inclusive integer ranges with `start..=end` alongside exclusive `start..end`, including single-evaluation bounds and overflow-safe native iteration.
- [x] Match expressions returning values; broader switch-like pattern expression ergonomics can build on the same lowering.
- [x] Assignment expressions are intentionally not part of Flux: assignment remains a statement restricted to explicitly mutable `var` bindings, keeping mutation locally visible and preventing hidden side effects inside conditions/calls/larger expressions. A standalone `=` inside an expression now produces a targeted parse diagnostic while `==` remains ordinary equality.
- [x] Strong primitive constant folding for literal/constant expression trees in ordinary code and compile-time UI/application metadata, with checked integer semantics, comparisons/equality, and boolean short-circuiting; runtime-dependent expressions remain dynamic.

## Async/generator ergonomics

Adapt the convenience of Dart's async language features while preserving Flux's explicit error-return model. The intended source model is native `async fn` plus `await`: an async function keeps its ordinary declared return shape, calling it creates compiler-owned task state, and `await` yields those declared values directly. Flux should not require a source-level `Future<T>`/`Promise<T>` generic or user-visible completion callback to express ordinary asynchronous work. The bootstrap compiler lowers reachable `async fn` declarations to compiler-owned native task structs with copied arguments, detached pthread-backed execution, declared result storage, and deterministic task-state release; zero-, single-, and multi-value return shapes including explicit `error` remain ordinary Flux values. Straight-line top-level awaits whose live state is entirely `Copy` now lower to compiler-owned resume states and child-completion continuations: the parent worker returns instead of blocking, the child resumes the parent after completion, and root/blocking consumers wait on a task condition rather than joining a child thread. Direct `return await ...` forwarding, direct mutable assignment awaits (including multi-value destructuring assignments), direct struct-pattern destructuring from awaited `Copy` struct results, and direct awaits inside an `if` with an await-free boolean condition now use the same non-blocking continuation path, including mutable outer `Copy` state saved across suspension and common fallthrough after the branch. A top-level `if` whose boolean condition is itself one direct `await` now suspends before branch selection and resumes through the same continuation state machine, including then/else arms that contain their own direct awaits. The `then` and `else` arms may each contain multiple sequential direct awaits; each arm gets disjoint continuation states that preserve branch-local and outer `Copy` values across suspension, with native regressions building and running taken, alternate, skipped, assignment, and multi-await paths end to end. Direct immutable optional-presence checks (`value != none` / `none != value`) promote through suspended branches when safe, while `value == none` / `none == value` promote in the corresponding branch; mutable optionals do not promote. Direct optional-binding `if let` branches use the same continuation path for `Copy` payloads. Direct `while` bodies whose condition is await-free or one direct awaited boolean and whose suspension points are supported direct/coalescing awaits use continuation machinery across repeated iterations, including mutable outer `Copy` state and `break`/`continue`. Direct integer range `for` bodies whose start/end bounds are each await-free or one direct `await` likewise preserve evaluated bounds, index state, and loop control without blocking. Enum and ownership-safe list `match` statements suspend supported direct/coalescing awaits through compiler-owned continuation states while evaluating their scrutinee once; ownership-sensitive list/rest bindings retain the safe blocking fallback. Top-level and supported split-body `optional ??= await asyncCall(...)` preserve lazy coalescing: present optionals skip child creation and continue toward later suspension points, while absent optionals suspend and resume through the corresponding continuation state. Split `if`/`else`, enum `match`, ownership-safe list `match`, `while`, and integer range `for` bodies may alternate direct awaits and lazy coalescing awaits in one state machine. A single nested `else`/`elif` branch whose condition is a direct awaited boolean or optional binding can now continue through direct awaits and lazy `??= await` suspension in either nested arm. `for`-each loops, deeper nested branch chains, ownership-sensitive list-match bindings, and other not-yet-split expression placements retain the safe blocking fallback. General CFG-wide suspension/resumption for those remaining placements is still required before the unchecked suspension item below is complete.

Straight-line continuation lowering now also treats a non-`Send` mutable local as post-suspension storage when its pre-suspension value is definitely overwritten before the first resumed read. The old borrowed value is not copied into task state, the resumed case gets fresh local storage, and any read-before-overwrite (including `??=` target reads) keeps the safe blocking fallback. Definite-overwrite analysis recognizes direct assignment, multi-value/list/struct pattern assignment, and `if`/`else` joins only when both branches overwrite before a read; partial branches remain on the blocking path.

Verified direct-await destructuring follow-up: explicit typed multi-value declarations, inferred `Copy` record declarations, `Copy` record assignments, and struct-pattern assignments now resume through compiler-owned continuations rather than blocking a worker; mutable destructured bindings retain mutability after resume. This advances the unchecked suspension track without claiming general CFG-wide suspension, `for`-each/non-`Send` view state, or first-class ownership/lifetime work complete.

Verified awaited optional-binding condition follow-up: `if let value = await optionalCall(...)` now suspends before branch selection, restores the completed optional without blocking the parent worker, binds the concrete `Copy` payload only on the present path, and can continue through further direct awaits in either branch. General CFG-wide suspension remains incomplete.

Verified awaited while-condition follow-up: direct `while await asyncBool(...):` conditions now suspend before every condition check through compiler-owned continuation state, resume the completed boolean before entering the body, and compose with direct body awaits plus `break` / `continue` without blocking the parent worker. Awaited enum-match scrutinees now likewise suspend before dispatch, restore the completed enum value, and compose with direct awaits inside the selected arm. General CFG-wide suspension, `for`-each loops, nested branch shapes, ownership-sensitive list-match bindings, and other not-yet-split expression placements remain incomplete.

Verified nested-branch continuation follow-up: an await-free outer `if` whose `else`/`elif` path is a single nested branch may suspend a direct awaited boolean condition or direct awaited optional-binding `if let` condition without blocking the parent worker, then continue through direct awaits and lazy coalescing awaits in either nested arm. Copy/Send outer and branch-local state is saved and restored across each child completion, including optional-binding payloads and mutable locals, while the common outer tail remains shared. Else-side immutable optional promotion from `value == none` / `none == value` now survives the nested suspension as well: parameters and saved locals are reconstructed from the proven-present optional payload before the nested branch and remain concrete through later branch awaits. Deeper nested chains, `for`-each/non-`Send` list state, and other ownership-sensitive shapes conservatively retain the safe blocking fallback.

Verified mixed branch/loop-suspension follow-up: `if`/`else`, enum `match`, ownership-safe list `match`, `while`, and integer range `for` bodies may perform direct awaits and lazy `optional ??= await asyncCall(...)` suspensions in either order without blocking the parent worker. Those split bodies can continue non-blockingly from conditional suspension into later direct awaits and later coalescing suspension, including the present-value path that starts no child task. Ownership-sensitive list-match bindings, `for`-each loops, deeper nested branch chains, and other unsplit placements retain the safe blocking fallback.

Verified chained top-level coalescing follow-up: multiple top-level `optional ??= await asyncCall(...)` statements may share one compiler-owned continuation state machine. Present optionals bypass child creation without preventing a later missing optional from suspending non-blockingly, and await-free statements between coalescing points retain source order. Copy/Send-safe top-level `let` and `var` declarations between conditional suspension points now join that continuation path: each later resume state reconstructs the declarations that are in scope and restores their saved values/mutability before completing the awaited fallback. Copy/Send-safe typed multi-value, inferred record, and struct destructuring declarations use the same saved-state path, including mutable destructured bindings; ownership-sensitive list destructuring still keeps the safe blocking fallback until its lifetime contract is represented explicitly.

Verified mixed straight-line suspension follow-up: top-level direct awaits and lazy `optional ??= await asyncCall(...)` statements can alternate in one continuation state machine instead of forcing the function onto the blocking body path. Both present and absent optional paths preserve source order into later direct awaits, and native regression coverage exercises coalescing → direct await → coalescing → direct await with both missing and already-present values.

- [x] Async functions lowered to compiler-owned native state machines/tasks rather than callback-shaped source APIs or a mandatory VM/runtime object hierarchy.
- [x] `await` syntax yields the awaited function's ordinary declared return values and is valid only in an async context; supported placements lower through compiler-owned continuations, while the documented safe blocking fallback remains for unsplit/ownership-sensitive placements.
  - Current continuation coverage keeps mixed top-level direct awaits and `optional ??= await asyncCall(...)` points non-blocking; supported split `if`/`else`, enum-match, ownership-safe list-match, `while`, and integer range `for` bodies may likewise continue from coalescing suspension into later direct/coalescing suspension. One nested `else`/`elif` branch with an awaited boolean/optional-binding condition may continue non-blockingly through direct/coalescing awaits in either arm. Copy/Send-safe `let`/`var` locals plus typed multi-value, inferred record, and struct destructuring declarations may cross top-level conditional suspension points; list destructuring, `for`-each loops, deeper nested branch chains, ownership-sensitive list-match bindings, and other unsplit placements remain on the safe blocking fallback.
- [x] Async failures returned explicitly through normal Flux `error` values/multi-value returns, not thrown as exceptions or hidden in rejected promises.
- [ ] Streams/sequences as explicit types/capabilities.
- [ ] Generator functions.
- [ ] `yield` / `yield from` equivalent if it fits ownership rules.
- [x] Cancellation as an explicit capability/value: structured native workers expose explicit `worker.cancel(handle)`, `worker.cancelAll()`, and cooperative `worker.cancelled()` state; cancellation propagates recursively through worker scopes, unfinished children are cancelled before automatic join, and blocking channel, timer, socket-readiness, and HTTP/network waits cooperate with cancellation rather than requiring unsafe thread termination. The compiler-owned helpers remain tree-shaken when unreachable.

## Dart features intentionally not copied

- [x] No classes.
- [x] No inheritance.
- [x] No mixins.
- [x] No abstract base classes as the polymorphism foundation; use interfaces.
- [x] No factory constructors or constructor hierarchy as an architectural pattern.
- [x] No class extension methods as an object-system feature; prefer free functions, interfaces, and compiler-known ergonomic sugar.
- [x] No exception-based async errors.
- [x] No source-language generics.

---

# Milestone 4 — Ownership and memory safety

The ownership track now has explicit compiler-level `Copy` and cross-thread `Send` classifications rather than relying on ad-hoc list checks. Bootstrap scalar handles (`i64`, `bool`, `str`, `error`), function values, closed interface values, and recursively copyable structs/enums are `Copy`; `void` is not a value and local list/view types are deliberately non-copy until owned collection storage and move/borrow rules exist. `Send` is deliberately stricter: borrowed `str` and any optional/record/struct/enum/interface value containing borrowed string storage are not sendable across detached async task boundaries, while `i64`, `bool`, explicit `error` values, capture-free function pointers, and recursively sendable aggregates remain permitted. Existing mutable-binding and aggregate-storage safety gates query `Copy`, while async task boundaries query `Send`, so pointer-copyability cannot masquerade as thread safety. Direct non-copy moves are represented on the semantic CFG and a deterministic forward may-analysis computes moved-value state at every reachable CFG node keyed by the exact reaching source definition; re-executing a lexical binding kills only that definition's prior dynamic loop instance, and use-after-move diagnostics intersect moved definitions with the definitions that actually reach each read rather than maintaining a second syntax-shaped branch/loop ownership state machine. Borrowed list-parameter aliases are likewise reclassified from normalized reaching-definition/value provenance before move-state propagation, including transitive aliases, so the bootstrap borrow checker no longer carries a parallel syntax-tree reborrow analysis. Zero-copy local list views now also participate in CFG-liveness ownership validation: a list owner cannot move while a derived slice/indexed-list/`first`/`last`/`only`/`take`/`skip`/`chunk` view or a list-typed binding projected by list destructuring/matching remains live, while a move after the view's final use stays valid. Expression-scoped list-match bindings retain their normalized borrow-source value, and nested-list aggregates preserve embedded borrow provenance through ordinary items, spreads, reachable conditional collection branches, and list comprehensions whose elements remain borrowed views, so wrapper IR cannot hide a live zero-copy view and permit its owner to move early. The normalized CFG now also exposes deterministic edges where each path-sensitive immutable borrow starts and ends and groups those boundaries into definition-scoped inferred lifetime records containing the active CFG nodes. Each lifetime carries both borrower-definition and concrete source-owner-definition identity, including same-named sibling source bindings, and live-owner move validation requires that exact source definition to reach the move node before rejecting the move. This keeps the safety check and exposed lifetime model on one normalized ownership path without conflating unrelated same-named bindings.

- [x] Define copyable primitive/value types with alias-aware structural classification shared by semantic validation.
- [ ] Move semantics for non-copy values.
  - [x] Bootstrap direct local transfers (`let destination: T = source`) move non-copy values, preserve ordinary reuse for `Copy` types, and conservatively merge moves across `if`/exhaustive `match` branches.
  - [x] Bootstrap loop control-flow recognizes iteration-safe direct moves through CFG back-edges: fallthrough and `continue` paths expose the moved source on a later iteration and fail, while paths that leave the loop through `break`/`return` do not create a repeated read.
  - [x] Return-terminated ownership paths do not poison reachable sibling/post-loop state because move facts propagate only over actual CFG successors; zero-iteration loop exits likewise preserve the pre-loop state where appropriate.
  - [x] Nested direct-move loop analysis follows composed CFG edges across `if`/`match`, nested loops, branch-specific `break`, and `continue`, removing the former syntax-shaped loop-exit ownership bookkeeping.
  - [x] Bootstrap direct-local move path-state is computed to a fixed point on the semantic CFG with deterministic definition-scoped move origins and reachability facts; each move attaches to the exact source definitions reaching that transfer, while executing a fresh lexical definition kills only that definition's prior dynamic loop instance. Compile-time primitive conditions and statically empty integer ranges remove impossible CFG edges before ownership propagation so dead moves do not poison live owners.
  - [x] Follow-up: fresh local definitions kill stale move facts only for their exact lexical definition identity before CFG propagation to successors, so a non-copy value created and moved inside a loop is fresh on the next iteration, while same-named sibling or later definitions remain distinct ownership values instead of sharing name-keyed state. Reads in the defining node still observe the incoming state before the new definition exists, preserving use-after-move checks while separating successive dynamic instances operationally.
  - [x] Verified explicit `drop` consuming-call follow-up: current non-copy list, set, map, and ownership-tracked optional-list/map descriptors may be explicitly consumed at a source-visible drop boundary; the normalized ownership IR records a `Consuming` call argument and exact source-definition move, later reads are rejected, native lowering remains allocation-free because bootstrap collection storage has no owned destructor yet, and LSP completion/signature/semantic-token support exposes the contract. Owned resource destruction remains pending.
  - [x] Verified consuming-call drop accounting follow-up: implicit destruction now consults exact definition-scoped `Consuming` call arguments as well as explicit move events, so a future owned/resource call represented only by normalized call mode cannot manufacture a second drop at the same liveness boundary; sibling definitions remain independent, with dedicated IR regression coverage.
  - [ ] General move analysis across calls, aggregate fields, partial moves, and owned resource types after normalized control-flow ownership facts exist. The current bootstrap has only the explicit `drop` consuming boundary; ordinary list/set/map parameters remain immutable borrows, structs/enums reject non-copy fields, and owned resource types have not landed. Do not invent consuming-call syntax merely to advance this checkbox; extend the normalized move facts when the first owned non-copy parameter/aggregate/resource contract exists.
- [ ] Immutable borrows.
  - [x] Explicit `borrow` expressions now cover named `set` and `map` descriptors in addition to lists and optional list views. Their normalized immutable-borrow events participate in the same definition-scoped lifetime regions and owner-move validation; projections and first-class owned/reference types remain pending.
  - [x] Bootstrap local list/view reads, indexing/slicing, transforms, iteration, matching, and list function parameters are non-consuming immutable borrows of their local owner; borrowed list parameters can be rebound through direct local alias chains without incorrectly moving either the parameter or an earlier borrowed alias.
  - [x] Explicit consuming boundaries cannot consume borrowed non-copy collection parameters (lists, sets, maps, and supported optional collection descriptors) or their direct immutable aliases; local collection storage remains eligible for the existing explicit `drop` boundary, while parameter-backed storage is rejected before ownership IR/code generation.
  - [x] Reachable non-copy name reads are represented explicitly as immutable-borrow facts on normalized CFG ownership nodes, derived from typed IR value reachability rather than syntax-wide read collection. Use-after-move validation consumes those borrow facts, so compile-time-dead nested regions such as a short-circuited boolean RHS do not spuriously borrow a moved owner while runtime-reachable borrows still fail deterministically.
  - [ ] First-class borrow/reference types and inferred borrow lifetimes for owned values. Bootstrap local collection/view lifetimes now expose path-sensitive CFG borrow state plus deterministic start/end edges grouped into definition-scoped inferred lifetime records, and source-visible `borrow expression` creates explicit immutable reborrows for named list/set/map storage or zero-copy list indexing/slicing/property projections rooted in it, including optional list descriptors (`T[]?`); those projection borrows retain the concrete root-owner definition in the same inferred regions. Distinct general reference types for future owned values and owned-value lifetime inference remain pending.
- [ ] Exclusive mutable borrows.
- [ ] Lifetime inference. Bootstrap list/view lifetime regions are now inferred from CFG liveness/reaching definitions and exposed with stable borrower-definition and source-owner-definition identity, active CFG nodes, and all start/end boundaries, including explicit whole-binding and rooted-projection `borrow`/reborrow bindings, distinct same-named sibling-scope lifetimes/source owners, and loop-local regions. Live-owner move validation consumes these inferred regions directly and verifies the recorded source definition actually reaches the move point; first-class owned/reference lifetime inference remains pending.
- [ ] Prevent use-after-move.
  - [x] Reject later reads of a non-copy local after a direct bootstrap move, including conservative post-branch invalidation for paths that can rejoin later code.
  - [x] Direct-local use-after-move diagnostics are driven by CFG/IR move-state before each normalized evaluation node, including loops, branch joins, `break`, `continue`, and returns.
  - [ ] Extend CFG/IR-wide move-state analysis to consuming calls, partial moves, future owned aggregates, and owned resource types.
- [ ] Prevent dangling borrows. Bootstrap zero-copy local borrow lifetimes, including source-visible `borrow` and transitive reborrow bindings, now have explicit active CFG regions plus start/end boundaries grouped by borrower definition, and moving an owner is rejected by querying those inferred regions directly. Borrowing temporaries and projections rooted in temporary list storage is rejected, while rooted indexing/slicing/property borrows preserve the stable source-owner definition; list returns/aggregate storage remain ownership-gated so these bootstrap references cannot escape, and general escaping/owned-reference lifetime checks remain pending.
  - [x] Bootstrap live-view move exclusion for zero-copy list borrows: CFG liveness rejects moving a local list owner while a derived slice, nested-list index/property (`first`/`last`/`only`), `take`, `skip`, `chunk`, list-rest pattern, list-match rest, destructured nested-list element, transitive view binding, or local nested-list literal containing its descriptor remains live, while permitting the move immediately after the borrow's last reachable use. Dedicated regression coverage now pins both sides of the `chunk` lifetime boundary: moving the owner while chunk descriptors remain live fails, while moving it immediately after their last reachable use succeeds. Full first-class lifetime inference and escaping-borrow validation remain pending.
  - [x] Follow-up: value-producing enum/list `match` results preserve zero-copy list-view provenance through the selected arm, including nested list views reached through expression-scoped arm bindings, so a live match-selected view prevents moving its owner while a move after the view's last use remains valid.
  - [x] Follow-up: preserve embedded nested-list borrow provenance through ordinary/optional-aware list spreads and conditional collection elements; the borrow analysis traverses the dedicated IR wrappers, ignores statically unreachable collection branches, keeps an embedded inner-list owner borrowed while the constructed aggregate remains live, and releases that restriction after the aggregate's final reachable use.
  - [x] Follow-up: nested-list comprehensions preserve borrow provenance from scoped element bindings into the constructed aggregate; moving the source owner is rejected while a comprehension-projected inner view remains live and becomes valid immediately after the aggregate's last reachable use.
  - [x] Follow-up: descriptor-preserving nested-list `where`, `merge`, and nested-result `flat` keep embedded list owners borrowed until the transformed aggregate's last reachable use; capture-free anonymous functions also reject list returns so stack-backed collection storage cannot escape a callback before owned collection transfer/lifetime semantics exist.
  - [x] Follow-up: live-view move validation resolves each live alias through the definitions that actually reach the move node, and transitive borrow provenance follows resolved definition identities rather than globally matching a binding name. Mutually exclusive sibling branches may therefore reuse local view names without an unrelated borrowed definition falsely poisoning a valid owner move, while real same-path transitive views remain protected.
  - [x] Follow-up: nested-list `for value in source` bindings retain their normalized borrow source from the foreach iterable. Moving the outer list owner while a projected list element is still live now fails with the same CFG-liveness borrow diagnostic as destructuring/match views, while a breaking path may move the owner after the element's final reachable use.
  - [x] Follow-up: list-typed foreach, destructuring, and match-pattern bindings that are already proven borrowed views now propagate that borrowed-definition identity through direct local aliases. Rebinding such a view is an immutable reborrow rather than a move of the view binding, while the alias still keeps the original list owner live and blocks moving that owner until the alias's final reachable use.
- [ ] Prevent aliasing violations.
  - [x] Bootstrap non-copy aliasing is immutable-only: local list/view storage cannot be declared mutable, borrowed list parameters and derived views may only form immutable reborrow aliases, and assignment cannot upgrade those aliases into mutable access. Regression coverage pins both mutable-declaration and assignment rejection, so the current bootstrap cannot create simultaneous mutable/shared aliases before first-class exclusive borrows exist.
- [ ] Deterministic destruction/resource release.
  - [x] SQLite handles opened by a native program are registered with compiler-owned process-exit cleanup; explicit `sqlite.close` remains idempotence-checked and removes its slot, while unclosed databases are closed before process termination without source-level destructors or a runtime object hierarchy.
  - [x] Compiler-owned TLS sessions are registered for deterministic process-exit cleanup when application code does not explicitly call `tls.close`; cleanup releases every live OpenSSL session/context, unregisters each underlying socket from the native descriptor registry before closing it, and closes its socket without requiring source-level destructors or a runtime object hierarchy. Explicit close remains idempotence-checked by the existing slot table; broader owned-resource destruction remains pending.
  - [x] Bootstrap native TCP/UDP socket handles are registered for process-exit cleanup, removed on explicit `net.close`, and now also removed when a WebSocket session closes (including coded close), preventing descriptor reuse from making the exit registry close an unrelated later socket. General owned-value destruction remains pending.
- [ ] Safe closures/captures under the ownership model.
- [x] Thread-safety/sendability rules for the current bootstrap concurrency model: `Signatures` now computes alias-aware structural `Send` independently from `Copy`; detached async parameters/results must be `Send`; borrowed `str` and aggregates/optionals/interfaces containing borrowed string storage are rejected at the task boundary; `i64`, `bool`, explicit `error` values, capture-free function pointers, and recursively sendable value types remain zero-cost; worker/channel primitives continue to expose only scalar handles/`i64` payloads. Future owned resources/strings/collections must opt into `Send` explicitly rather than inheriting it from copyability.
- [x] Narrow, explicit `unsafe` escape hatch only where necessary: the bootstrap language reserves `unsafe` for native FFI declarations, where `unsafe extern c` is accepted only for borrowed `str`/`error` results whose foreign pointer lifetime Flux cannot prove. Safe scalar/void imports reject unnecessary `unsafe`, and undefined aggregate/owned/function/interface ABI shapes remain rejected rather than being admitted by the escape hatch.
- [x] First bootstrap borrow checker over normalized semantics/IR rather than parser syntax: direct non-copy moves, immutable non-copy reads, borrowed list-parameter reborrows, path-sensitive move state, zero-copy list/view borrow provenance, and use-after-move diagnostics are derived from CFG/typed-value/reaching-definition facts. Borrow-source provenance is now queried directly through `ControlFlowGraph` rather than being reconstructed inside the type checker, keeping this ownership fact on the normalized IR boundary. Full borrow/reference types, inferred lifetimes, mutable aliasing, dangling-borrow prevention, partial moves, and drop semantics remain tracked explicitly above rather than being implied complete.

# Milestone 5 — Standard library and built-in concrete containers

Flux has no generics, so common containers need compiler-known/specialized implementations and ergonomic concrete typing.

- [ ] Owned string type plus immutable string views/slices.
  - [x] Bootstrap immutable borrowed string slices through `str.slice(value, start, end, callback) -> error`: bounded UTF-8 byte ranges must land on code-point boundaries, the native result is stack-backed and lent only to the exact callback scope, invalid/range/oversize inputs fail explicitly, and the helper is tree-shakeable with LSP completion/signature help. Owned strings and escaping slices remain pending.
  - [x] Bootstrap allocation-free UTF-8 scalar length through `str.length(value) -> i64`: native lowering validates UTF-8 (including overlong, surrogate, truncated, and out-of-range sequences), counts Unicode scalar values with checked `i64` conversion, and tree-shakes with LSP completion/signature help.
- [ ] Arrays/lists.
  - [x] Bootstrap immutable local `T[]` lists with literals, checked indexing, zero-copy slicing, and filtered comprehensions.
  - [ ] Owned/escaping list storage, owned list argument/return transfer, aggregate fields, mutation, and lifetime/ownership integration; current list parameters are immutable borrowed views.
- [x] Maps.
  - [x] Bootstrap typed `map<K, V>` literals with primitive compile-time keys/values, deterministic insertion-order storage, duplicate-key diagnostics, formatter support, and native paired key/value lowering.
  - [x] Map match arms with compile-time scalar keys, typed value bindings, native linear lookup, guards, and required wildcard fallback coverage.
  - [x] Optional keyed lookup through `map[key]` and `map?[key]`, including exact key typing, missing-key results as `value?`, absent-map short-circuiting, native string/scalar equality, and allocation-free linear lookup.
  - [x] Map iteration through `for key, value in map`, preserving insertion order and binding keys/values directly from native storage.
  - [x] Scalar membership queries through `contains(collection, key)` for lists, sets, and map keys, with exact static typing and allocation-free native scans.
  - [x] Read-only map cardinality properties: `.count`, `.empty`, and `.nonempty` type-check, lower directly to native key storage, and are exposed through LSP completion/hover.
- [x] Sets.
  - [x] Bootstrap typed `set<T>` literals with primitive compile-time values, insertion-order-preserving deduplication, deterministic iteration, and native list-descriptor lowering.
  - [x] Scalar membership queries through `contains(set, key)` with exact key typing and allocation-free native scans.
  - [x] Read-only set cardinality properties: `.count`, `.empty`, and `.nonempty` type-check, lower directly to the native list descriptor, and are exposed through LSP completion/hover.
- [ ] Byte buffers.
  - [x] Bootstrap bounded binary socket reads through canonical `net.readBytes(socket, maxBytes, callback)` (with compatibility alias `receiveBytes`): native bytes are exposed as `i64[]` values in the exact `0..=255` range for callback scope, arbitrary NUL bytes are preserved, the stack-backed descriptor cannot escape, static/runtime size limits match text reads, and the helper is tree-shaken with compiler/LSP coverage. Owned/escaping byte-buffer storage and mutation remain pending.
  - [x] Verified socket-kind hardening follow-up: `net.readBytes` now validates the descriptor as a connected TCP socket before performing its blocking read, matching the native contract already enforced by binary writes and batched text reads; invalid datagram/listener handles fail explicitly without attempting a read, with generated-C regression coverage.
  - [x] Verified cancellable timed-read follow-up: `net.readBytesTimeout(socket, maxBytes, timeoutMillis, callback)` waits through the compiler-owned cancellation-aware readiness path, preserves arbitrary binary/NUL payloads in a bounded callback-scoped `i64[]`, returns `(received, ready, error)`, and validates TCP/socket/size/timeout constraints with generated-C regression coverage. Owned/escaping byte-buffer storage remains pending.
  - [x] Bootstrap borrowed binary TCP writes through canonical `net.writeBytes(socket, bytes)` (with compatibility alias `sendBytes`): the immutable `i64[]` view is validated element-by-element in the native boundary, statically known literal values outside `0..=255` are rejected during checking, dynamic values outside the range return an explicit error after preserving exact completed-byte progress, NUL/high bytes are sent without text conversion, EINTR is retried, and no payload is retained or allocated. Native lowering, C syntax validation, LSP signature help, and a real TCP loopback regression cover the contract; timed binary writes and owned/escaping byte-buffer storage remain pending.
  - [x] Bootstrap cancellation-aware timed binary TCP writes through `net.writeBytesTimeout(socket, bytes, timeoutMillis)`: immutable borrowed `i64[]` bytes retain exact completed-byte progress, enforce `0..=255` values, require connected nonblocking TCP, wait through one monotonic deadline after backpressure, cooperate with worker cancellation, tree-shake with the list runtime, and expose typed LSP signature help. Owned/escaping byte-buffer storage remains pending.
  - [x] Borrowed resumable timed binary TCP writes through `net.writeBytesFromTimeout(socket, bytes, offset, timeoutMillis)` (canonical short name; compatibility alias `sendBytesProgressWithTimeout`) preserve a caller-owned global byte offset and `complete` flag across nonblocking backpressure, apply one monotonic deadline, retain exact progress on timeout/failure/cancellation, validate byte values and literal offset/timeout bounds, tree-shake with the existing cancellable network runtime, and expose completion/signature help plus native loopback regression coverage. Owned/escaping byte-buffer storage remains pending.
  - [x] Verified batched borrowed binary TCP output: ordinary, resumable, timed, and resumable-timed `net.writeBytes` paths validate strided `i64[]` values into a bounded 4 KiB stack buffer and issue chunked native sends, preserving exact partial progress, nonblocking retry, timeout, cancellation, and ownership behavior without per-byte syscalls or retained payload storage.
  - [x] Borrowed nonblocking TCP binary batch reads through `net.readBytesMany(socket, maxBytes, maxCount, callback)` (compatibility alias `receiveBytesMany`) drain a caller-bounded number of immediately available chunks into a callback-scoped `i64[]` view, preserve arbitrary `0..=255` bytes and exact total progress, stop cleanly at `EAGAIN`/EOF, cooperate with worker cancellation, validate TCP/nonblocking/socket and bounds at the native boundary, tree-shake when unreachable, and expose LSP signature help. Owned/escaping byte-buffer storage and task-integrated event-loop I/O remain pending.
  - [x] Cancellation-aware timed binary batch reads through `net.readBytesManyTimeout(socket, maxBytes, maxCount, timeoutMillis, callback)` (compatibility alias `receiveBytesManyWithTimeout`) wait for initial TCP readability through the structured cancellation-aware poll path, then reuse the bounded borrowed drain while returning `(received, ready, error)` and preserving partial progress/errors. Native bounds/socket checks, tree shaking, and LSP signature help are covered; owned/escaping byte-buffer storage and task-integrated event-loop I/O remain pending.
  - [x] Borrowed nonblocking UDP binary batch reads through `net.readBytesFromMany(socket, maxBytes, maxCount, callback)` (compatibility alias `receiveBytesFromMany`) preserve datagram boundaries, arbitrary `0..=255` payloads, numeric peer address/port metadata, exact aggregate byte progress, cancellation, and callback-scoped storage; the timed form waits through the shared cancellation-aware readiness path. Native loopback execution, truncation/bounds checks, socket-kind validation, tree-shaking, and LSP signatures are covered. Owned/escaping byte-buffer storage and task-integrated event-loop I/O remain pending.
  - [x] Verified peer-addressed borrowed binary UDP output through `net.writeBytesTo(socket, host, port, bytes)` (compatibility spelling `sendBytesTo`): validates the immutable `i64[]` byte view and UDP port statically where possible, enforces the IPv4/IPv6 socket family and 65507-byte datagram limit natively, preserves NUL/high-byte payloads as one datagram, returns `(sentBytes, error)`, tree-shakes when unreachable, and is covered by LSP plus real loopback native execution. Owned/escaping byte-buffer storage and task-integrated event-loop I/O remain pending.
  - [x] Verified peer-addressed borrowed binary parts output through `net.writeBytesToParts(socket, host, port, parts: i64[][])`: nested borrowed byte descriptors are validated for storage, stride, byte range, and the 65507-byte datagram bound before being flattened into one allocation-free native datagram; exact byte order/progress, UDP family validation, EINTR handling, tree-shaking, and LSP signature help are covered by compiler and loopback execution tests. Owned/escaping byte-buffer storage and task-integrated event-loop I/O remain pending.
- [ ] Paths and filesystem APIs.
- [x] Verified path-capability follow-up: `path.isAbsolute(value) -> bool`, bounded callback-scoped `path.join(base, child, callback) -> error`, and lexical `path.dirname`/`path.basename` component callbacks are compiler-owned native operations with typed checking, LSP completion/signature help, fixed stack-backed result storage, absolute-child replacement, separator insertion, NUL/65536-byte bounds checks, and complete dead-helper tree shaking. Owned/escaping path values and broader path normalization remain pending.
- [x] Verified lexical path-normalization follow-up: bounded callback-scoped `path.normalize(value, callback) -> error` canonicalizes repeated separators plus `.`/`..` components for absolute and relative paths in fixed stack-backed storage, rejects oversized inputs/results, participates in typed checking/LSP completion/signature help, and tree-shakes when unreachable. It deliberately remains lexical and does not inspect the filesystem or create an owned/escaping path value.
- [x] Verified lexical path-component follow-up: bounded callback-scoped `path.extension(value, callback) -> error` and `path.stem(value, callback) -> error` extract the final dotted suffix and filename stem without treating a leading dotfile marker as an extension; both use fixed stack-backed storage, typed checking, LSP completion/signature help, native execution coverage, and reachability-based tree shaking. Owned/escaping path values remain pending.
- [x] Verified file-read overflow-probe hardening: bounded file.read now distinguishes clean EOF from an I/O error while checking for bytes beyond maxBytes, releases its temporary buffer on both paths, and retains the existing UTF-8/NUL/callback-lifetime contract.
  - [x] Bootstrap compiler-owned filesystem capabilities: the ordinary API is now split into short `file.*` / `directory.*` operations (`file.exists`, `file.write`, `file.append`, `file.copy`, `file.rename`, `file.remove`, `directory.exists`, `directory.create`, recursive `directory.createAll`, `directory.remove`, and recursive `directory.removeAll`) while the older `fs.*` spellings remain accepted for compatibility. All are strictly typed, lower directly to native/POSIX filesystem primitives, return explicit Flux `error` values for mutations, participate in LSP qualified completion/signature help, and tree-shake completely when unreachable. Recursive directory creation validates existing path components are directories and releases its temporary native path buffer on every exit path; recursive removal does not follow directory symlinks and releases per-entry traversal buffers on every path.
- [ ] Owned path/string results, directory enumeration, richer filesystem metadata/errors, and ownership-safe byte I/O after owned strings/collections exist. Do not expose a callback-shaped text-read stopgap. The canonical text API is reserved as callback-free `await file.read(path)`, yielding owned text plus an explicit `error` once owned strings and the task/`await` model can make that lifetime sound. Callback-free scalar metadata now includes `file.size(path) -> (i64, error)`, file/directory `modified(path) -> (i64, error)`, file/directory `accessed(path) -> (i64, error)`, file/directory `changed(path) -> (i64, error)`, file/directory `permissions(path) -> (i64, error)`, file/directory `owner(path) -> (i64, error)`, file/directory `group(path) -> (i64, error)`, file/directory `inode(path) -> (i64, error)`, file/directory `device(path) -> (i64, error)`, file/directory `hardLinks(path) -> (i64, error)`, file/directory `blockSize(path) -> (i64, error)`, and file/directory `allocatedSize(path) -> (i64, error)`: timestamps validate the expected path kind and return Unix milliseconds without allocating (`changed` reports POSIX inode-status change time), permissions return the POSIX permission/special-mode bits, owner/group expose native numeric UID/GID values, inode/device/hard-link metadata expose their native numeric identities/counts with checked `i64` conversion, and storage metadata exposes preferred I/O block size plus allocated bytes without owned buffers; all participate in LSP completion/signature help and unreachable helpers tree-shake. Callback-free native mutation now also includes `file.truncate(path, size)`, file/directory `setPermissions(path, permissions)`, file/directory `setModified(path, unixMillis)` / `setAccessed(path, unixMillis)`, file/directory `setOwner(path, owner)` / `setGroup(path, group)`, and `directory.rename(source, destination)`, with static checks for known invalid sizes/modes/identities, runtime path-kind/range validation, explicit `error` results, LSP completion/signature help, and dead-helper tree shaking. Native durability/linking now also includes `file.sync(path)`, `file.syncData(path)`, `directory.sync(path)`, and `file.link(source, destination)`, with path-kind validation, explicit errors, LSP support, native regression coverage, and dead-helper tree shaking. Access-time reads now preserve native sub-second precision so timestamp updates round-trip in Unix milliseconds. The existing callback-scoped `file.read` path validates bounded input as UTF-8 before lending it to Flux, rejecting malformed sequences in addition to NUL and size violations; `directory.list` now derives each entry's name length from its bounded directory-record span before filtering dot entries or validating UTF-8, including rejecting truncated multibyte names without reading past the directory entry. Owned directory results and owned reads still wait for owned collections/strings rather than adding callback APIs.
- [x] Date/time/duration.
  - [x] Bootstrap native scalar time primitives: `time.now()` exposes wall-clock Unix milliseconds, `time.monotonic()` provides an elapsed-time clock, `time.sleep(durationMs)` performs an EINTR-safe native sleep with compile-time rejection of constant negative durations plus a runtime guard for dynamic negatives, and `time.sleepUntil(deadlineMillis)` provides an absolute monotonic deadline. Legacy longer spellings remain source-compatible but are omitted from default completion. The helpers are tree-shaken when unreachable and participate in qualified LSP completion/signature help; calendar values, time zones, formatting, and richer duration types remain pending.
  - [x] Allocation-free UTC calendar decomposition from Unix milliseconds: `time.year`, `month`, `day`, `hour`, `minute`, `second`, `millis`, `weekday`, and `dayOfYear` are strictly typed scalar accessors, normalize negative pre-epoch timestamps correctly, lower through the native UTC calendar conversion on current targets, participate in LSP completion/signature help, and tree-shake when unreachable. First-class calendar values, formatting, named time zones/local-time conversion, and richer duration types remain pending.
  - [x] Verified allocation-free local-calendar decomposition from Unix milliseconds: `time.localYear`, `localMonth`, `localDay`, `localHour`, `localMinute`, `localSecond`, `localMillisecond`, `localWeekday`, and `localDayOfYear` are strictly typed scalar accessors lowered through native `localtime_r`, preserve negative pre-epoch millisecond normalization, participate in LSP completion/signature help, and tree-shake when unreachable. Named time-zone selection and first-class calendar values remain pending.
  - [x] Allocation-free UTC calendar construction with `time.utc(year, month, day, hour, minute, second, millisecond)`: component arguments are strictly typed, statically known invalid ranges/impossible month-day combinations are rejected during checking, dynamic invalid inputs trap instead of silently normalizing, native lowering uses checked integer calendar arithmetic including pre-epoch dates, LSP completion/signature help expose the full contract, and unreachable use tree-shakes completely.
  - [x] Verified UTC formatting follow-up: `time.format(unixMillis, callback)` (compatibility spelling `time.formatUtc`) emits a bounded ISO-8601 UTC timestamp from a fixed native stack buffer, preserves negative pre-epoch millisecond normalization, zero-pads years before 1 CE using ISO extended-year width, lends the string only to the exact callback, and is covered by native execution plus LSP completion/signature-help regression tests. Owned calendar values, named time zones, local-time conversion, and richer duration types remain pending.
  - [x] Verified local formatting follow-up: `time.formatLocal(unixMillis, callback)` emits a bounded ISO-8601 local-calendar timestamp through native `localtime_r`, preserves negative pre-epoch millisecond normalization, lends the stack-buffer string only to the exact callback, participates in LSP completion/signature help, and is covered by native execution under a controlled `TZ`; named time-zone selection remains pending.
  - [x] Fixed-offset formatting follow-up: `time.formatOffset(unixMillis, offsetMinutes, callback)` emits a bounded ISO-8601 timestamp from UTC plus a checked `-1439..=1439` minute offset, preserves negative pre-epoch normalization, lends the stack buffer only to the exact callback, and is covered by native execution plus LSP completion/signature-help regression tests. Named IANA time-zone selection remains pending.
  - [x] Host local-offset follow-up: `time.localOffset(unixMillis) -> i64` returns the native host time-zone offset in minutes at the supplied instant, including daylight-saving transitions, without allocating; native `TZ`-controlled execution and LSP completion coverage verify the contract. Named IANA time-zone selection and richer first-class calendar/date values remain pending; the allocation-free structural `time.calendar` value is covered by the date/time follow-up below.
  - [x] Verified duration-unit ergonomics follow-up: `time.milliseconds`, `time.seconds`, `time.minutes`, `time.hours`, `time.days`, and `time.weeks` accept typed `i64` quantities and return checked integer milliseconds, using allocation-free native arithmetic with compile-time constant propagation and LSP signature/completion coverage. `time.isLeapYear`, `time.daysInMonth`, `time.daysInYear`, and `time.local` now provide checked native calendar validation/construction; first-class duration values, richer calendar/date values, and named time-zone types remain pending. `time.calendar(unixMillis)` now returns a typed allocation-free structural UTC calendar value with independently addressable named fields, native lowering, LSP signature/completion support, and native execution coverage.
  - [x] Verified named-zone formatting follow-up: `time.formatZone(unixMillis, zone, callback)` selects a bounded IANA zone name through a compiler-owned, serialized native `TZ` transaction, formats the local calendar with its DST-aware numeric offset into a fixed stack buffer, restores the host zone before returning, and lends the result only to the exact callback. Invalid/oversized/control-character zones and unsupported native targets return explicit errors; Linux native execution and typed lowering are regression-covered.
  - [x] Verified named-zone offset follow-up: `time.zoneOffset(unixMillis, zone) -> (i64, error)` returns the DST-aware IANA offset in minutes through the same bounded zone validation and serialized native `TZ` transaction, restores the host zone on every return path, and is covered by native execution, invalid-zone behavior, and LSP completion/signature-help regression tests.
  - [x] Verified named-zone calendar callback follow-up: `time.calendarZone(unixMillis, zone, callback)` converts one instant through a bounded IANA zone transaction and lends year/month/day/time/millisecond/weekday/day-of-year plus the DST-aware offset to an exact typed callback without allocating or exposing native time-zone objects; invalid zones and timestamp overflow return explicit `error` values, and native execution covers the New York offset transition path.
  - [x] Structural duration values now use an allocation-free `(milliseconds: i64)` record from `time.duration(milliseconds)`; its field is directly addressable and the value is accepted by `time.sleep`, `time.after`, and `time.every`, while existing scalar duration constructors remain checked and compatible. Constant negative structural durations are rejected at compile time for sleep/one-shot timers, and zero structural durations are rejected for repeating timers just like raw duration literals.
- [ ] JSON parsing/encoding.
  - [x] Callback-free `json.validate(value) -> error` validates bounded JSON syntax through the existing native parser without exposing callback tokens, with compiler, LSP, documentation, and native end-to-end coverage; owned JSON trees and value-producing parsing remain pending.
  - [x] Verified JSON descriptor-stride hardening: direct, nested, recursive, optional, and generated aggregate JSON encoders reject negative or undersized borrowed list/map strides before typed pointer arithmetic, while preserving zero-stride native defaults. Compiler regression coverage verifies the guards are emitted for nested array/object paths; owned dynamic JSON values remain pending.
  - [x] Verified descriptor-offset hardening: the shared native JSON stride guard also bounds the largest possible `index * stride` offset for the 65536-element contract, preventing signed `ptrdiff_t` overflow before direct, nested, recursive, optional, and aggregate encoders dereference borrowed storage; zero-stride defaults remain unchanged and generated-C regression coverage pins the guard. Owned dynamic JSON values remain pending.

# Milestone 6 — Concurrency

- [x] Compiler-owned native task model for the current `async fn` / direct-`await` bootstrap: each reachable async invocation lowers to private task state containing copied arguments, native pthread execution, declared single/multi-value result storage, and deterministic join/free at `await`, with no source-level task/future generic or callback-shaped application API. Task boundaries currently admit only compiler-classified `Copy` parameters/results so borrowed lists and other non-copy values cannot cross threads before sendability/owned-transfer rules exist; unreachable async functions tree-shake without pthread support. Structured concurrency, cancellation propagation, task-integrated timers/event loops, and higher-throughput scheduling remain tracked separately below rather than being implied complete.
- [ ] Structured concurrency. Bootstrap worker scopes expose `worker.joinAll() -> error`, which recursively drains every currently registered descendant of the calling worker (or every root worker and its descendants when called from the main thread) using compiler-owned parentage and ordinary native joins. Worker entry scopes drain all descendants automatically before the worker thread completes, so joining a parent waits for its full child tree and consumes descendant handles even when application code never calls `worker.joinAll()` inside that worker. Headless root-program scope now drains all remaining root workers automatically on every synchronous or asynchronous `main` return; cleanup failures are surfaced on stderr and turn an otherwise-successful exit into failure, while programs that never reach worker APIs retain a zero-cost identity main-return macro and no pthread runtime. Async tasks now receive compiler-private worker-scope identities whenever the worker runtime is reachable; workers started inside a task are drained before that task publishes completion or resumes its awaiting parent, and continuation resumption re-enters the parent task scope so workers started after suspension stay attached to the correct task. Async-only programs that never reach worker APIs retain the tree-shaken no-worker runtime path. Explicit `worker.joinAll()` remains available for earlier synchronization, while `worker.cancelAll()` requests cancellation for every current child and recursively propagates that request through each registered descendant without requiring application code to retain every handle. Direct `worker.join(handle)` and `worker.cancel(handle)` are now scope-confined at runtime: only the worker/task/root scope that directly created a handle may join or cancel it, so passing the opaque numeric handle into a sibling worker cannot steal or cancel another structured scope's child; cancellation still propagates recursively through that owned child's descendants. Automatic structured-scope exit now requests cooperative cancellation before draining unfinished worker children for worker entry scopes, compiler-owned async task scopes, and headless root `main`, so cancellation-aware descendants cannot keep a completed parent scope alive indefinitely; explicit `worker.joinAll()` remains a wait-only synchronization operation and does not cancel active work. Structured cleanup now preserves the first descendant scope failure while continuing to drain siblings and descendants that are safe to remove, stopping only when a native join failure leaves a registered child that cannot be safely consumed. Compiler-private async task state carries worker-scope cleanup failures through continuation-based await chains and surfaces them at the blocking/root await boundary instead of aborting from the detached child thread. Source-visible handles can now inspect propagated scope failure with `worker.failure(handle) -> error` before consuming the handle through join; broader source-visible task-handle cancellation/failure aggregation and static ownership/sendability enforcement remain required before this item is complete.
  - Verified worker-completion descriptor hardening: `worker.waitAny` and `worker.joinAny` now reject missing non-empty handle storage, undersized/invalid positive or negative element strides, and pointer-offset overflow before entering the worker registry or dereferencing borrowed list data. Native completion-observation coverage pins both explicit errors while preserving zero-copy list slices and the existing scope checks.
- [x] Cooperative worker cancellation propagation: `worker.cancel(handle) -> error` marks one owned target worker plus every currently registered descendant it spawned, `worker.cancelAll() -> void` marks every direct child of the current worker/task/root scope and propagates through all registered worker descendants, and `worker.cancelled() -> bool` lets worker code observe the request without unsafe native thread termination. Worker parentage is compiler/runtime-owned through thread-local execution identity, children started after their parent is already cancelled inherit the request, cancellation state is synchronized through the worker registry mutex, invalid/already-joined targeted handles fail explicitly, main-thread observation remains false, unreachable cancellation support tree-shakes with the rest of the worker runtime, LSP completion/signature help expose all three calls, and native regression coverage proves both targeted parent cancellation and scope-wide sibling/grandchild cancellation before structured joins complete.
  Verified blocking-channel cancellation follow-up: `channel.write` and `channel.read` now wake when their owning worker or structured scope is cancelled, return an explicit cancellation `error`, and do not leave structured joins hung behind channel backpressure; cancellation broadcasts only touch compiler-owned wait conditions and ordinary non-worker channel use is unchanged.
  Verified blocking-timer cancellation follow-up: workers blocked in `time.sleep` or `time.sleepUntil` now poll their cooperative cancellation state at bounded 50ms intervals and return early when their worker/structured scope is cancelled, so automatic scope exit does not wait out long timer durations. Main-thread and async-task sleeps retain the ordinary direct blocking path; task-integrated asynchronous timers and task-handle cancellation remain pending.
  Verified structured socket-readiness cancellation follow-up: worker-scoped single- and multi-socket readiness waits now recheck cooperative cancellation at bounded 50ms intervals, return an explicit cancellation error, and cannot pin automatic structured-scope cleanup behind an infinite readiness timeout.
  Verified high-throughput socket cancellation follow-up: `net.writeTimeout` now uses the same bounded cancellation-aware poll path, while `net.acceptMany`, `net.readMany`, and `net.readManyFrom` recheck worker cancellation between drained items and return explicit cancellation errors with their completed count/offset preserved. Native regression coverage proves automatic structured-scope exit interrupts an otherwise infinite timed TCP send in under one second, while non-worker behavior and the existing batched socket paths remain unchanged.
- [x] Timers. Structured `time.after(durationMs, callback)` and `time.every(durationMs, callback)` schedule typed `fn() -> void` callbacks on compiler-owned worker threads and return ordinary worker handles, so callers use the existing `worker.cancel` / `worker.join` API and automatic structured-scope cleanup instead of a second timer-lifetime model. One-shot delays accept zero, repeating intervals must be positive, cancellation is checked in bounded 50ms sleep slices, unreachable timer support tree-shakes with pthread/time runtime support, and native plus LSP regression coverage verifies execution, repetition, cancellation, type errors, completion, and signature help.
