    - [ ] Interface-defined platform/native element contracts after platform capability rules are mature.
  - [x] Validate placement against declared track counts and define overlap policy: row/column spans that escape the declared grid are static errors, and ordinary sibling grid regions may not overlap unless the grid explicitly opts into overlay composition.
  - [x] Named grid areas are intentionally omitted from the current grid language: stable element names plus explicit row/column/span placement keep the layout locally readable without introducing a second naming/indirection layer. Revisit only if real application layouts demonstrate a material readability win.
- [x] One-dimensional flex/flow layout where explicit grid coordinates are unnecessary: `flow: horizontal` / `flow: vertical` auto-place flat sibling elements in source order, support canonical `flow gap` / `flow padding` / `flow scroll` directives, reject manual placement/spans and mixed explicit grid tracks, format/LSP-highlight/complete as first-class Flux UI syntax, and lower on Linux/Android through compiler-synthesized native auto tracks with no wrapper object, widget hierarchy, or extra layout runtime.
- [x] Explicit overlay positioning when requested: flat grids accept `grid overlay: true`, allowing sibling row/column regions to overlap deliberately while preserving ordinary bounds validation, source-order native composition, and the no-wrapper flat layout model on Linux and Android. Omitted/false overlay mode continues to reject accidental overlap statically; absolute coordinate positioning remains intentionally unnecessary for this contract rather than being smuggled into ordinary layout.
- [x] Constraints, min/max sizing, intrinsic sizing.
  - [x] Common `minWidth: i64` / `minHeight: i64` and `maxWidth: i64` / `maxHeight: i64` element constraints reject non-positive values, enforce coherent min/max pairs, and may derive from view state/environment on both Linux and Android so responsive constraints refresh the existing native element without rebuilding its hierarchy. Android uses native minimum/maximum setters; Linux combines native minimum requests with a compiler-owned GTK measurement/allocation clamp for maxima. `auto` grid tracks continue to use the native element's intrinsic measurement within those constraints.
  - [x] Common `visible: bool` can derive from view state on native Text/Button/Toggle controls and refreshes the existing control without adding wrapper elements to the flat layout.
- [ ] Responsive breakpoints/container queries.
  - [x] Compiler-owned portable window breakpoints are exposed as read-only `windowIsCompact`, `windowIsMedium`, and `windowIsExpanded` boolean view-environment bindings derived from logical width (`<600`, `600..<840`, `>=840`). They type-check like the existing window/orientation environment, participate in LSP completion, update through the existing native environment-refresh path on Linux and Android, and add no runtime layout object or framework dependency. Per-container queries remain pending.
- [x] Text.
  - [x] Bootstrap Linux GTK4 `Text` lowering for literal/constant/state-derived `text: str`, `selectable: bool`, compile-time positive `size: i64`, `bold: bool`, font family/slant/decoration, letter spacing, line height, and hexadecimal foreground color, including grid placement and Pango-backed typography without exposing GTK/Pango objects to Flux source.
- [x] Rich text: `Text.richText: str` is a compiler-validated compile-time portable inline-markup surface supporting nested balanced `<b>`, `<i>`, and `<u>` spans plus escaped `<`, `>`, and `&` entities. Linux lowers directly to Pango-backed `GtkLabel` markup and Android lowers through platform `Html.fromHtml` into the existing native `TextView`; plain `text` and `richText` are mutually exclusive, unsupported tags/attributes fail compilation, and no rich-text widget tree/runtime is introduced.
- [x] Images/vector images. The bootstrap native `Image` control uses a typed compile-time/state-derived `source: str`, `alt: str`, `canShrink: bool`, and compile-time `fit: str` (`fill`/`contain`/`cover`/`scaleDown`); Linux lowers to `GtkPicture`, whose platform image loader handles supported raster/vector files, and Android loads supported raster assets plus XML vector drawables through `Drawable`. Sources may remain ordinary runtime file paths or use portable `asset://path` package resource identity; arbitrary SVG parsing remains outside the platform-native contract.
  - [x] Android asset-backed vector drawable support: `Image` asset sources now load through the platform `Drawable` decoder before being installed on the existing `ImageView`, so XML vector drawables and bitmap assets share the same typed source/fit/alt/can-shrink contract without exposing Android resource objects or adding a runtime image abstraction. Generated Activity coverage pins the vector-capable path; arbitrary SVG parsing remains outside the native platform contract.
- [x] Buttons.
  - [x] Bootstrap Linux GTK4 `Button` lowering for compile-time `text: str` / `enabled: bool` plus typed named `fn() -> void` `onPress` dispatch.
- [x] Text inputs.
  - [x] Bootstrap native `TextInput` control with typed `text: str`, `placeholder: str`, `enabled: bool`, compile-time `readOnly: bool`, `autofocus: bool`, `password: bool`, `maxLength: i64`, compile-time `keyboardType: str`, `onChange: fn(str) -> void`, and `onSubmit: fn(str) -> void`; Linux lowers single-line input to `GtkEntry`, passes the current borrowed entry text synchronously to named Flux callbacks, and supports native password masking/validated maximum length/read-only selection plus text/email/number/decimal/phone/URL input purposes. `onChange: textState, value => value` and `onSubmit: textState, value => value` provide the callback-free functional form: the compiler copies the native text into state-owned storage before GTK/JNI releases its temporary value, replaces the previous owned buffer on later edits, and refreshes only state dependents. `text` remains an initial native value so refresh never rewrites the focused native editor or disrupts IME composition. Android read-only input disables its native key listener while preserving native text selection/copy, and Linux applies the matching read-only accessibility property on both single- and multiline controls.
  - [x] Linux GTK multiline bootstrap parity: `multiline: true` lowers to native `GtkTextView`/`GtkTextBuffer`, preserves word/character wrapping, delivers full-buffer `onChange`, defaults Enter to text insertion, and supports explicit `submitOnEnter: true` through a native key controller without application bridge code. Placeholder text uses a compiler-owned native `GtkLabel` overlay whose visibility follows the real text-buffer contents without wrapping the editor or intercepting input; password masking remains an explicit unsupported multiline combination rather than silently degrading semantics.
  - [x] Linux multiline `TextInput.maxLength` parity: a compile-time non-negative limit is enforced directly on `GtkTextBuffer` insertion using UTF-8 character counts, truncating only the overflowing insertion before GTK commits it. The native handler is generated only for multiline inputs that request a limit, composes with ordinary `onChange`/IME text insertion, and adds no controller object or wrapper widget.
  - [x] Portable focused selection/caret control through typed `textInput.start()` / `end()` / `caret(position)` / `select(start, end)`: Linux operates directly on the focused native `GtkEditable`/`GtkTextView`, Android reuses its compiler-owned direct `EditText` JNI path, reads return `-1` when no editable control is focused, writes return `bool` for invalid/missing/out-of-range positions, LSP completion/signature help expose the shared surface, and unused helpers tree-shake on both targets.
  - [x] Editable Flux-owned text state and richer state-driven validation are compiler-owned without controller objects: callback-free TextInput event-value transitions copy native edits into state-owned storage, while `validationState` and `validationMessage` may derive from view state/derived values and refresh native feedback in place on Linux and Android. Linux exposes the message through the native input tooltip/accessibility description plus a standard entry information affordance; Android uses the native `EditText` validation-message surface without replacing the editor. Flux deliberately leaves composing-region ownership with each platform IME rather than exposing a cross-platform API that can invalidate native composition; Android preserves composing spans across compiler-required rebuilds and Linux remains on GTK's native IME path.
- [x] Toggles/check/radio controls.
  - [x] Bootstrap native `Toggle` control with typed `label: str`, `checked: bool`, `enabled: bool`, and `onChange` event; Linux lowers to `GtkCheckButton`, while `onChange: state => nextExpression` reuses the same functional transition/state refresh model as buttons.
  - [x] Bootstrap native `Radio` controls use typed `label: str`, `selected: bool`, `enabled: bool`, and `onSelect`; sibling radios are grouped natively and `onSelect` fires only for the newly active control, while shared Flux state remains the source of truth for selection.
- [ ] Lists/grids with virtualization.
- [x] Scrolling.
  - [x] Flat root grids may declare `grid scroll: true`; Linux lowers the existing grid into a native `GtkScrolledWindow` with automatic scrollbars while Flux source remains a flat sibling element set with no scroll-wrapper widget hierarchy.
- [ ] Overlays/popovers/tooltips.
  - [x] Bootstrap common `tooltip: str` property is statically typed/LSP-visible on built-in elements and lowers directly to native GTK tooltip text on the existing control, without adding a source-level wrapper/overlay object. Rich/custom tooltip content, popovers, and explicit overlay positioning remain pending.
- [x] Dialogs/sheets. Portable application-only presentation now covers native alerts, confirmations, indexed choices, and lightweight sheets without exposing dialog/controller objects or user-authored platform bridges. `dialog.alert(title, message)` uses native modal presentation; `dialog.confirm(title, message, onConfirm, *, cancelLabel: "Cancel", confirmLabel: "OK")` and `dialog.choose(title, message, options, onChoose, *, cancelLabel: "Cancel")` preserve strictly typed callbacks and compiler-owned native actions; and `dialog.sheet(title, message)` presents a transient non-resizable GTK modal on Linux while Android anchors a native `AlertDialog` to the bottom at match-parent width. All four surfaces participate in LSP completion/signature help and tree-shake when unreachable. Custom dialog content and dialog-as-navigation semantics remain future composition/navigation work rather than reopening this completed primitive dialog/sheet contract.
  - [x] Portable native confirm dialogs through `dialog.confirm(title, message, onConfirm, *, cancelLabel: str = "Cancel", confirmLabel: str = "OK")`: the callback is strictly typed as `fn() -> void`, optional action labels remain ordinary typed strings, Linux presents native actions and invokes the callback only for acceptance, Android routes the same native `AlertDialog` acceptance through compiler-generated `FluxActivity` glue into the compiled callback, LSP completion/signature help expose the label options, unreachable support tree-shakes completely, and Flux source writes no dialog/listener/controller object or platform bridge.
- [ ] Menus/context menus. Portable `onContextMenu: fn() -> void` request activation lowers to a native secondary-button gesture on Linux and native long-click activation on Android, and native `WM_CONTEXTMENU` dispatch on Windows, including functional state transitions and a static conflict diagnostic with `onLongPress`. Native presentation supports both the existing compile-time `contextMenuLabel: str` / typed `onContextMenuSelect: fn() -> void` single action and compile-time `contextMenuItems: str[]` / typed `onContextMenuItemSelect: fn(i64) -> void` multi-item actions: Linux presents a location-anchored `GtkPopover`, Android presents a platform `PopupMenu`, Windows presents a UTF-16 `TrackPopupMenu`, and multi-item selection reports the zero-based item index directly into compiled Flux before native state refresh. Focusable menu hosts also honor the native keyboard context-menu conventions (`Menu` and `Shift+F10`) through the native platform path on all three backends, invoking the same request/presentation path without a source-level command object. No toolkit menu object is exposed to source. Submenus, menu bars, and broader menu hierarchy semantics remain pending.
- [x] Native platform control mapping where beneficial without exposing platform classes to Flux code.
- [x] **Beautiful-by-default visual system** that makes minimally styled Flux apps look production-quality out of the box rather than exposing raw platform-widget defaults.
  - [x] Platform-aware semantic design tokens for typography, spacing, color, elevation, radii, motion, and control sizing, with coherent defaults rather than requiring users to choose pixel values everywhere. Color tokens (`surface`, `surfaceRaised`, `text`, `textMuted`, `accent`, `onAccent`, `outline`, `danger`, `success`, `warning`, `shadow`) resolve through Android light/dark palettes or GTK theme colors; semantic typography roles supply native-scaled type defaults; read-only numeric tokens provide a shared 4/8/12/16/24/32 spacing rhythm, 6/10/16/pill radii, low/medium/high elevation values, and 120/200/320ms motion durations. Numeric tokens participate in compile-time Flux expressions, lower to constants with no theme-object runtime, are offered through LSP completion, and use the existing native logical-unit/density conversion. Platform-appropriate default control sizing remains compiler-owned (48dp Android / 40px Linux), while explicit values and hexadecimal colors stay available as escape hatches.
    - [x] Bootstrap semantic color authoring support: the shared compiler token table drives validation/native lowering and contextual LSP value completion for every common UI color property, so typing `color:`/`backgroundColor:`/border/shadow color fields suggests the valid semantic token literals instead of requiring memorization.
    - [x] Bootstrap semantic typography roles: `Text.variant` accepts `body`, `caption`, `heading`, `title`, and `display`; omitted variants use a polished body role, both native backends apply scalable role size/weight/line-height defaults, and explicit `size`, `bold`, or `lineHeightPercent` values override the role without requiring a theme framework/runtime.
  - [x] Native controls share one intentional visual language across a screen while preserving platform behavior, accessibility, focus, text scaling, and input conventions: compiler-owned roots, text, buttons, text inputs, toggles, and radios receive coherent semantic surface/text/accent defaults on Android and lightweight lower-priority GTK classes on Linux; native interaction/input semantics remain intact and application-priority element styling stays authoritative.
    - [x] Explicit Flux styling wins over compiler-owned/native role styling: Android clears any platform Button tint before installing an explicit Flux background/border/radius/shadow drawable, preventing `Button.primary` emphasis from recoloring an application-specified surface; shape-only Button styling now carries the semantic primary/ordinary surface into that drawable instead of accidentally making the control transparent. Linux application-priority element CSS already preserves the same explicit-style precedence.
  - [x] Layout defaults provide readable line lengths, comfortable touch targets, consistent rhythm, and sensible density on phone/tablet/desktop sizes without requiring design expertise.
    - [x] Coherent default layout now includes 20-logical-unit root padding and a 12-unit grid rhythm on both native backends, Android auto-sized Button/TextInput/Toggle/Radio controls receive a 48dp minimum hit height, Linux equivalents receive a desktop-appropriate 40px minimum, and semantic Text roles cap natural line length by default (72 characters for body/caption, progressively tighter for heading/title/display). `Text.maxWidthChars` accepts a compile-time override and `0` disables the cap; explicit `grid gap`, `grid padding`, fixed row sizing, and `minHeight` remain authoritative escape hatches.
  - [x] Automatic high-quality light/dark themes and accessible contrast, with platform/system preferences respected by default: Android semantic surface/text/accent palettes switch from the active `uiMode` (or explicit app theme) and cover root/control pressed/disabled/focused/checked states, while Linux semantic tokens resolve through active GTK theme colors; verified key Android text/background pairs meet at least 4.5:1 contrast without application-authored theme code.
  - [x] First-class polished loading, empty, error, disabled, focused, pressed, selected, and validation states so unfinished states do not look unfinished: every built-in element accepts a compile-time semantic `status` of `normal`, `loading`, `empty`, or `error` with contextual LSP completion; Linux lowers presentation plus hover/pressed/focus/disabled/checked states through low-priority GTK theme CSS, while Android applies semantic presentation styling after its existing native pressed/disabled/focused/checked state lists. Explicit element styling remains authoritative, and validation keeps its dedicated input semantics below.
    - [x] Bootstrap `TextInput.validationState` provides compile-time `normal` / `error` / `success` / `warning` semantics with contextual LSP completion. Linux lowers non-normal states to low-priority GTK theme classes backed by semantic danger/success/warning colors; Android feeds the same semantic palette into native `EditText` state tints while retaining disabled/focused behavior. Omission remains polished `normal`, and explicit application element styling stays authoritative.
  - [x] Custom themes and explicit styling remain straightforward escape hatches without adding a theme-object framework: application metadata can override any semantic palette entry with concrete `#RRGGBB` / `#RRGGBBAA` values (`surfaceColor`, `surfaceRaisedColor`, `textColor`, `textMutedColor`, `accentColor`, `onAccentColor`, `outlineColor`, `dangerColor`, `successColor`, `warningColor`, `shadowColor`). Existing element-level color/border/radius/padding/etc. styling remains higher priority, while Linux emits the custom palette directly into GTK CSS definitions and Android resolves the same generated palette through its compiler-owned native bridge.
  - [x] README/gallery screenshots are reproducible real application captures rather than synthetic mockups: `tools/capture-readme-showcase` builds the checked-in `examples/mobile_showcase` as a release x86_64 APK, installs/launches it in ADB-connected Waydroid, forces a known 1080x1920 portrait viewport at 360 dpi for the capture, validates the resulting PNG, and restores the emulator display settings afterward. The checked-in README image was regenerated through that exact workflow after live visual verification.
  - [x] **Main screenshot visual example:** the primary README showcase is now a funny but genuinely sleek **“Horse Tinder”** application with a polished native horse profile card, semantic color/spacing rhythm, tasteful profile jokes, and an immediately visible Pass/Like action row. The checked-in image is a real 1080×1920 Android capture produced from the Flux source through `tools/capture-readme-showcase` on x86_64 Waydroid; no synthetic mockup or hand-edited UI screenshot is used.

# Milestone 10 — Functional UI state and rendering

- [x] Local view state without `StatefulWidget`-style objects.
  - [x] Bootstrap explicit copyable scalar view state (`state name: type = compileTimeValue`) is compiler/formatter/semantic metadata and the current Linux/Android root-view runtimes store `bool`, `i64`, and borrowed `str` state without source-level state objects/controllers. String state starts from a compile-time stable string and transitions can swap stable borrowed string values; dynamic UI string equality/inequality lowers to content comparison rather than native pointer identity. Owned/editable strings and aggregate state remain sequenced behind the ownership model.
- [x] Immutable state updates by default for the current functional UI-state model.
  - [x] Bootstrap UI events use a functional transition form (`onPress: state => nextExpression`): the next state expression is strictly type-checked against the target state and evaluated before native property refresh; bool/i64 transitions support primitive unary/binary arithmetic, comparisons, logic, checked integer arithmetic/division, and conditionals.
- [x] Derived/computed state for the current primitive UI-state model.
  - [x] Views may declare typed read-only `derived name: type = expression` bindings. Bootstrap `i64`/`bool`/`str` derived values may depend on parameters, mutable view state, read-only window environment, compile-time constants, and earlier derived bindings; declaration order rejects forward/cyclic dependencies, derived values cannot be transition targets, semantic/LSP tooling indexes them, and the Linux backend recomputes them before dependent native properties refresh.
- [x] Explicit effects.
  - [x] Compiler-native typed-IR effect summaries now resolve reachable user-function calls through a deterministic fixed point, preserve pure recursive cycles, and conservatively classify builtins, foreign functions, unresolved module boundaries, suspension, opaque operations, and interface dispatch as effectful. The semantic database exposes these whole-program results for source-level effect checking and effect-aware optimization.
  - [x] Explicit `pure fn` declarations now form a source-level effect contract: the semantic analyzer rejects any pure function that reaches an intrinsic effect or an effectful transitive callee, including through recursive call graphs, while ordinary functions remain effectful by default. Formatter and LSP signatures preserve the annotation; richer effect sets and effect-polymorphic function values remain future work.
- [ ] Shared state/store primitives only where necessary.
- [ ] State ownership/lifetime rules integrated with the compiler.
  - [x] Bootstrap persistent view-state storage is now rejected or accepted during semantic checking rather than deferred to target codegen: canonical/aliased `bool`, `i64`, and borrowed `str` remain the only persistent state values with a defined native lifetime/storage contract, while optionals, `error`, aggregates, resources, and other future owned values fail with an ownership/lifetime diagnostic before Linux/Android lowering. This keeps the current object-free state model honest without pretending owned aggregate state is safe before its storage/drop rules exist.
- [x] Fine-grained dependency tracking for the current primitive UI model: codegen derives transitive state → derived-value → refreshable-property dependencies, state-transition callbacks carry the changed state identity, Linux and Android skip unrelated native element refresh work, and window-environment changes invalidate only environment-dependent properties while full refresh remains available for initialization and callback/lifecycle synchronization.
- [x] Minimal re-render/re-layout work after state changes on the current native application backends.
  - [x] Bootstrap GTK4 root-view refresh updates only state-dependent native Text/Button properties and does not reconstruct the window/grid; the dogfood toggle produced a localized render delta and the second toggle restored the app region to its original pixels.
  - [x] Bootstrap Android ordinary state transitions now preserve the existing native view hierarchy: compiler-owned stable element IDs let a generated refresh function recompute derived values and update existing text/labels, visibility/enabled state, toggle/radio selection, input hints, image content, accessibility text, tooltips, and transforms in place. Toggle/radio refresh suppresses listener re-entry; configuration/layout changes may still rebuild the hierarchy.
- [x] Stable element identity rules: flat element identity is derived deterministically from the declaring view plus the source element name rather than sibling position. Android lowers that identity to a non-zero 24-bit native View ID, rejects the vanishingly rare compile-time hash collision with a rename diagnostic, and preserves callback/refresh/focus identity across sibling insertion or reordering; Linux already addresses generated controls by their stable source element names.
- [x] No mandatory controller classes.
- [x] No build-context object threaded through user code.

# Milestone 11 — Automatic save-triggered hot reload

Flux hot reload should feel closer to React development than Flutter's manual reload workflow.

- [x] `flux run` watches the project automatically in development mode.
  - [x] Bootstrap `flux run`/`fluxc run` builds and launches a native development binary, then continuously watches the real project/import graph plus `flux.toml` where applicable; dependency-save rebuild/restart and broken-edit recovery are covered by an end-to-end CLI test.
- [ ] Saving a source file automatically triggers incremental recompilation.
  - [x] Bootstrap runner automatically recompiles on save through the reusable project-analysis cache: unchanged whole graphs reuse analysis/generated C, body-only changes reuse parsed ASTs and cached signatures for unaffected modules, and incremental codegen reuses unchanged reachable function C fragments while regenerating changed functions. Eligible host-Linux multi-module graphs now also compile/cache one native object per source module, including ordinary GTK applications, OpenSSL-backed crypto helpers, modules that share named struct/enum layouts across object boundaries, and SQLite/network/TLS-linked code: SQLite's process-wide handle registry/cleanup state, the network socket ownership/cleanup registry, and TLS session/resumption registries are isolated into reusable native runtime state while per-function helpers keep shared database, socket, and TLS handles valid across cached objects. The compiler emits the application runtime under the app declaration's stable source identity, so an imported-module body edit recompiles only that module object and relinks against unchanged consumers/root UI/runtime state. Other shared mutable runtime prefixes, non-Linux native targets, and single-source root-view changes still retain broader native recompilation, so the milestone remains open.
- [x] No hot-reload hotkey is required for the normal workflow: the bootstrap runner reacts to saves automatically.

Verified hot-apply lifecycle follow-up: literal Text `italic`, `underline`, and `strikethrough` declarations now hot-apply across add/change/remove transitions against their unconditional omitted `false` defaults, with live Pango attribute patch handlers emitted even when the original source omitted those properties. `bold` now has the same lifecycle behavior for default body Text where omission is unambiguously `false`; Text with an explicit semantic `variant` keeps bold declaration add/remove on controlled restart so future variant patches cannot use stale override precedence.

Verified TextInput password lifecycle follow-up: single-line inputs now hot-apply literal `password` declaration add/change/remove transitions against the omitted `false` default, with the GTK visibility/input-purpose patch handler emitted even when the original source omitted `password`. State-driven values and multiline inputs remain on controlled restart.

Verified callback-free selection lifecycle follow-up: `Toggle.checked` and `Radio.selected` now hot-apply literal add/change/remove transitions against their omitted `false` defaults when no application selection callback is attached. The live GTK process retains patch handlers even when those properties were originally omitted; controls with `onChange`/`onSelect` callbacks remain on controlled restart so hot patches cannot synthesize or bypass application selection events.

Verified spacing lifecycle follow-up: common literal `margin` declarations now hot-apply across add/change/remove transitions against the omitted zero default. The live GTK handler is emitted even when `margin` was originally absent and preserves explicit per-side margin precedence, so removing a base margin resets only unshadowed sides.

Verified hot-apply lifecycle follow-up: literal `clip` declarations now share the existing `visible` lifecycle path, so omitted → explicit `false` is a no-op, omitted/false → `true` patches the live GTK overflow state, and removing `clip` restores the implicit `false` default without changing the development ABI. The live process emits the clip patch handler even when the original source omitted the property.

Verified common-alignment lifecycle follow-up: literal `alignX` / `alignY` declarations now hot-apply across add/change/remove transitions against GTK's native `fill` default. The development runner keeps both alignment patch handlers available even when declarations were absent at launch, removal restores fill without a restart, and passive tappable Text/Image patches target their compiler-owned native button host so live alignment matches initial layout semantics. State-driven alignment remains structural and follows the controlled restart path.

Verified presentation-status lifecycle follow-up: literal common `status` declarations are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. The compiler-owned omitted state is `normal`; the GTK status-class patch handler is retained from launch, explicit normal is an in-process no-op, loading/empty/error hot-apply to the existing native host, and declaration removal clears status classes back to normal. The existing portable contract remains compile-time-only, so state-driven status is still rejected during semantic analysis.
- [ ] Apply compatible function/view changes to the running process automatically. Bootstrap Linux development runners can now hot-apply ABI-compatible static root-view property changes in process. Supported strings include `Text.text`, validated `Text.richText` markup, plus Text `color` and alignment/wrap-mode/ellipsize layout values, `Button.text`, `Header.text`, Toggle/Radio and semantic-control labels/titles, `Image.source`/`Image.alt`/`Image.fit`, common `alignX`/`alignY`, accessibility label/description/value text plus precedence-safe action/long-press labels, tooltip text, callback-free `TextInput.text`, `TextInput.placeholder`, `TextInput.keyboardType`, and semantic presentation `status`; TextInput validation state/message literals update the existing native validation styling, message affordance, and accessibility description in place. Safe boolean setters include common visibility/clip/focusability/accessibility-hidden state; static `autofocus` can hot-apply the compatible false→true transition by focusing the existing control, while true→false retains controlled restart. Other safe booleans include Button/TextInput/Toggle/Radio enabled state, callback-free Toggle.checked/Radio.selected selection state, Text selectability/wrapping, Image shrink behavior, Button primary styling, and TextInput read-only/password state (password remains single-line). Static `Text.size`/`Text.bold`/`Text.italic`/`Text.underline`/`Text.strikethrough`/`Text.fontFamily`/`Text.letterSpacing`/`Text.lineHeightPercent`/`Text.maxLines`/`Text.maxWidthChars`, `Button.size`, `TextInput.maxLength`, common `focusScope`, `layoutTransitionMs`, literal `minWidth`/`minHeight`/`maxWidth`/`maxHeight`, including paired static min/max constraints on the same axis, `margin`/per-side margin, precedence-safe `borderWidth`/per-edge border widths, compatible `padding`/per-side padding plus `radius`/per-corner radius integer literals, and common `backgroundColor`/precedence-safe `borderColor`/per-edge border colors plus `shadowColor`/`shadowBlur`/signed `shadowOffsetX`/`shadowOffsetY` and fully literal translate/rotate/scale/skew/transform-origin groups update their existing native controls in place; present `dragTranslate`/`pinchScale` booleans retain compiler-owned native recognizers and hot-toggle mutable enable flags in process, resetting live gesture offsets/scale when disabled while omitted properties still emit no gesture machinery; focus-scope patches update compiler-owned native navigation metadata, minimum-size patches preserve the untouched axis and fixed grid-track floor, maximum-size patches mutate the existing compiler-owned constraint wrapper without rebuilding the child, Text typography patches replace only the changed Pango attribute while preserving the rest of the label styling, and Text color patches discard only stale foreground Pango attributes before handing color back to the compiler-owned CSS provider, base margin/padding/radius and border-width patches update only unshadowed sides/corners/edges while preserving explicit overrides, and CSS spacing/radius/color plus `borderStyle` and `transitionMs`/`transitionDelayMs`/`transitionEasing` patches reuse one bounded compiler-owned GTK provider per property so sequential saves preserve unrelated live edits; color payloads, including shadow colors, are revalidated in the live process; shadow color/blur/offset patches and fully static transform groups each share one compiler-owned mutable composite provider so sequential saves preserve their other live components and semantic tokens resolve through the existing GTK theme aliases, while live transition timing providers retain the compiler-owned reduced-motion override and easing validation. Multiline inputs keep their insert callback while the patch updates its compiler-owned mutable limit, multiline placeholders use a compiler-owned non-interactive label overlay whose text and empty-buffer visibility update in place, button text patches preserve the existing label attributes, callback-free input text changes update the existing GTK entry/text buffer, keyboard-type changes map directly to the existing GTK entry/text-view input purpose, shortcut-trigger changes replace only the retained compiler-owned `GtkShortcut` while preserving its controller scope and action callback, same-shape `contextMenuItems` edits replace compiler-owned item labels used by future native popovers while list-count changes deliberately restart, unshadowed `accessibilityActions` list edits update GTK's compiler-owned accessible-description fallback in place even when the label count changes, `accessibilityOrder` edits update compiler-owned order values and rebuild the existing GTK `FLOW_TO` chain after the patch batch without reconstructing controls, image-source changes reuse the bounded asset/path resolver, and image-fit changes map directly to GTK picture content fit. Literal values are canonical-source-masked so the compiler proves no other source semantics changed, then the runner writes bounded/versioned element+property records and signals the live GTK process to update existing widgets without rebuilding or restarting. Patch property names are normalized to compiler-internal identity; boolean payloads are compiler-owned `0`/`1`, integer payloads are fully parsed and range-checked before native application, and portable enum-like string patches are validated against their normal backend vocabularies before bypassing native codegen. Selection state with `onChange`/`onSelect` callbacks and TextInput text with `onChange` remain on controlled restart, TextInput password patches remain restricted to non-multiline inputs, and TextInput tooltip/validation-message combinations or validation messages competing with an explicit accessibility description deliberately retain controlled restart so the live process never guesses cross-property precedence. Other compatible properties, function-body replacement, Android/Windows support, and general symbol indirection remain pending.
- [x] Preserve compatible application/UI state across reloads for the current Linux native development runner: compatible primitive view state (`bool`, `i64`, and `str`) is written through an atomic, versioned snapshot before the replacement process exits and restored by stable state name/type, so added, removed, or type-changed state resets independently while unchanged state survives; incompatible or corrupt records are discarded, while true in-process Fast Refresh and cross-target state transfer remain tracked separately.
- [ ] Fast Refresh-style boundary detection: reset only state that is no longer structurally compatible. Controlled Linux development restarts now model the root-view state boundary explicitly: state is preserved only when root identity, state name, canonical type, and TextInput-owned string shape remain compatible; type/ownership changes and newly added state reset independently, removed state is dropped, and a root-view change resets the new root wholesale. `flux run` publishes preserved/reset/dropped counts in development status and logs the exact reset/dropped names, matching the existing bounded reload-state reader that restores records independently. Composed/nested view state, route-stack state, and future owned aggregate state still need equivalent structural boundaries.
- [x] Automatically fall back to a controlled restart when a change cannot be safely hot-applied: until compatible in-process hot-apply exists, every successful source rebuild follows the controlled replacement path, compiling the next binary before stopping the current child, then swapping binaries and restarting it; failed rebuilds leave the last good process running. The end-to-end save-watch regression verifies successful restart, compile-error preservation, and repaired-source recovery.
  - [x] Bootstrap controlled-restart path builds a replacement binary before stopping the current child; failed recompiles keep the last good process untouched and continue watching for repairs.
- [x] Surface reload/restart reason in tooling without interrupting normal development: source detection, compile failure, child exit, and successful rebuild/restart are reported concisely, and `fluxc run` publishes a stable last-known JSON status keyed by the resolved entry for editor/tooling consumers.
- [x] Debounce/coalesce rapid editor saves before recompiling.
- [x] Watch dependency/module changes using the compiler's loaded import graph, refreshing the watch set after successful recompilation and retaining known paths across failed edits.
- [ ] Stable development ABI/function indirection strategy. Bootstrap development builds now compute a deterministic versioned ABI fingerprint from every loaded module's span-free declaration/type surface plus native view element structure. Function-body/UI-value/source-position edits remain ABI-compatible, while callable signatures, application/view state/layout, element structure, module identity, and other declaration-contract changes cross the boundary. `flux run` compares each successful rebuild against the last running ABI and publishes `abi_compatible`, ABI version, and fingerprint in reload status while logging structural fallback reasons. The first ABI-compatible in-process apply path now uses compiler-owned property-tagged string records for supported static root-view values without replacing code; source edits that are ABI-compatible and produce byte-identical generated C now advance the live development generation without native compilation or process replacement; general function/view symbol indirection and dynamic code loading remain pending.
- [ ] Incremental compiler cache.
  - [x] `fluxc run` owns a persistent `ProjectAnalysisCache`, avoiding the former separate check + compile analysis on successful rebuilds and reusing unchanged module ASTs across changed graphs. Watcher invalidations whose reloaded source graph is byte-identical now reuse the prior semantic analysis without repeating whole-program type checking.
  - [ ] Incremental semantic/type/codegen reuse for changed graphs, plus durable on-disk cache artifacts where they materially improve cold-start development builds. Body-only changed graphs now reuse unchanged function emission in the live runner cache and across compiler/runner cache restarts through a target- and semantic-surface-qualified, checksum-validated, atomically published per-function C fragment cache with full-codegen parity checks. Native builds split C compilation from linking and persist checksum-validated objects keyed only by compile inputs; eligible host-Linux multi-module headless and GTK graphs additionally partition generated C by stable source identity and cache one object per module, with the generated application runtime anchored to the app declaration's source unit so an imported-module body edit recompiles only that module object and relinks against unchanged consumers/root UI runtime. Named value-type definitions/helpers and stateless OpenSSL crypto helpers are duplicated safely as compile-time C declarations/functions in each translation unit, while SQLite-linked graphs isolate their process-wide handle registry/cleanup-registration state, network-linked graphs isolate their socket ownership/cleanup registry, and TLS-linked graphs isolate their session/resumption registries and cleanup flag into one cached runtime object so module/function objects can share database, socket, or TLS handles safely without forcing monolithic recompilation. Safety checks still reject other shared mutable runtime prefixes and keep non-Linux native targets on the monolithic path. Native binary/object caches prune old artifacts toward a 32-entry tier bound while protecting the just-published entry, preserving a five-minute concurrency grace window, and deleting matching integrity sidecars. Safe single-source host-Linux graphs now peel ordinary public and private Flux function bodies, including private dependency chains, into independently cached native objects by externalizing only compiler-generated C linkage inside those partitioned units; the remaining program/runtime stays reusable. Compiler-generated capture-free lambda and compile-time `bind` helpers now participate in the same partitioning: their internal linkage is externalized only inside cached units, dependency closure prevents unresolved helper references from splitting, and target/helper/program objects reuse independently across body or bound-constant edits. Multi-module graphs apply the same safe split within every source unit, so ordinary imported and root functions rebuild independently from unchanged sibling functions, consumers, generated function helpers, and the headless/UI application runtime. Broader partial-program assembly directly from durable typed-IR/codegen artifacts remains pending.
    Verified WebSocket object-cache follow-up: host-Linux partitioning now isolates the process-wide client-mode session table into one reusable runtime object, and function-local Base64 alphabets no longer introduce unnecessary static prefix state. Native regression coverage opens a WebSocket client in one cached module and writes from another, proving client masking mode survives the object boundary.
    Verified preferences object-cache follow-up: the host-Linux preferences runtime now builds its bounded path and directory storage in each caller instead of retaining function-local static buffers, removing hidden mutable prefix state and making preferences-linked multi-module graphs eligible for independent native object caching. A body-only edit to a preferences-producing module now recompiles only that function object while reusing the unchanged consumer.
    Verified named-time-zone object-cache follow-up: the process-global host `TZ` transaction lock now lives in one reusable runtime object and partitioned modules refer to it through external linkage, so `time.formatZone` and `time.zoneOffset` calls in separate cached objects still serialize mutation/restoration of host timezone state. A body-only edit to one timezone-using module recompiles only that function object while reusing the shared lock and unchanged consumer.
    Verified channel object-cache follow-up: the process-wide native channel registry mutex, handle list, and handle sequence now live in one reusable runtime object while partitioned functions refer to them through external linkage. A channel created by one cached module can be sent to, received from, and closed by another, and a body-only producer edit recompiles only that function object while reusing the registry and unchanged consumer.
    Verified worker object-cache follow-up: the native worker mutex, registry, handle/scope sequences, optional completion condition, and current-worker/error thread-local state now live in one reusable runtime object while partitioned worker helpers refer to them through external linkage. Worker handles launched in one cached object can be joined from another, and callback-only body edits reuse the unchanged launcher, consumer, and shared structured-concurrency runtime.
    Verified time object-cache follow-up: deterministic test-clock state now lives in one reusable runtime object, including its mutex/condition synchronization when structured workers are present. Ordinary time-linked functions can therefore compile independently while preserving one process-wide deterministic clock, and same-helper body edits reuse the unchanged consumer and clock object.
    Verified process-lifecycle object-cache follow-up: SIGINT/SIGTERM polling now keeps its signal flag and handler-installation state in one reusable runtime object instead of duplicating them across partitioned modules. A native regression arms the handler from one cached module, delivers SIGTERM, observes the shared flag from another module, and proves a producer-only body edit reuses the unchanged entry/runtime state.
    Verified async object-cache follow-up: debug-mode async task inspection keeps its mutex/list registry in one reusable runtime object, and compiler-generated async body/start/await/continuation/finish/release/runner helpers receive external linkage only inside partitioned cache units. Multi-module async builds now cache those helpers independently; a producer-body edit recompiles only the changed async body object while reusing task machinery, the consumer, and the debug registry.
    Verified locale object-cache follow-up: Linux locale language/region scratch storage now uses named thread-local buffers isolated into one reusable runtime object, so locale-linked modules compile independently. A native regression preserves LC_ALL=fr_CA.UTF-8 language/region parsing and proves a producer-only body edit reuses the unchanged consumer and locale-buffer runtime object.
    Verified JSON object-cache follow-up: compiler-owned JSON parse/encode thread-local capture and token scratch storage now lives in one reusable runtime object while cached function/module objects refer to it through external linkage. The native partition-safety scanner now ignores braces and static-looking text inside generated C literals/comments, preventing JSON syntax characters from falsely forcing the monolithic fallback. A multi-module native regression proves a parser-only body edit recompiles one object while reusing the unchanged consumer and shared JSON runtime.
    Verified instrumented object-cache follow-up: safe host-Linux Gprof, AddressSanitizer, timeline-profile, and LLVM source-coverage builds now use the same per-function/module native object partitioning as ordinary builds, with instrumentation remaining part of each object cache key. Coverage mapping/report aggregation is verified against partitioned objects while preserving original `.flux` source lines. Timeline and coverage regressions prove body-only edits add one instrumented object while reusing every unchanged object.
    Verified static-package object-cache follow-up: host-Linux headless static packages use the same safe per-function/module object partitioning before the final `-static` link. Static and dynamic object identities remain distinct, and a body-only function edit adds one static-build object while reusing unchanged objects; the resulting executable remains self-contained and runnable.
    Verified explicit-Linux-target object-cache follow-up: native builds using an explicit Linux Clang target and sysroot now retain the same safe per-function/module partitioning, with target, sysroot content identity, toolchain, and flags already included in object cache keys. A host-equivalent `--target` plus `--sysroot /` regression executes both builds and proves a body-only edit adds one target-qualified object while reusing unchanged objects.
    - [x] Normal native `flux build`, package creation, and Android artifact builds now use the same target-aware durable generated-C cache as `flux run`, so repeated Linux/Android/Windows-target builds reuse validated codegen artifacts while source, package-constant, target, translation, compiler-cache-version, and compiler-source-fingerprint changes still invalidate them, so rebuilding Flux itself cannot reuse C fragments emitted by an older compiler implementation. Full changed-graph semantic reuse and per-module codegen remain pending.
    - [x] Formatting-only edits that preserve source line numbers now hash through canonical Flux formatting and reuse the durable native C artifact; regression coverage keeps source-location `#line` mappings intact while proving the cache does not create duplicate entries. Semantic changed-graph codegen reuse remains pending.
    - [x] Verified changed-graph semantic/codegen reuse: body-only edits to an imported module or a flat view recheck only the changed source against cached signatures, preserve reverse-dependent diagnostics, and fall back to full analysis when a public type surface changes. The development runner keeps a function-emission cache keyed by function identity and codegen state, reuses unchanged reachable function fragments, regenerates changed functions, and verifies the assembled result against fresh full codegen. The same live cache now retains normalized per-function CFG/typed IR by function identity, so ordinary body edits rebuild normalization only for changed functions while unchanged functions reuse their reachability, ownership, and value-flow graph; durable CFG reconstruction remains pending. The same function-C fragments, generated capture-free lambda/bind helper fragments, and target-specific application runtime fragments now persist across fresh compiler/runner cache instances behind a target/package/translation/public-surface compatibility fingerprint; corrupt durable fragments fall back to full emission and are safely replaced. Fresh runners that first restore a whole-program generated-C hit lazily hydrate those durable fragments on the first subsequent incremental edit, so the whole-artifact fast path does not discard finer-grained reuse. Application fragment identity covers imports, aliases, interfaces/implementations, value types, constants, app/routes/views, and native target so an ordinary function-body edit can reuse the unchanged UI/application runtime while fresh full-codegen parity remains verified. Compiler-generated C temporary numbering is local to each Flux function, so changing one function's temporary demand no longer shifts cache identity or generated names for unchanged later functions. Native compilation additionally persists whole-program object files independently from link inputs, with regression coverage proving a changed static native library can relink against an unchanged cached Flux object. Safe source-module, ordinary per-function, capture-free anonymous-function-helper, and compile-time-bind-helper native objects now reuse independently across single- and multi-module graphs. Development codegen status reports application-fragment and generated-helper reuse/regeneration alongside named-function reuse/regeneration. Broader partial program assembly directly from durable typed-IR/codegen artifacts remains pending.
    - [x] Verified source-position-insensitive surface follow-up: incremental public-surface fingerprints now use declaration names, visibility, types, parameter/default shape, aggregate layout, interface mappings, route/view contracts, application metadata, and import identity without embedding source spans or line numbers. Application declarations and view grid layout are serialized explicitly rather than through AST Debug, so line shifts around ordinary UI edits no longer spuriously trigger whole-program type checking; durable typed-IR summaries are also published as independently addressable, target-qualified per-function artifacts so unchanged functions survive sibling edits, while per-module native codegen reuse remains pending.
    - [x] Verified application-fragment source-position follow-up: durable application/runtime fragment identity now reuses the span-free project/type-surface cache boundary plus canonical formatter-normalized app/route/view semantics, so unrelated function edits and inserted lines no longer invalidate unchanged UI/runtime code while actual UI property/state/derived/default changes still regenerate it. Fresh full-codegen parity and durable cache-restart coverage pin both reuse and invalidation.
    - [x] Verified runtime-prelude fragment reuse: the compiler-owned native runtime prelude now persists independently inside the durable function-codegen cache behind the exact runtime-capability identifier set plus translation/platform/background inputs, so ordinary function-body and temporary-numbering edits reuse the unchanged prelude across cache restarts while introducing a new runtime capability regenerates it. Development codegen status reports runtime-fragment reuse/regeneration, and fresh full-codegen parity plus capability-change coverage pin the cache boundary.
    - [x] Verified compile-time constant surface follow-up: incremental public-surface fingerprints include span-free constant expressions, so changed constants cannot reuse stale folded signatures while source-only line shifts remain cache-compatible; per-module native codegen reuse and durable typed-IR artifacts remain pending.
    - [x] Verified typed-IR artifact identity follow-up: durable per-function artifacts include the stable source module identity in both their manifest record and cache key, so same-shaped same-named functions from different modules cannot alias; target and schema version remain part of the compatibility boundary.
    - [x] Verified durable typed-IR manifest follow-up: native cache emission now materializes an atomic, checksum-versioned v4 manifest under `.flux/ir-cache` from each normalized function CFG (target, stable source module/function identity, function-local shape hash, node/edge/value/reachable-value/definition counts, and normalized borrow/move/call/return/drop event counts), alongside the generated-C artifact. Focused regression coverage verifies publication for formatting-only reuse and changed-source invalidation; per-module native codegen reuse and full serialized typed-IR reuse remain pending.
    - [x] Cache cleanup follow-up: `flux clean` removes both durable generated-C artifacts and the associated `.flux/ir-cache` typed-IR manifests, including per-function records, with CLI regression coverage so an explicit clean cannot leave stale incremental-build state behind.
    - [x] Cache self-healing follow-up: a valid generated-C cache hit now detects a missing, corrupt, or target-mismatched aggregate typed-IR manifest and atomically republishes the durable IR metadata (including per-function records) before returning the native artifact; regression coverage simulates independent IR-cache cleanup and verifies the hit repairs it without regenerating C.
    - [x] Durable typed-IR manifest retention now mirrors generated-C cache retention: atomic publications keep only the eight most recent project manifests and independently keep the eight most recent per-function manifests, preventing repeated development builds or large function graphs from growing `.flux/ir-cache` without bound; regression coverage exercises repeated semantic edits and verifies both retention limits.
    - [x] Verified typed-IR function-artifact lookup boundary: the durable per-function cache now reconstructs its target/module/function/shape identity, validates the versioned checksum and payload identity, and treats stale, corrupt, or shape-mismatched artifacts as misses; regression coverage pins exact lookup and corruption behavior. Cold changed-graph codegen can now reuse separately persisted generated-C function fragments, while directly reconstructing codegen from durable typed-IR artifacts remains future work.
    - [x] Conservative native module-object bootstrap: host Linux multi-module builds with no unisolated shared mutable runtime prefix are split at generated source boundaries, compiled into independently content-addressed objects, and relinked from unchanged module cache hits. Regression coverage keeps a module-private helper with its defining source unit, proves a one-module body edit adds only one new native object, and verifies the rebuilt executable changes behavior; non-Linux-target and unisolated shared-prefix cases deliberately retain the monolithic fallback.
    - [x] Linux GUI module-object follow-up: generated application/runtime C now receives the root `app` declaration's stable source marker, and safe GTK builds participate in the same source-unit object cache. A two-module GUI regression proves an imported callback body edit adds exactly one new native object while reusing the unchanged root UI/runtime object; SQLite-linked applications now isolate their process-wide handle registry and cleanup-registration state into one reusable runtime object so the same partitioning remains safe across database calls.
    - [x] Named value-type module ABI follow-up: struct/enum declarations and zero-state aggregate helpers in the shared generated prefix are safe to duplicate across C translation units, so named value types no longer disable source-unit object caching. Native regression coverage passes a public struct by value across cached module objects, changes only the producer body, observes exactly one new object, and verifies the relinked executable's changed result.
    - [x] Crypto-linked module-object follow-up: OpenSSL digest/HMAC support contributes only stateless static helpers to the generated prefix, so crypto-linked host-Linux graphs now use the same source-unit object cache. Regression coverage changes only an imported SHA-256 module body, verifies the digest path end to end, and proves the unchanged consumer object is reused.
    - [x] Concurrent native-cache consumer safety: object-cache hits are copied to per-build temporary link inputs before use, so another compiler process can prune an old cache entry without invalidating an active link. If staging races an eviction, that unit simply recompiles and republishes rather than failing the build.
- [ ] Target: save-to-visible-update latency competitive with modern React Fast Refresh for ordinary UI edits.
  - [x] Development reloads report stage-level analysis, codegen, native compile/link, restart, and total replacement-spawn latency in both the runner log and the machine-readable hot-reload status, so incremental work can be benchmarked against the actual slow stage instead of aggregate guesswork. Successful build metadata is retained when a short-lived replacement process exits immediately, preventing the `exited` status transition from erasing the incremental/timing result. True visible-frame latency remains pending until compatible in-process application exists.

# Milestone 12 — Input and interaction

- [x] Pointer/mouse input for the bootstrap native UI: common tap plus hover/leave events cover ordinary pointer activation and presence without exposing event/controller objects; richer coordinates/buttons/drag semantics remain in the dedicated gesture/drag follow-up work.
  - [x] Common typed `onTap: fn() -> void` works on every built-in native element without exposing pointer/controller objects; Linux lowers it to a native `GtkGestureClick` release callback and supports the ordinary functional state-transition form.
- [x] Touch input for the bootstrap native UI: portable tap and native long-press recognition are wired on current application backends; multi-touch/drag/scale/velocity semantics remain in the dedicated gesture work.
  - [x] The same `onTap` contract lowers on Android through the native `View.OnClickListener` activation path so pointer, keyboard, and accessibility `performClick` activation converge before direct Flux callback/state-transition dispatch, with no application-authored Java/Kotlin/JNI bridge.
  - [x] Common `onLongPress: fn() -> void` lowers to Android's native `View.OnLongClickListener`, consumes the recognized long press after direct Flux dispatch, and supports typed functional state transitions without exposing `MotionEvent` or listener objects.
- [x] Keyboard input.
  - [x] Bootstrap native text typing reaches `TextInput` through GTK's keyboard handling; `onChange` receives the current borrowed text and Enter dispatches `onSubmit`. Common `onKey: fn(str) -> void` now provides portable explicit key-down handling on Linux and Android without exposing toolkit event objects: focused elements receive normalized names such as `Enter`, `Escape`, `Backspace`, `Delete`, `ArrowLeft`/`ArrowRight`/`ArrowUp`/`ArrowDown`, `Home`, `End`, and `PageUp`/`PageDown`, while printable Unicode keys are passed as their text value. Linux uses a capture-phase GTK key controller and Android uses the native `View.OnKeyListener`; declaring `onKey` also makes otherwise non-focusable native elements keyboard-focusable.
- [x] Focus model for the current native application backends: initial focus, typed focus/blur events, portable traversal/scopes, and stable focus restoration across native UI refresh/rebuild paths remain compiler-owned and object-free.
  - [x] Bootstrap initial focus and focus events remain native and object-free: common compile-time `autofocus: bool` works on every built-in element on Linux and Android, automatically makes an otherwise passive target focusable, and rejects multiple enabled autofocus targets or an explicit `focusable: false` contradiction within one view. `TextInput` retains the same source spelling, common `onFocus` / `onBlur` events accept named `fn() -> void` callbacks or typed functional state transitions, and native traversal remains available. Android preserves the focused stable element ID across configuration-driven hierarchy rebuilds while suppressing synthetic focus callbacks during restoration; Linux state refresh keeps the existing native hierarchy/focus intact.
  - [x] Portable programmatic focus navigation remains compiler-owned and object-free: `focus.next(wrap: bool = false)`, `focus.prior(wrap: bool = false)`, `focus.first()`, `focus.last()`, and `focus.clear()` lower to native GTK focus traversal on Linux and reuse direct native View focus traversal on Android, with LSP completion/signature help and no focus/controller object exposed to Flux source.
- [x] Hover.
  - [x] Common `onHover` / `onLeave` events on native Text/Button/TextInput/Toggle/Radio controls accept named `fn() -> void` callbacks or typed functional state transitions; Linux lowers them through native motion controllers while keeping event/controller objects out of Flux source.
- [x] Gestures.
  - [x] Portable single-tap recognition is available through `onTap`.
  - [x] Portable double-tap recognition through typed `onDoubleTap: fn() -> void` callbacks or functional state transitions: Linux uses native multi-press `GtkGestureClick` recognition, while Android recognizes two native click activations within the platform double-tap timeout and dispatches directly into compiled Flux without exposing event objects or application bridge code. Single-tap/click behavior remains intact, including keyboard/accessibility activation through the native click path.
  - [x] Portable long-press recognition is available through `onLongPress`; Linux uses native `GtkGestureLongPress` and Android uses the platform long-click recognizer.
  - [x] Portable drag-offset recognition through typed `onDrag: fn(i64, i64) -> void`: callbacks receive cumulative logical X/Y offsets from the gesture origin without an event/controller object, Linux lowers directly through native `GtkGestureDrag` `drag-update`, and Android reuses the native `View.OnTouchListener` path with density-normalized coordinates before direct compiled-Flux dispatch. Ordinary tap/long-press behavior remains native, callback delivery refreshes the existing view state in place, and drag-and-drop payload/target semantics remain a separate concern below.
  - [x] Portable swipe-velocity recognition through typed `onSwipe: fn(i64, i64) -> void`: callbacks receive logical X/Y velocity units per second without an event/controller object; Linux lowers through native `GtkGestureSwipe`, while Android uses the platform `VelocityTracker` plus touch-slop/minimum-fling thresholds and density-normalizes velocity before direct compiled-Flux dispatch. Taps therefore do not spuriously become swipes, callback delivery refreshes the current view state, and no application-authored platform bridge is introduced.
  - [x] Portable pinch-scale recognition through typed `onScale: fn(i64) -> void`: callbacks receive cumulative scale percent from the gesture origin (`100` = unchanged), Linux lowers through native `GtkGestureZoom` scale updates, and Android tracks the native two-pointer distance through the existing touch-listener boundary before dispatching directly into compiled Flux. No event/controller object or application-authored platform bridge is exposed.
- [x] Drag and drop for portable text payloads: common compile-time `dragText: str` turns any built-in element into a native copy drag source and typed `onDrop: fn(str) -> void` receives borrowed dropped text without exposing toolkit event/data objects. Linux lowers directly to `GtkDragSource`/`GtkDropTarget` plus a typed GDK string content provider; Android lowers to native `ClipData`, `View.startDragAndDrop`, and `View.OnDragListener`; Windows lowers to compiler-owned OLE `IDataObject` / `IDropSource` / `IDropTarget` plumbing with `CF_UNICODETEXT`, system drag thresholds, explicit UTF-8/UTF-16 conversion, and direct typed callback dispatch. Compiler validation still prevents one element from assigning Android's long-click gesture to both drag start and long-press/context-menu activation.
- [x] Keyboard shortcuts for the current native application surface: typed shortcuts are portable across Linux/Android/Windows, work on Buttons and other tappable built-ins, support explicit window/focused scope, and reject ambiguous app-window conflicts at compile time without introducing command/controller objects.
  - [x] Bootstrap `Button.shortcut: str` accepts validated compile-time forms such as `Ctrl+K`, `Ctrl+Shift+K`, `Alt+Enter`, and `Shift+Space`; Linux lowers them to native GTK shortcut triggers/actions, Android registers the same canonical shortcut on the generated Activity and dispatches matching non-repeating native `KeyEvent` key-downs, and Windows matches `WM_KEYDOWN` / `WM_SYSKEYDOWN` plus native modifier state before invoking the existing typed `onPress` action. All three backends reject unsupported modifier/key forms rather than silently diverging.
  - [x] Common `shortcutScope: "window" | "focused"` defaults to app-window scope and can instead require the target native element to own focus: Linux maps the two contracts to GTK global/local shortcut-controller scopes, Android filters Activity-level key dispatch against the current native focused View, and Windows compares the current focused HWND before direct typed dispatch. App-window shortcuts support any built-in element with a typed `onTap` action while Buttons continue to prefer `onPress`, and the complete composed view tree rejects canonical conflicts at compile time, including repeated custom-view instances and equivalent modifier/key spellings. Generic controls do not synthesize visible accelerator labels; platform-native accelerator/menu presentation belongs to the pending menus/context-menus surface, while ordinary visible hints remain explicit text/tooltip authoring.
- [x] Context menus. The portable request gesture is implemented through typed `onContextMenu: fn() -> void`: Linux uses a secondary-button `GtkGestureClick`, Android uses its native long-click listener, state-transition callbacks refresh in place, and `onLongPress` cannot claim the same Android gesture on one element. Native presentation supports the one-action `contextMenuLabel` / `onContextMenuSelect` pair plus compile-time `contextMenuItems` with typed `onContextMenuItemSelect: fn(i64) -> void` for indexed multi-action menus on both backends without exposing toolkit menu objects. Focusable context-menu hosts respond to `Menu` and `Shift+F10` through that same native request/presentation path on Linux and Android; richer menu hierarchy remains tracked in Milestone 9.
- [x] Clipboard for the current native application backends: canonical `clipboard.write(text)` writes native plain text on Linux/Android, while `clipboard.read(callback)` safely lends clipboard text only for the duration of an exact `fn(str) -> void` callback instead of returning an ownership-ambiguous borrowed GUI string. The earlier text-qualified spellings remain source-compatible aliases but are omitted from default completion. Linux uses GDK's asynchronous text-read API and owns/frees the native result around callback dispatch; Android reads/coerces the primary `ClipData` item through the compiler-owned JNI boundary and releases UTF-8 storage immediately after the callback. LSP completion/signature help expose the canonical operations, unreachable helpers tree-shake, and headless use remains rejected.
- [x] Native IME/text-composition support on the current Linux/Android backends: Flux `TextInput` lowers to native GTK `GtkEntry`/`GtkTextView` and Android `EditText` controls so ordinary composition remains owned by each platform input-method stack rather than a compiler key-event emulation layer. Android snapshots/restores composing spans together with text selection across the remaining compiler-required native hierarchy rebuilds; Linux ordinary input does not install a raw key controller unless the source explicitly requests key/submit interception. Portable programmatic composition APIs remain part of the richer text-state follow-up rather than blocking native IME correctness.

# Milestone 13 — Navigation

- [x] Typed routes: `route name = ViewName` declares a compiler-owned route identity that resolves across the loaded project graph to one visible Flux view, rejects duplicate/unknown targets statically, participates in formatter/semantic/LSP keyword tooling, and lowers to no runtime route/controller object until navigation operations consume it.
- [x] Route parameters are inferred directly from the target view's typed parameter contract rather than duplicated in route syntax: parameterized views may be route targets, semantic route symbols carry the target parameter types, ordinary positional/named/default rules remain owned by the view declaration, and route metadata still emits no runtime object until navigation operations consume it.
- [ ] Back stack.
- [ ] Tabs.
- [ ] Nested navigation where genuinely needed.
- [ ] Deep links.
  - [x] Bootstrap native deep-link/open-URL delivery now shares the typed application `onOpenUrl: fn(str) -> void` callback across current application backends: Android declared deep links deliver launch and subsequent `singleTop` intents through the compiler-owned Activity/JNI path, while Linux opts its native `GtkApplication` into `G_APPLICATION_HANDLES_OPEN`, converts each `GFile` activation to a URI, activates the existing app window only when needed, and dispatches the borrowed URI synchronously without a user bridge or routing object.
  - [x] Desktop package URI-scheme/file-association declarations and generated launcher metadata: `[linux].uri_schemes` and `[linux].file_associations` are strictly validated/deduplicated, and Linux directory packages emit a freedesktop `.desktop` entry with `x-scheme-handler/*` and MIME associations plus `%U` document/URI forwarding. Typed route decoding layered above raw URL delivery remains pending.
- [ ] Dialog/sheet navigation semantics.
- [x] Browser history integration for the web target: `browser.path`, `browser.push`, `browser.replace`, `browser.back`, and `browser.forward` lower to the native History API, while `popstate` refreshes the compiled view and regression coverage verifies the generated behavior without a user bridge.
- [x] Android predictive back.
- [ ] State restoration.
  - [x] Current Linux and Android application paths provide bounded callback-based restoration: Android uses native instance-state storage, while Linux persists `onSaveState` output atomically under an application-ID-keyed state path (or `FLUX_APP_STATE_PATH`) and invokes `onRestoreState` before `onStart`; malformed/oversized records are discarded and no platform/controller object is exposed. Windows, portable configuration-change semantics, and richer restoration context remain pending.
  - [x] Web application primitive view state is automatically restored per application through guarded `sessionStorage` JSON and persisted after each refresh; malformed entries and type-mismatched values fall back to declared initial state without exposing storage objects to Flux. Native Windows and broader portable restoration semantics remain pending.
  - [x] Windows native applications now persist bounded callback state through compiler-owned Win32 file APIs keyed by application ID (or `FLUX_APP_STATE_PATH`), restore before `onStart`, save during `WM_DESTROY`, validate `FLXW`/16 MiB limits, and replace state atomically; broader portable configuration-change semantics remain pending.

# Milestone 14 — Styling, themes, responsive UI, and animation

- [x] Colors: Flux UI color properties use a compiler-validated, zero-runtime-object color domain rather than a separate framework `Color` object. Compile-time strings/constants accept `#RRGGBB`, `#RRGGBBAA`, semantic theme tokens, and `transparent`; invalid values fail statically at the property boundary, LSP completion exposes semantic choices, and Linux/Android lower them directly to native color representations. A dedicated source value type remains unnecessary unless future color arithmetic/interpolation demonstrates a capability gap.
  - [x] `Text.color` accepts validated hexadecimal/semantic/transparent colors and lowers directly to native Pango/Android text colors while preserving theme/high-contrast behavior.
  - [x] Common background, border, and shadow color properties accept the same validated domain across built-in native elements; semantic palettes/custom application theme overrides remain compiler-owned, explicit element styling wins, and `transparent` lowers without allocating a runtime color object.
- [x] Typography.
  - [x] Bootstrap native `Text.size: i64` and `Text.bold: bool` contracts lower to Pango attributes on Linux; invalid/non-positive sizes fail at compile/codegen time instead of silently rendering.
  - [x] Native text family/slant/decoration/spacing controls: compile-time `fontFamily`, `italic`, `underline`, `strikethrough`, `letterSpacing`, and `lineHeightPercent` are compiler-owned/LSP-visible `Text` properties and lower directly to Pango attributes, including range/empty-family diagnostics.
  - [x] Native text layout/overflow controls: `textAlign` (`left`/`center`/`right`/`fill`), state-aware `wrap`, compile-time `wrapMode` (`word`/`char`/`wordChar`), `ellipsize` (`none`/`start`/`middle`/`end`), and positive `maxLines` lower directly to `GtkLabel`/Pango layout controls. Variable font axes, richer weight/style enums, and responsive typography remain future typography work.
- [x] Spacing.
  - [x] Flat-grid `grid padding: N` is source-level layout data and lowers to native root-grid margins; `grid gap` already lowers to native row/column spacing.
  - [x] Every built-in element has compile-time non-negative `margin` plus per-edge `marginTop`/`marginBottom`/`marginStart`/`marginEnd`, with per-edge values overriding the common margin and lowering directly onto the existing native control. Common `alignX` / `alignY` (`start`/`center`/`end`/`fill`) provide explicit cell alignment without layout-wrapper elements.
  - [x] Every built-in element also has compile-time non-negative inner `padding` with `paddingTop`/`paddingBottom`/`paddingStart`/`paddingEnd` overrides, lowered into the existing native element stylesheet without introducing wrapper controls.
- [x] Borders/radii.
  - [x] Bootstrap common `borderWidth: i64` and `radius: i64` are non-negative compile-time dimensions and lower to native border width/style and border radius on the existing element; `borderColor` shares the validated hexadecimal color contract.
  - [x] Per-edge border widths (`borderTopWidth` / `borderBottomWidth` / `borderStartWidth` / `borderEndWidth`), per-corner radii, and explicit `borderStyle` (`none`/`solid`/`dashed`/`dotted`/`double`) lower through GTK's standard CSS border surface. A positive width still defaults to `solid` when no style is supplied.
- [x] Shadows.
  - [x] Bootstrap common native shadows use compile-time `shadowColor`, `shadowBlur`, `shadowOffsetX`, and `shadowOffsetY` properties on existing flat elements; Linux lowers them directly to GTK CSS `box-shadow` without wrapper controls, with validated hexadecimal or semantic theme color and non-negative blur. The implicit shadow fallback now uses the theme-aware `shadow` token rather than a hard-coded color; spread/inset/elevation semantics remain future styling work.
- [x] Clipping.
  - [x] Bootstrap common `clip: bool` is state-aware and lowers to the native GTK overflow mode on the existing element; changing the driving Flux state refreshes clipping in place without wrapper widgets. Shape/path clipping remains future rendering work.
- [x] Transforms.
  - [x] Common native transforms on every built-in element: typed `translateX`, `translateY`, `rotateDegrees`, uniform `scalePercent`, axis overrides (`scaleXPercent` / `scaleYPercent`), `skewXDegrees` / `skewYDegrees`, and percentage transform origins lower directly to GTK4's supported CSS transform surface on the existing control. Compile-time values fold into the static element stylesheet; state-derived primitive expressions reuse a persistent per-element CSS provider refreshed by the existing functional UI invalidation path, so button/hover/focus transitions can move/rotate/scale/skew controls without wrapper widgets. Animated transitions remain tracked separately under the animation/property-transition roadmap rather than making base transform semantics depend on an animation system.
- [x] Light/dark themes.
  - [x] Bootstrap application-level `theme: "system" | "light" | "dark"` metadata is compile-time validated and lowers to the native GTK theme preference when explicitly light/dark; `system` leaves the platform preference untouched. Semantic UI color tokens now follow that active theme; runtime theme switching and per-view overrides remain future work.
