- [x] Generated C header/binding support for the currently stable export ABI: headers expose ABI-v2 package/module-qualified public symbols and type identities, compile as both C11 and C++17, and cover the supported scalar/Copy aggregate/optional/callback surface without widening ownership-sensitive FFI contracts.
  - [x] Bootstrap generated C headers for the currently safe export ABI: `flux emit-c-header <file|package> [-o file.h]` exposes only `pub fn` declarations, supports `i64`/`bool`/borrowed `str`/`error`, scalar multi-return result structs, recursively `Copy` public structs/enums and their `Copy` optionals, public aliases, ABI-safe plain function-pointer values, explicit `int32_t` enum discriminants, deterministic zero-based variant tags, dependency-ordered payload layouts, and module-qualified ABI-v2 type/tag names. It emits C++ `extern "C"` compatibility and still rejects lists/interfaces/nested callbacks and other ownership-sensitive shapes until their cross-FFI ownership/layout contracts are explicit. The current ABI and borrowed-string caveats are documented in `docs/c-interop.md`; owned foreign values and broader foreign ownership rules remain pending under their separate Milestone 19 items.
  - [x] Generated scalar C bindings now preserve public transparent aliases as compiler-prefixed typedefs and expose public compile-time `i64`/`bool`/`str` constants as typed macros while keeping private constants absent. This adds source-level binding names without introducing runtime storage or widening the ownership-sensitive FFI type surface.
- [x] Android Kotlin/Java/NDK bindings: stable ordinary platform APIs are exposed directly as typed `android.*` calls backed by compiler-generated JNI/JVM or direct NDK lowering, and the manual native escape hatch now lowers ABI-safe `extern c` symbols for Android and links manifest-declared native libraries/search paths with NDK Clang. Ownership-sensitive signatures remain rejected, static third-party libraries can link into `libflux.so`, and dynamic dependency redistribution remains explicit follow-up rather than hidden bridge/runtime behavior.
- [x] Apple Swift/Objective-C bindings following the same no-user-written-bridge principle where platform ABI/runtime constraints allow it: `flux emit-apple-bindings <package-dir|flux.toml> [-o directory]` emits the package's stable ABI-v2 header plus a deterministic Clang `module.modulemap`, giving Objective-C a native module import and Swift the same Clang-importer surface without a user-authored bridging header/shim. Regression coverage on Nox validates the generated module with both Clang Objective-C modules and Swift 6.3.3 `swiftc -typecheck`; the surface remains the stable allocation-free C ABI, so ownership-sensitive values stay rejected until their ABI is explicit.
- [x] Windows native API bindings: supported ordinary platform capabilities including `windows.processId`, `windows.uptimeMillis`, `windows.messageBox`, `windows.open`, `windows.beep`, `windows.screenWidth`, and `windows.screenHeight` are typed `windows.*` calls lowered directly to Win32 APIs, while ABI-safe `extern c` imports also lower to exact Windows linker symbols and package native libraries/search paths feed Windows Clang. The same conservative foreign ownership rules apply, preserving the no-user-written-bridge and native-first contracts.
- [x] Linux native library bindings: Flux accepts typed `extern c "symbol" fn ...` imports that lower directly to exact C linker symbols with no application-authored bridge and rejects ownership-sensitive return/parameter shapes plus first-class escape paths that lack a sound foreign lifetime contract. Package `[native].libraries`/`search_paths` feed validated compiler-owned linker arguments through package-aware Linux build, run, test, debug, profile, and packaging flows; native-library builds bypass the executable cache so replacing a library cannot silently reuse a stale cached binary. The same narrow import syntax is now also available through the Android NDK path tracked above rather than being a Linux-only source construct.
- [x] Callbacks from native code into Flux for the stable allocation-free `Copy` C ABI: generated headers expose compatible C function-pointer typedefs, native callers can pass those callbacks into exported Flux functions, and compiled Flux invokes them directly with ordinary native calls. A C11 regression now compiles, links, and executes the full C → exported Flux → C callback path under package ABI v2; ownership-sensitive callback shapes remain conservatively rejected under the separate FFI ownership item below.
- [x] Safe ownership rules across the current FFI boundary: ordinary `extern c` imports are pointer-free (`i64`/`bool` parameters and scalar/void results), borrowed `str`/`error` pointer parameters or results require an explicit `unsafe extern c` lifetime trust boundary, ownership-sensitive aggregate/import shapes remain rejected even under `unsafe`, and foreign functions cannot escape as first-class values without a Flux wrapper. Stable exported recursively `Copy` aggregates/callbacks cross by value, borrowed exported strings remain non-owning, and unsupported future owned values stay compile errors until they gain explicit allocation/drop contracts.
- [x] Platform implementation modules behind shared interfaces: package format v1 supports target-selected `[platform.linux]` / `[platform.android]` module mappings behind one stable package-relative import path. Selection happens before type checking/native codegen, selected implementations retain the logical package/module identity, unsafe/duplicate mappings are rejected, and regression coverage proves both root-package and path-dependency selection without runtime dispatch or application-authored bridges.
- [x] Package registry: manifest SemVer dependencies resolve through a configured local-directory or static HTTPS registry provider, reject yanked/incompatible releases, fetch checksum-verified `.fluxpkg` archives into the compiler-owned cache, materialize transitive package graphs without install hooks, support offline replay/vendor/outdated/update flows, and load `pkg:<dependency>/...` imports from the exact locked release. Integration coverage exercises real registry metadata, archive fetching, lock creation, registry removal, and successful cache-backed replay rather than treating registry dependencies as unresolved placeholders.
- [x] Semantic versioning/resolution: package dependencies use a deterministic SemVer 2.0 precedence/matching engine for exact, caret, tilde, wildcard, prerelease, and build-metadata forms; the resolver selects the highest compatible non-yanked registry release and applies the same requirement compatibility rules before package loading. Local path dependencies may declare an optional `version` requirement that is enforced against the dependency package before any module import is loaded.
- [x] Reproducible lockfile: `flux lock <package-dir|flux.toml>` writes deterministic format-v1 `flux.lock` data for the complete declared dependency graph, recursively pinning path-package identity/version metadata plus exact registry repository/version/checksum/asset identities and immutable Git revisions. Package-aware command frontends refresh missing/stale deterministic locks by default, `--locked` rejects them without rewriting for CI, package loading consumes a current validated lock, dependency-metadata drift invalidates cached analysis, exact registry/Git releases replay through the verified package cache, and unchanged inputs regenerate byte-identical lockfiles.
- [x] Native/plugin package metadata: package format v1 supports a strict optional `[native]` table with a tooling/plugin marker, deterministic logical native-library names, and package-root-resolved search paths that reject absolute or traversal forms. Linux native compilation now consumes those validated library/search-path values through compiler-owned linker arguments, while callable symbols still require typed `extern c` declarations and ownership/per-platform restrictions remain enforced separately.
- [x] Per-platform package implementations: current Linux and Android package loading can substitute target-specific Flux modules while callers keep the same relative or `pkg:<dependency>/...` import contract. Each path dependency owns and applies its own mappings, non-selected target implementations stay out of the loaded program, and the mechanism adds no method channel, serialized plugin protocol, or user-maintained platform bridge.

# Milestone 20 — Accessibility and internationalization

- [x] Semantic roles: every built-in native element accepts compile-time `accessibilityRole` values (`label`, `heading`, `button`, `textBox`, `checkbox`, `radio`, `image`, `switch`). Linux lowers the role through GTK accessibility role-description metadata and heading level, while Android installs a compiler-owned native accessibility delegate that exposes the matching platform class semantics plus API 28+ heading state. LSP value completion exposes the role vocabulary, invalid/dynamic roles fail statically, and applications write no platform accessibility bridge code.
- [x] Accessible labels/descriptions: every built-in native element accepts typed `accessibilityLabel: str` and `accessibilityDescription: str`; Linux lowers them to GTK accessible label/description properties on the existing native control, refreshes state-derived accessible text alongside the visible UI, and the properties flow through the compiler-owned UI contract/LSP surface without exposing platform accessibility objects.
- [x] Accessibility-tree visibility: every built-in native element accepts typed `accessibilityHidden: bool`; Linux maps it to native accessible hidden state and Android maps it to view accessibility importance independently of visual `visible` state, including in-place state-driven refresh.
- [x] Native accessibility actions for the current portable activation surface: Android portable tap/long-press handlers use native click/long-click actions so accessibility services invoke the same compiled Flux path through platform `performClick`; Linux passive `Text`/`Image` elements with `onTap` are hosted by compiler-owned frameless native `GtkButton` controls, so pointer, keyboard, and assistive-technology activation converge on the same Flux callback without application bridge code. Explicit `onKey` still takes precedence for Enter/Space when supplied.
  - [x] Richer named/custom semantic actions beyond the primary portable activation action, including action-specific labels and additional platform-native action vocabularies where they are useful. Existing `accessibilityActionLabel` and `accessibilityLongPressLabel` continue to label the native primary/long-press actions. Typed `accessibilityActions: str[]` plus `onAccessibilityAction: fn(i64) -> void` now add named custom actions without platform objects in Flux source: Android emits native `AccessibilityNodeInfo.AccessibilityAction` entries and dispatches the selected zero-based action directly into compiled Flux through compiler-generated JNI. GTK4 exposes no arbitrary custom-action registration API, so when no explicit `accessibilityDescription` is present Linux preserves the declared action labels through the native accessible description instead of inventing a parallel semantics runtime or application-authored bridge. Future native backends must preserve the same direct/native contract.
- [x] Human-readable accessibility value/state text for built-in native controls: portable `accessibilityValue: str` lowers to GTK's native accessible `VALUE_TEXT` property on Linux and Android API 30+ `View.setStateDescription`, while state/derived-driven values refresh in place with the existing compiler-owned UI refresh path. Older supported Android releases retain their native control semantics instead of growing a synthetic accessibility tree; the property is part of the typed UI/LSP contract and requires no application platform bridge.
- [x] Focus ordering for assistive traversal on current native UI backends: every built-in element accepts a unique compile-time non-negative `accessibilityOrder: i64`; ordered elements form an explicit GTK `FLOW_TO` reading chain and Android `setAccessibilityTraversalAfter` chain while unordered elements retain platform-default ordering.
- [x] Screen-reader bridges for the current native UI backends: built-in controls remain real GTK4 and Android platform controls in the native accessibility tree, while compiler-owned `accessibilityLabel`, `accessibilityDescription`, `accessibilityHidden`, and traversal ordering lower onto those platform semantics rather than a parallel widget/emulation tree. Cross-backend regression coverage verifies the native control/accessibility surfaces; custom semantic roles/actions remain tracked separately.
- [x] Native text scaling for current application backends: Flux `Text.size` and semantic typography roles stay in scalable platform font units instead of raw pixels. Linux emits Pango point sizes so GTK's system font DPI/settings remain authoritative, while Android's generated `TextView.setTextSize(float)` path keeps scaled-pixel (`sp`) semantics and follows the user's font-size preference; unoverridden native controls continue to inherit platform/theme sizing.
- [x] Native high-contrast adaptation for current application backends: Linux binds generated theme CSS to GTK's live interface-contrast preference and adds stronger native outlines/theme foreground/background treatment when increased contrast is requested; Android API 34+ reads `UiModeManager` contrast, refreshes when it changes, and uses higher-contrast semantic palette values while preserving explicit application color overrides.
- [x] Reduced motion for the currently supported transition backend: generated native transition styling follows GTK's live system reduced-motion setting and suppresses duration/delay without requiring application code.
- [x] Keyboard-only navigation for the current native UI backends: native Button/TextInput/Toggle/Radio controls retain platform focus traversal and keyboard activation; common typed `focusable: bool` explicitly includes/excludes built-in elements and may be state-derived, with `onKey` implying focusability only when no explicit override exists. Passive Linux Text/Image elements with `onTap` now use a compiler-owned native `GtkButton` activation host, so Enter/Space, pointer, and accessibility activation all reach the same typed Flux action while an explicit `onKey` handler consumes those activation keys first; Android `onTap` lowers through a focusable native `OnClickListener` so D-pad/keyboard activation reaches the same action without synthetic touch events. No framework focus manager or platform object is exposed to Flux source.
- [x] Locale detection for current native targets: portable `locale.language()` and `locale.region()` return borrowed language/region strings without platform-specific source imports; Linux follows `LC_ALL` → `LC_MESSAGES` → `LANG` and normalizes the POSIX `C` locale to `und`/no region, while Android reads `java.util.Locale.getDefault()` through compiler-owned JNI. Both paths are statically typed, exposed through LSP completion/signature help, and tree-shaken when unreachable.
- [x] Translation resources: package `[translations]` entries compile language and language-region strings directly into native output; typed `locale.text(key, fallback)` resolves exact region → language → fallback using the existing native locale bridge on Linux/Android, keeps results as borrowed static strings, participates in LSP completion/signature help, validates manifest keys/tags, and tree-shakes the lookup/resources completely when unreachable.
- [x] Plural/select rules.
- [x] Locale-aware number/date/currency formatting for the current native targets: typed `locale.number`, `locale.date`, and `locale.currency` lend platform-formatted text only to an exact `fn(str) -> void` callback and return explicit `error`, avoiding an unsound owned-string escape. Linux lowers directly through native locale/monetary/time APIs and Android through compiler-owned `NumberFormat`/`DateFormat` JNI, with no user-written bridge; unreachable formatter support tree-shakes completely and LSP completion/signature help expose the portable API. Currency currently accepts whole-major-unit `i64` values until a sound decimal/money value type exists.
- [x] RTL layout for current native application backends: compile-time `layoutDirection: "system" | "ltr" | "rtl"` application metadata sets the root GTK/Android native direction without a framework layout layer; `system` preserves locale/platform inheritance while explicit direction flows through native logical start/end layout behavior.
- [x] Locale-aware text/input behavior for current native application backends: Linux keeps GTK/Pango plus the native GTK input method authoritative for locale-sensitive shaping/input, while compiler-generated Android text controls apply the current `Configuration` locale list and propagate the same locale hints to `EditText` IMEs on API 24+, with `Locale.getDefault()` compatibility on older supported releases. Android configuration changes rebuild the native surface so locale changes are picked up without application code or a platform bridge.

# Milestone 21 — Media, persistence, and application services

- [ ] Raster image codecs.
- [ ] Vector image support.
- [ ] Video playback.
  - [x] Android native media playback primitive: `android.play`, `android.pause`, `android.resume`, and `android.stop` use compiler-owned `MediaPlayer`/`Uri` JNI glue with deterministic release and boolean failure reporting; source code exposes no platform object or plugin bridge. Cross-target playback and richer lifecycle/streaming semantics remain pending.
- [ ] Audio playback.
  - [x] Android native audio playback is covered by the same compiler-owned `MediaPlayer` primitive, accepting URI/path-like `str` sources and providing pause/resume/stop without an application-authored bridge. Other targets and richer audio/session semantics remain pending.
- [ ] Camera capture.
  - [x] Android camera capture primitive: `android.camera(callback)` launches the compiler-owned capture intent and returns the resulting URI through a synchronous borrowed callback, with generated JNI result dispatch and tree-shaking when unused. Other targets and owned media results remain pending.
- [ ] Microphone capture.
  - [x] Android microphone recording primitive: `android.startMicrophoneRecording(path)` / `android.record(path)` and `android.stopMicrophoneRecording()` / `android.stopRecord()` use compiler-owned `MediaRecorder` JNI glue, retain only a native global recorder handle while active, and release it on stop. Other targets, permission UX, and richer media ownership remain pending.
- [ ] Hardware decoding interfaces.
- [ ] Files. Bootstrap scalar file/directory metadata includes size, modification/access/inode-change times, permission bits, numeric owner/group IDs, inode/device identities, and hard-link counts with explicit errors and no escaping borrowed data. Native callback-free mutation covers file truncation, file/directory permission and modification/access-time updates, and directory rename with path-kind/range validation and explicit errors; access-time reads preserve native millisecond precision. Bounded callback-scoped text reads through `file.read(path, maxBytes, callback) -> error` validate limits, reject binary/NUL data, and never expose the temporary buffer beyond the exact callback. Callback-scoped directory enumeration through `directory.list(path, callback) -> error` lends each entry name synchronously while the directory handle remains owned by the native helper. Owned reads, directory listings, byte I/O, and owned path values remain sequenced behind the owned-string/collection work.
- [x] Preferences/settings: Linux/headless applications use bounded, user-only compiler-owned preference persistence through `preferences.get`, `preferences.set`, and `preferences.remove`; Android applications use the same typed API through compiler-generated direct `SharedPreferences` calls, with callback-scoped reads, explicit errors, target validation, LSP support, and reachability-based Java/runtime tree shaking. Other target-specific stores remain separate platform work.
  - [x] Linux/headless compiler-owned preferences: `preferences.get(key, fallback, callback)`, `preferences.set(key, value)`, and `preferences.remove(key)` use a bounded tab-delimited append log at `FLUX_PREFERENCES_PATH` or `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/flux/preferences` (falling back to `$HOME/.config/flux/preferences`). Reads return fallback values or lend the latest stored value only during the exact callback; keys/values reject control characters and oversized records, writes return explicit errors, and the helper is tree-shaken. Android uses the same typed surface through compiler-generated `SharedPreferences`; macOS preference storage remains pending.
  - [x] Windows application preferences: the same typed API uses compiler-owned bounded append-log storage under `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Flux` (falling back to `%APPDATA%\Flux`), honors `FLUX_PREFERENCES_PATH` for deterministic deployment/testing, creates the directory through Win32, and keeps reads callback-scoped with explicit errors and no source-visible platform objects. Generated Windows code omits the POSIX preference-only headers/helpers; macOS preference storage remains pending.
  - [x] Preference directories are created with user-only `0700` permissions and the preference file is created/reasserted with user-only `0600` permissions, preventing other local users from reading stored values; native regression coverage checks the file mode.
- [ ] Secure storage.
  - [x] Android application secure key/value storage: `android.secureStore` / `android.store`, `android.secureRead` / `android.load`, and `android.secureRemove` / `android.erase` use an API-23+ Android Keystore AES-GCM key with authenticated ciphertext in private app preferences; reads lend plaintext only for the exact callback scope, failures return `false`, and generated Java/JNI support is tree-shaken when unreachable.
  - [x] Windows application secure key/value storage: `windows.secureStore`, `windows.secureRead`, and `windows.secureRemove` lower directly to the native Windows Credential Manager with compiler-owned application-identity/key target names, bounded UTF-8 credential blobs, callback-scoped reads, explicit temporary-buffer zeroing, and reachability-selected `advapi32` linkage.
  - [x] Linux application secure key/value storage: `linux.secureStore`, `linux.secureRead`, and `linux.secureRemove` lower directly to the desktop Secret Service through `libsecret-1`, use compiler-owned application-identity/key attributes, keep reads bounded and callback-scoped, wipe returned password buffers before release, and tree-shake both helpers and native dependency when unreachable. macOS secure storage remains pending.
- [x] SQLite interface.
- [ ] Database driver/plugin model.
- [ ] HTTP client.
  - [x] Bootstrap bounded borrowed HTTP/1.0/1.1 response-head parsing through compiler-owned `http.readResponse(socket, maxBytes, responseCallback, headerCallback)`: the API requires a TCP socket, validates a `1..=65536` byte cap statically where possible and at runtime, reads exactly through `\r\n\r\n` without consuming response-body bytes, validates HTTP version/status/reason/header grammar, lends response/header strings only to exact callback scope, tree-shakes when unreachable, participates in LSP completion/signature help, and has native loopback coverage proving body bytes remain unread for subsequent I/O. This head-only helper intentionally leaves body framing/decoding to `http.readResponseBody`; redirects, TLS, and owned response values remain pending at the higher client layer.
  - [x] Bootstrap plain-text HTTP/1.1 client framing through compiler-owned `http.request(socket, method, target, host, contentType, body, keepAlive = false)` plus bounded `http.readResponseBody(socket, maxHeadBytes, maxBodyBytes, responseCallback, headerCallback, bodyCallback)`. Requests validate framing inputs, emit Host/Content-Length/Content-Type/Connection headers, default to `Connection: close`, and can explicitly request persistent framing with `keepAlive: true` without transferring socket ownership; sends handle EINTR/partial writes. Responses validate status/header grammar, consume bounded `Content-Length` bodies, decode bounded `Transfer-Encoding: chunked` text bodies (including chunk extensions), or consume close-delimited bodies through peer EOF while enforcing `maxBodyBytes`. Ambiguous Content-Length/Transfer-Encoding framing is rejected, chunk trailer fields are validated and consumed within the existing `maxHeadBytes` resource cap with framing fields forbidden in trailers, length/chunked framing stops exactly at its wire boundary without over-read, and close-delimited framing fails rather than buffering beyond its configured body cap. Response/header/body strings remain callback-scoped borrows. Both helpers require TCP sockets, tree-shake when unreachable, participate in LSP completion/signature help, and have native loopback coverage for real close/persistent POST framing, trailer-bearing chunked responses, successful EOF-delimited responses, and close-delimited overflow rejection. Redirects, binary bodies, TLS, custom request headers, surfaced trailer values, and owned response values remain pending.
  - [x] Bootstrap validated custom HTTP request headers through `http.requestHeaders(socket, method, target, host, contentType, body, headers, keepAlive = false)`: the borrowed `headers` text is a CRLF-separated field block, every field name/value is grammar/control-character validated, framing-owned `Host`/`Content-Length`/`Content-Type`/`Connection`/`Transfer-Encoding` overrides are rejected, the complete request head is bounded to 8192 bytes, sends remain EINTR/partial-write safe with `MSG_NOSIGNAL`, unreachable support tree-shakes, LSP completion/signature help expose the API, and native loopback coverage verifies exact custom-header plus keep-alive framing. This deliberately provides a safe bootstrap escape hatch for headers without pretending Flux has the owned map/string response model required by `fetch`.
  - [ ] Make `fetch(url, method: "GET", headers: ..., body: "", timeoutMillis: ...) -> (FetchResponse, error)` the ordinary application-facing HTTP API, with compiler/runtime-owned URL parsing, DNS, TCP, HTTPS/TLS, request framing, redirects, response framing/decoding, cleanup, and bounded resource limits hidden behind one call. `FetchResponse` must own its status/body/headers so results safely outlive native receive buffers; the existing `http.*` and `net.*` socket/callback APIs remain the lower-level substrate for servers, libraries, streaming, and unusual networking. Do not implement `fetch` by returning callback-scoped `str` values or by introducing an untracked heap lifetime: land it after owned strings plus owned maps/byte buffers and URI/URL/TLS support make the return value sound. The first implementation may be synchronous; task-integrated/asynchronous execution should reuse the same request/response model once Milestone 6 concurrency exists.
- [ ] WebSocket client/server.
  - [x] The current Linux/server session slice is compiler-owned and native: upgrade, client handshake initiation, masked text/binary receive, text/binary send, control-frame handling, fragmentation, and close are typed, bounded, tree-shakeable, and LSP-visible; owned payloads, task-integrated scheduling, and mobile providers remain pending.
  - [x] Verified bounded client-host validation follow-up: WebSocket client handshake setup now uses the shared 255-byte bounded terminator scan before validating or formatting the host, eliminating an unbounded native string read while preserving the existing control-character and handshake-size checks; generated-C regression coverage pins the bounded path.
- [x] Background networking. Typed `net.*` / `http.*` operations can run in compiler-owned worker scopes without a user-managed thread/runtime layer, and the blocking HTTP server accept/request plus client response paths now observe structured cancellation within bounded readiness slices so automatic scope cleanup cannot leave background networking pinned indefinitely. Higher-throughput task/event-loop I/O remains tracked by Milestone 6 and Milestone 28, while owned high-level HTTP response values remain tracked by the separate `fetch` item.
- [ ] Serialization/code generation where useful without source generics.

# Milestone 22 — Testing

- [x] Bootstrap `flux test`: direct `.flux` tests or sorted package `tests/*.flux` programs compile through the ordinary checker/native backend and pass only on a successful real process exit; GUI `app` tests are rejected in this headless integration slice.
- [x] Unit tests. Package `tests/*.flux` programs now analyze in the package's real manifest/module context and may import source under test through the reserved root-contained `pkg:self/<module.flux>` namespace. Unit tests therefore exercise ordinary typed package code, dependencies, constants, native link metadata, and the normal native backend without test-only declarations, reflection, or a second runtime; direct standalone `.flux` tests remain supported.
- [x] Bootstrap integration tests use ordinary headless `fn main() -> i64` Flux programs; `flux new` scaffolds `tests/smoke.flux` so a new package is immediately testable. Richer assertions/fixtures remain follow-up work rather than hidden test-only language magic.
- [x] UI tests.
- [x] Golden/screenshot tests. `tools/flux-golden-test` builds and installs the real Android release showcase on x86_64 Waydroid, forces the canonical 1080x1920/360-dpi portrait surface, captures the native UI, and compares decoded RGBA pixels exactly against the checked-in README golden; mismatches fail with a generated pixel diff and the capture helper can target a disposable output without rewriting the baseline.
- [x] Accessibility tests.
- [x] Platform end-to-end tests. `tools/flux-platform-e2e` exercises both current native targets through user-facing release builds: Linux must execute and return the expected real process output, while Android must install through ADB, launch the generated `FluxActivity` with ActivityManager `Status: ok`, and remain alive as a real Waydroid process. The harness auto-selects x86_64 Waydroid or accepts an explicit device.
- [x] Deterministic test clocks/timers. `flux test --deterministic-time` runs time-aware tests against a compiler-owned virtual clock with a fixed Unix epoch and monotonic zero point; blocking sleeps/deadlines advance virtual time immediately, and `time.after` / `time.every` use the same virtual deadlines so tests can wake structured timer workers without wall-clock delay. The private test-runner activation leaves ordinary builds/tests on native clocks and is covered together with package-unit imports by a real native test-runner regression.
- [x] Dependency/interface fakes based on functions/interfaces rather than mocking class hierarchies: first-class function values provide lightweight single-capability injection, while ordinary interface values accept small fake implementations for grouped capabilities. `docs/testing.md` documents both patterns, and compiler regression coverage verifies the fake path uses normal typed native lowering without proxy objects, vtables, reflection, or heap allocation.
- [x] Source-level coverage through `flux test --coverage`: opt-in LLVM instrumentation is merged across executed tests and mapped back to original `.flux` source lines rather than generated C/stdin locations; ordinary test binaries remain uninstrumented and `flux doctor` reports the required coverage tools.
- [x] Benchmarks: the checked-in Flux/Rust/C native performance harness covers compute, collections, interface dispatch, and dynamic calls/control flow with output equivalence, runtime/code-size reporting, a checked-Rust threshold, and a push/PR CI performance gate.
- [x] Compiler self-tests across supported targets: `tools/flux-self-test` runs the complete Rust/compiler suite, then performs a real optimized Linux native build/execution and an Android x86_64 release APK build through the user-facing compiler. `.github/workflows/compiler-self-test.yml` provisions the Android SDK/NDK and runs the same cross-target self-test on every main push and pull request, so target-lowering/toolchain regressions fail CI rather than remaining codegen-only coverage.

# Milestone 23 — LSP and editor experience

- [x] Diagnostics reuse the same structured diagnostic model and source spans as the terminal renderer, including open-buffer project overlays; there is no separate editor-only error model.
  - [x] Bootstrap `fluxc lsp` speaks stdio JSON-RPC/LSP, negotiates UTF-8/UTF-16 positions, uses full-document `didOpen`/`didChange`/`didClose` synchronization, and publishes the compiler's existing parse/type diagnostics for open standalone buffers including related labels and structured fix data.
  - [x] Workspace/import-aware unsaved-buffer diagnostics: the project loader accepts in-memory source overlays keyed by canonical path, and `fluxc lsp` type-checks imported open documents against the real import graph while substituting every currently open unsaved Flux buffer. Diagnostics are republished across open dependent documents after overlay changes and filtered to each document's stable source ID.
- [x] Completion.
  - [x] Bootstrap `textDocument/completion` provides Flux keywords, builtin types/functions, current-module declarations, public declarations from the forward import graph, and position-aware parameters/local bindings declared earlier in the current function, including unsaved overlay signatures/details. Local suggestions are filtered by active indentation scope so completed branches/loops do not leak bindings.
  - [x] Qualified namespace completion for enum variants and interface capabilities works at incomplete member edits such as `Outcome.` / `Storage.`, including explicitly imported declarations. A tolerant probe preserves declarations/imports before the active broken top-level declaration rather than requiring the edit to parse first.
  - [x] Struct-field completion follows explicitly typed visible parameters/`let`/`var` values, transparent struct aliases, nested field paths, and single-return named function-call results while the active `value.` expression is incomplete; contracts come from the same local/imported program model. More complex arbitrary expressions can build on typed expression recovery later without introducing method semantics.
  - [x] Named-argument completion now also uses parsed local Flux function headers, offering the active `named_only` parameter with its concrete type while suppressing a parameter already present in the call. Incomplete edits that cannot be parsed remain tolerant; imported and richer recovered-call completion remain follow-up work.
- [x] Signature help for every current parenthesized Flux call form: ordinary/imported functions, first-class concrete `fn(...) -> ...` values, namespace-qualified interface capabilities with an explicit receiver, enum payload constructors, interface packing, and compiler built-ins (`print`/`error`). Active arguments are tracked across nested calls. Declarative view composition is property-oriented rather than a call surface and remains covered by UI completion/hover.
- [x] Hover/type information.
  - [x] Bootstrap `textDocument/hover` reports exact declaration types/signatures and safely resolves unambiguous usage-site hover across the loaded forward import graph, including unsaved overlays. Scope-resolved local shadowing and reverse-workspace discovery remain pending with richer reference indexing.
  - [x] Struct member hover is receiver-type-aware for nested field paths, so repeated field names across unrelated structs resolve to the field selected by the actual typed receiver instead of requiring global field-name uniqueness.
  - [x] Local hover resolves parameters/bindings by the active function and lexical visibility rules already shared with completion, so same-named locals in other functions no longer make an otherwise unambiguous usage disappear.
- [x] Go to definition.
  - [x] Bootstrap `textDocument/definition` navigation uses the shared semantic database and resolves declarations/usages only when the target name is unambiguous; when the current file imports modules, typed overlay project analysis resolves public imported declarations to their actual source-file URI/range. Scope-aware shadow resolution and reverse-workspace discovery remain pending.
  - [x] Struct member go-to-definition follows the receiver's concrete/aliased struct type through nested field access and navigates directly to the selected field declaration, including imported project sources.
  - [x] Local go-to-definition uses the active function plus lexical visibility rather than global name uniqueness, resolving same-named parameters/bindings in separate functions safely.
- [x] Find references.
  - [x] Bootstrap `textDocument/references` returns identifier occurrences only after unambiguous semantic target resolution; strings are excluded and Flux has no comment syntax. From an importing document, references cover the entire typed loaded dependency graph, including imported declarations and unsaved overlays. Reverse dependents outside that graph still require workspace indexing.
  - [x] Function-local parameter/binding/pattern/loop references are resolved through lexical visibility rather than global name uniqueness, so same-named locals in other functions/scopes are excluded from the result.
- [x] Rename.
  - [x] Bootstrap `textDocument/rename` emits workspace edits only for an unambiguous semantic target and a valid non-keyword Flux identifier; from an importing document it safely edits every occurrence in the typed loaded dependency graph and rejects collisions with existing semantic symbols. Ambiguous/shadowed names are refused rather than edited unsafely; reverse dependents outside the loaded graph remain pending.
  - [x] Function-local parameter/binding/pattern/loop rename is lexical-scope aware, edits only occurrences resolving to the selected local, and refuses names colliding with another local/parameter in the same function.
  - [x] Follow-up: project-wide global-symbol references/rename and fallback symbol resolution respect function-local shadowing. Unrelated locals with the same spelling no longer make an imported/top-level symbol appear ambiguous, and local declarations/usages are excluded from global reference and rename edits while the project graph remains compiler-typed.
- [x] Semantic highlighting through full-document LSP semantic tokens: Flux keywords, strings, numbers, operators, builtin types/functions, and semantically resolved declarations/unambiguous usages receive standard token categories with negotiated UTF-8/UTF-16 positions. There is intentionally no comment token category because comments are invalid Flux syntax.
  - [x] Project/import-aware semantic tokens reuse the same cached typed project analysis as other editor services, so imported symbol usages and unsaved-overlay graphs retain their resolved semantic token kinds instead of degrading to generic lexical variables merely because the document contains imports.
- [x] Formatting through `textDocument/formatting`, returning the canonical Flux formatter as a whole-document LSP text edit without a second formatting implementation.
- [x] Code actions/fixes for machine-applicable compiler diagnostics: `textDocument/codeAction` returns preferred `quickfix` workspace edits directly from each diagnostic's structured replacement fix.
- [x] Inlay hints where they add value: `textDocument/inlayHint` reports compiler-inferred types for struct/enum pattern bindings and range-loop variables, while deliberately omitting already-explicit `let`/`var`/parameter types.
- [x] Incremental workspace analysis.
  - [x] Reusable typed project-analysis caching: unchanged source graphs are reused across LSP completion/hover/signature/navigation/refactoring requests after validating every loaded source against the current unsaved overlay or disk text. Open/change/close invalidates cached graphs containing that path, and manifest-backed caches also validate the current `flux.toml` contents.
  - [x] Per-module parse reuse inside changed project graphs: the project analysis cache keeps parsed ASTs keyed by canonical source path plus exact source text, so invalidating/changing one module reuses unchanged module ASTs and reparses only changed sources. Parse-cache hit/miss statistics are covered by project tests.
  - [x] Incremental type checking inside a changed project graph. The project/LSP cache now retains the last successful typed graph across source invalidation and reuses its signatures when the changed module set has the same imports, declaration/type surface, manifests, and source graph; only changed function bodies are rechecked, while changed view bodies reuse the ordinary full-view validator. Declaration/import/manifest/source-set changes conservatively fall back to whole-program checking so reverse dependents are never skipped. Focused regressions cover successful and failing function-body edits, view-body diagnostics, signature-change fallback, and unchanged repeated LSP queries.
- [x] Package/module awareness: editor analysis now respects each source module's forward import reachability for unqualified symbols while references/rename additionally discover unopened reverse dependents, so broad package analyses do not make unrelated sibling declarations compete at a usage site.
  - [x] Open-buffer import graphs use the compiler's real module loader and in-memory overlays, preserving module visibility/import semantics across unsaved files.
  - [x] Bootstrap workspace/package discovery considers canonical open-file targets plus the nearest enclosing `flux.toml`, chooses the broadest valid typed analysis containing the current document, and powers imported completion/hover/signature help, go-to-definition, and dependency-graph references/rename.
  - [x] Reverse-dependency/scope indexing for dependents outside the current loaded graph and fully resolved usage-site shadowing across editor services: references/rename use bounded reverse-dependent discovery, while unqualified hover/definition/signature help/semantic highlighting resolve only declarations reachable through the current source's import graph and lexical locals win within their scope. Completion already loads the current forward graph directly. Typed project analyses remain cached for unchanged graphs; per-module incremental rebuilds stay tracked above.
    - [x] Bootstrap references/rename now discover unopened reverse dependents through a bounded deterministic workspace source scan, analyze every valid project graph containing the selected declaration, deduplicate occurrences across roots, and retain function-local shadow exclusions.
    - [x] Follow-up: unqualified editor symbol resolution considers only declarations that can actually occupy an unqualified source position and now filters those declarations by the usage source's transitive import reachability. Lexically visible locals take precedence in semantic highlighting just as they do in navigation/refactoring, and named-function signature help applies the same module reachability rule. Project-wide references/rename additionally require the scanned source module to be the declaration module or have an import path to it, preventing same-named symbols in unrelated sibling modules from being treated as references to an imported declaration.
- [x] UI syntax awareness.
  - [x] Contextual `textDocument/completion` inside flat `view` blocks uses compiler-owned built-in element/property contract metadata: grid declaration and built-in element kinds are suggested at layout indentation, built-in properties are typed and exclude already-declared properties, and composed view parameters are offered as typed properties when the current source/project parses.
  - [x] UI contract hover recognizes flat element-kind and property positions: built-in element/property types come directly from compiler contracts and remain available even while the surrounding view is temporarily incomplete; valid composed views show their typed parameter contract.
  - [x] Composed-view property navigation maps a property usage such as `Badge.label:` to the declared `label` parameter on `view Badge(...)`, including public imported views resolved through typed project overlays.
  - [x] Malformed-view contract recovery reuses the real Flux parser on the complete top-level prefix before the currently edited view; for recovered imports it then loads dependencies through the normal project loader with package containment and unsaved overlays. Property completion, hover, and property-definition navigation therefore retain both earlier local and public imported view contracts while the current view is incomplete.
- [x] Hot-reload status integration: `fluxc run` atomically publishes versioned starting/running/compiling/compile-error/restarted/exited status with generation, mode, runner PID, timestamp, and reason; `fluxc lsp` advertises `fluxHotReloadStatus` and serves the newest applicable status through `flux/hotReloadStatus` without coupling the runner and editor into one process.

# Milestone 24 — Debugger

- [x] Source-level breakpoints. Bootstrap `flux debug` compiles an isolated native debug binary and launches GDB with original `.flux` paths/line tables; repeated `--break file.flux:line` or function breakpoints can be installed before the interactive debugger starts.
- [x] Stepping. GDB `step`/`next` operate on the compiler-preserved Flux source lines rather than requiring developers to inspect generated C.
- [x] Stack frames. Native backtraces resolve frames to original Flux files and source lines through the compiler-emitted debug locations.
- [x] Locals. The debugger embeds a Flux-aware GDB support layer: `flux-locals` enumerates the currently visible parameters/locals using source-language names and values, and `flux-print name` resolves one visible Flux local without requiring the developer to know compiler-generated `flux__local_*` symbols.
- [x] Watches. `flux-watch name` tracks a visible Flux local and reports it with its source-language name whenever execution stops; `flux-unwatch name` removes the watch and out-of-scope transitions are explicit instead of silently binding a different native symbol.
- [x] Expression evaluation. Bootstrap `flux-eval expression` evaluates side-effect-free primitive Flux expressions directly over visible debugger locals instead of forwarding source text into GDB/C: `i64`/`bool`/`str` values, parentheses, unary `!`/`-`, checked integer arithmetic/division, comparisons/equality, and short-circuit `&&`/`||` follow Flux semantics. Function calls, assignment/platform effects, and aggregate/member evaluation remain intentionally unavailable until they can preserve Flux ownership/effect rules.
- [x] Async/task debugging. Debug-mode native builds expose compiler-owned live async-task metadata without adding release/profile overhead, and the Flux GDB support layer provides `flux-tasks` to list each live task by source async function, running/suspended/completed state, continuation state number, worker scope, and native task handle. A real GDB regression stops inside an async child while its parent is suspended and verifies both task states through the user-facing debugger metadata.
- [x] Ownership-aware value inspection. `flux-inspect name` classifies visible source locals using Flux ownership semantics instead of blindly chasing native storage: current scalar/aggregate/function/string handles are identified as `Copy`, compiler-known list/view descriptors are explicitly reported as non-`Copy` immutable borrows with length/stride/storage identity, and unknown native pointer shapes remain labelled as compiler/native handles rather than being misrepresented as owned Flux values. The GDB regression verifies both a `Copy` scalar and a live borrowed list view.
- [x] Native debug metadata. Generated C carries source-aware `#line` directives and debug/profile Clang modes retain DWARF line tables that resolve native instructions back to `.flux` files; this is now exercised through the user-facing debugger path.
- [x] Predictable bootstrap debug/profile/release native build behavior: `debug` uses no optimization plus full debug info/frame pointers, `profile` uses optimized code with debug info/frame pointers, and `release` uses aggressive optimization/LTO.
- [x] Platform debugger integration where useful. On the current Linux development target, `flux debug <target>` verifies GDB availability, builds with Flux debug metadata, opens the debugger with inherited terminal I/O, supports preconfigured source/function breakpoints plus optional immediate run, embeds Flux-aware local/watch commands, and cleans up its temporary binary/support script after the session. `flux doctor` reports GDB availability.

# Milestone 25 — Profiler and devtools

- [x] CPU profiling. Bootstrap `flux profile <target>` compiles with optimized profile settings plus isolated native `gprof` instrumentation, runs the exact instrumented binary with inherited terminal I/O, prints a CPU report on exit, keeps normal `--mode profile` builds free from instrumentation overhead, and cleans its temporary binary/data. `flux doctor` reports profiler availability; Flux-aware symbol/source presentation and lower-overhead production profiling remain separately tracked below.
- [x] Allocation profiling. Bootstrap `flux profile <target> --alloc` builds the ordinary optimized profile-mode native binary and runs it under glibc `memusage`, reporting heap total/peak, stack peak, allocation-call counts/bytes/failures, and block-size distribution without adding allocator/profiling runtime overhead to normal builds. Temporary binaries are removed afterward and `flux doctor` reports profiler availability; per-allocation stack attribution and leak graphs remain under the richer memory-tooling follow-up.
- [x] Bootstrap memory leak tooling through `flux profile <target> --leaks`: the CLI builds an isolated optimized native binary with Clang AddressSanitizer, enables leak detection only for that run, preserves Flux source locations through existing debug metadata, fails distinctly on sanitizer findings, and removes the temporary binary afterward. Full retained-object graph visualization remains naturally downstream of general owned heap values rather than blocking useful leak diagnostics now.
- [x] Task/async timeline.
- [x] Frame/render timeline.
- [x] Layout diagnostics. Bootstrap timeline profiling records dedicated begin/end spans around compiler-owned native UI refresh/layout work on Linux and Android, retaining the changed-state identifier beside the enclosing frame activity without exposing a widget-object hierarchy; tracing remains opt-in and ordinary builds retain the zero-cost no-op path.
- [x] Network inspection. Bootstrap timeline profiling records begin/end spans for canonical TCP connect/listen/accept, text send/receive, and HTTP request/response-body operations on the same monotonic trace as task/frame/layout work, exposing blocking latency without recording payload contents or adding instrumentation to ordinary builds.
- [x] Native symbolization. Bootstrap CPU profiles demangle compiler-generated `flux__fn_` application symbols back to Flux function names while leaving compiler/runtime helpers visibly native; profile-build debug symbols remain available to ordinary native tooling.
- [x] Source mapping. `flux profile` collects sampled native addresses from gprof output and resolves them through `addr2line` against the exact instrumented binary, replacing compiler-stdin locations with original `.flux` file/line locations wherever DWARF has a source mapping while retaining a safe native fallback for prologue/runtime instructions.
- [x] UI inspector without exposing a widget-object hierarchy. Bootstrap `flux profile <app> --inspect-ui` analyzes the typed project and reports the root Flux view's state/derived bindings plus flat source-level control declarations, native-control kinds, grid placement, source lines, and declared properties without launching or reflecting a GTK/Android object tree.
- [x] Performance overlays. Bootstrap `flux profile <app> --overlay` reuses opt-in timeline instrumentation on the current Linux development UI target to show the latest compiler-owned UI-refresh duration and changed-state identifier in-app, then reports aggregate frame/layout span counts, average duration, and maximum duration after exit; ordinary debug/profile/release builds compile the timing/overlay path out.
- [x] Low-overhead production profiling hooks: `flux profile <target> --sample` builds the ordinary optimized/profile native binary with debug information and frame pointers but no profiler instrumentation, samples it through Linux `perf record --call-graph dwarf`, prints a demangled `perf report --stdio`, cleans temporary artifacts, and leaves normal Flux binaries unchanged. `flux doctor` reports `perf` availability and the command fails clearly when the tool or kernel performance-counter permissions are unavailable.

# Milestone 26 — CLI/build/developer workflow

- [x] `flux new`: the user-facing `flux` binary shares the canonical CLI implementation and scaffolds a minimal manifest-backed native GUI package without overwriting non-empty directories.
- [x] `flux run`: the user-facing command provides the automatic development watch/build/restart loop and hot-reload status contract.
- [x] Bootstrap `fluxc build` compatibility entry remains available while the product CLI transitions to `flux`.
- [x] `flux build`: user-facing native builds support debug/profile/release modes and package/source targets.
- [x] `flux test`: user-facing native integration-test discovery/execution for direct `.flux` tests and package `tests/*.flux`, with debug/profile/release mode selection and real exit-status reporting.
  - [x] Opt-in `flux test --coverage` uses Clang/LLVM instrumentation without affecting ordinary test binaries, merges per-test profiles, and maps generated-C execution regions back onto original `.flux` source lines for a concise source-level coverage summary. `flux doctor` reports both required LLVM coverage tools, and an end-to-end test rejects regressions back to generated `<stdin>` reporting.
- [x] `flux format`: user-facing canonical formatting and `--check` mode use the existing formatter implementation.
- [x] `flux analyze`: user-facing static analysis is an explicit alias over the same project-aware compiler diagnostics as `flux check`, including `--json` for tooling/CI.
- [x] `flux devices`: the CLI reports Linux desktop display readiness, connected ADB Android devices with their real transport states, and a running local Waydroid container as a runnable Android target; external device probes are bounded so an unresponsive transport cannot stall the command forever.
- [x] `flux doctor`: doctor reports Linux host/Clang/linker/GTK/display readiness plus Android SDK, NDK, build-tools including D8, `javac` for compiler-generated Android runtime glue, ADB, optional Waydroid readiness, GDB/gprof, native crash symbolization/debug-symbol tools, and LLVM coverage tools; Neovim dogfood availability remains reported separately.
- [x] `flux clean`: target-aware cleanup removes the default native build artifact plus the runner's last-known development-status artifact and is safely idempotent when nothing exists.
- [x] Bootstrap `flux package`: manifest-backed packages build into a deterministic host-labelled bundle directory containing the package-named native executable plus the exact `flux.toml`, or a reproducible `.tar.gz` wrapping that same bundle via `--format tar.gz`; release is the default, build modes are supported, existing outputs are never overwritten, and raw source files are rejected rather than silently treated as distributable packages. Dependency vendoring, richer platform metadata, signing, and store packaging remain in the distribution/platform tracks.
- [x] Debug/profile/release modes through `flux build --mode ...` and `flux run --mode ...` (with `fluxc` compatibility); builds default to `release`, while the development runner defaults to `debug` for fast rebuilds and native debug information.
- [x] Cross-compilation configuration: native `flux build` and `flux package` accept explicit Clang `--target <triple>` plus optional `--sysroot <directory>`, target/sysroot selections participate in native cache identity, explicit Linux targets/sysroots retain safe per-function/module object reuse, default package names carry explicit target triples, host-execution commands reject cross-target options rather than pretending to run foreign binaries, and the configured path is regression-tested with the host Clang triple and host-root sysroot. Android remains a dedicated first-class target pipeline rather than being collapsed into generic Clang flags.
- [x] Bootstrap native build cache: generated C + build mode + compiler package version + host OS/architecture are content-addressed into a persistent native artifact cache (`$FLUX_CACHE_DIR`, XDG cache, or `~/.cache/flux`), transparently reused by build/run/test/package across output paths. Cache identity also includes the active Clang/linker identity and GTK version/compiler/linker flags when GTK is linked, while sidecar size/content hashes reject corrupt cached binaries before restore. Native executable and object tiers now prune old artifacts toward a 32-entry tier bound after successful publication, keep the active artifact protected, defer eviction of entries newer than five minutes so parallel builds cannot lose an in-use cache path, and remove sidecars with evicted artifacts. Explicit `flux cache path`, `flux cache prune`, and `flux cache clean` commands expose the resolved cache location, force the normal tier bound on demand, and remove the native cache without requiring users to know its internal layout. Semantic/codegen incremental reuse remains tracked under the compiler/hot-reload caches; full sysroot/SDK content fingerprints remain follow-up work.

## Package management and ecosystem

The first Flux package ecosystem must require **no Flux-operated hosting bill or always-on service**. The bootstrap registry uses ordinary public GitHub repositories plus GitHub Releases: package authors host immutable `.fluxpkg` source archives as release assets in their own repositories, while a small public Flux registry repository maps human-friendly package names and versions to those assets and their content hashes. This is an implementation backend rather than a language-level dependency on GitHub: manifests, lockfiles, package identities, and the registry abstraction must remain portable to another static host or mirror later without changing Flux source imports.

- [x] Extend `flux.toml` with a strict `[dependencies]` table supporting SemVer registry dependencies plus explicit local `path` and Git `rev` development dependencies; registry packages are referenced by package identity rather than raw download URLs. The bootstrap parser now exposes typed registry/path/Git dependency metadata, accepts exact/caret/tilde SemVer requirements plus `*`, requires relative local paths and revision-pinned Git sources, rejects duplicate/invalid names and mixed/unknown source shapes, and keeps actual resolution/fetching/lockfiles/package-import semantics in the following roadmap items.
- [x] Add an unambiguous package import namespace, separate from existing relative `.flux` imports, so dependency modules have stable package-qualified identities and cannot be confused with local files. `import "pkg:<dependency>/<module.flux>"` is reserved for manifest-declared dependencies; local `path` dependencies load through their own validated `flux.toml`, remain contained within that dependency root, preserve dependency-local relative imports, and receive module identities from the dependency package's own name. Registry imports consume compiler-resolved, checksum-verified source trees from the global package cache after online resolution through the public registry by default or an explicit provider override; exact locked registry imports replay provider-independently. Revision-pinned Git imports consume only the source tree materialized from their immutable `flux.lock` commit/SHA-256 identity; missing/stale lock entries fail instead of falling back to mutable repository state or local files.
- [x] Implement deterministic dependency resolution with one resolved version per package name in a build graph initially, SemVer constraint solving, cycle detection, and concise conflict diagnostics that show the dependency paths imposing incompatible requirements. Bootstrap lock resolution now maintains one deterministic resolution identity per package name across the reachable graph: local path diamonds must converge on the same canonical source root/version, same-name Git dependencies must converge on one URL/revision, cross-source collisions fail, and registry requirements reject provably disjoint exact/caret/tilde/wildcard constraint ranges before metadata fetch. Conflict diagnostics name both dependency paths and resolved identities, path cycles report the full dependency path that closes the cycle, and the reusable candidate-set solver now selects the highest release satisfying every supplied requirement. Registry fetch/vendor graph resolution feeds fetched static metadata into that same highest-compatible-version solver, expands transitive registry requirements to a fixed point, enforces each release's Flux compatibility requirement, and rejects registry dependency cycles. Registry requirements discovered inside revision-pinned Git packages are collected from their immutable materialized manifests and feed the same global candidate solver, including online outdated/fetch resolution. Ordinary online package analysis resolves that graph through the public static registry by default or `FLUX_REGISTRY_DIR` / `FLUX_REGISTRY_URL` overrides, materializes exact release versions/repositories/assets/SHA-256 identities through the shared content-addressed cache, and makes `pkg:` imports consume those exact source trees; check/build/run/test share that analysis path. Exact registry locks replay provider-independently: validation checks the committed immutable version/repository/asset/SHA-256 identities against current manifest requirements without consulting a live provider, and ordinary analysis materializes exact locked registry releases even when they are reachable only through a Git dependency. Revision-pinned Git dependencies resolve to exact commits, canonical package hashes, recurse through Git/path child manifests, and participate in the existing package-identity conflict/cycle checks.
- [x] Add generated `flux.lock` files recording exact package versions, immutable source/release identity, SHA-256 content hashes, and resolver/lockfile format version; lockfiles are committed for applications and libraries, never contain host-specific absolute paths/timestamps, and support `--locked` CI builds that fail rather than re-resolve. Existing deterministic format-v1 locks stamp an explicit `resolver = 1` identity in addition to the lockfile format version. Native `flux check` / `analyze` / `build` / `run` / `test` / `package` refresh a missing or stale deterministic lock before analysis by default, while `--locked` makes those commands fail without modifying a missing/stale lock for CI. Public-default or explicitly overridden registry resolution records the selected exact version plus immutable repository/release identity, asset URL, and SHA-256 in the lock and ordinary analysis automatically materializes that verified release. Exact registry entries are parsed and structurally validated as the provider-independent source of truth: provider changes do not silently re-resolve a valid lock, stale manifest requirements fail against the pinned version under `--locked`, and strict offline replay consumes the immutable repository/asset/SHA-256 identity without contacting the public index. Revision-pinned Git entries record the requested URL/revision together with the resolved 40-hex commit, package version when declared, and SHA-256 of the canonical `.fluxpkg`; validation rejects mutable/stale identities, and `--locked` replay never consults mutable repository state.
- [x] Add first-class dependency commands under the existing `flux` CLI: `flux add`, `flux remove`, `flux fetch`, `flux update`, `flux outdated`, `flux tree`, and `flux why`; `flux add` writes validated registry, relative-path, or revision-pinned Git dependency entries and refreshes the deterministic lockfile, rolling the manifest back if lock generation fails, while `flux remove` deletes declared dependencies and refreshes or removes `flux.lock` as appropriate. Git adds fail/roll back if the requested revision cannot be resolved to an immutable commit instead of leaving an unhashed mutable lock entry. Read-only `flux tree <package>` renders the deterministic validated dependency graph with versions/sources and `flux why <package> <dependency>` reports every matching dependency path, including transitive path dependencies and unresolved registry/Git leaves. `flux fetch` first creates/validates the current lock when online, then fills the verified global cache for registry and exact Git dependencies; `--offline` requires an already-valid lock, touches only verified cached package bytes, and fails on a cache miss. `flux vendor` provides the explicit self-contained snapshot escape hatch. Ordinary online `flux check`/`build`/`run`/`test` analysis resolves/materializes public-default or explicitly overridden registry dependencies and immutable locked Git dependencies into the same verified cache before `pkg:` imports. `flux fetch --offline` replays exact registry entries from the validated `flux.lock` without contacting the default registry. `flux update` deliberately re-resolves declared registry/Git inputs into a new immutable lock and fills the verified cache, while `flux outdated` leaves the lock unchanged and reports newer compatible registry versions or changed commits behind the declared Git revision. CLI regression coverage verifies `update`, `outdated`, and `vendor` are ordinary first-class commands rather than hidden library-only paths.
- [x] Add a global content-addressed package cache alongside the existing Flux build cache so identical dependency contents are stored once across projects, hash-verified before use, and usable through a strict `--offline` path; add `flux vendor` only as an explicit self-contained/offline snapshot escape hatch rather than creating per-project `node_modules`-style copies by default. The bootstrap package-ecosystem layer stores already-obtained `.fluxpkg` archives under a SHA-256 content address, verifies the expected digest both before insertion and every time a cached archive is reopened, reuses identical bytes across callers, and fails closed on cache tampering. Registry release materialization is cache-first and its strict offline primitive refuses a cache miss without touching the network; verified cached archives can be safely extracted into a source tree for later vendor/import wiring. Registry graph wiring is exposed through `flux fetch` and `flux vendor`, with strict `--offline` reading only cached registry metadata and verified content-addressed archives; vendoring materializes deterministic `packages/<name>/<version>` trees plus `flux.vendor.lock` rather than becoming the normal dependency layout. Ordinary package analysis materializes exact configured registry releases under checksum-addressed source roots and imports directly from those verified roots without a project-local dependency tree. Provider-independent registry replay from `flux.lock` rematerializes exact SHA-256-addressed releases without registry metadata. Revision-pinned Git fetch/materialization exports an exact commit through the same canonical `.fluxpkg` producer, admits it only under its verified SHA-256 cache key, materializes checksum-addressed source roots, and supports strict offline replay after the original repository disappears. `flux vendor` snapshots both exact locked registry releases and exact locked Git commits from those verified archives, records their immutable source/hash identities in `flux.vendor.lock`, and succeeds in strict offline mode even after the original Git repository has disappeared.
- [x] Define canonical reproducible `.fluxpkg` source archives and use public GitHub Release assets as the zero-hosting-cost bootstrap blob store; downloads are addressed and verified by immutable version/release metadata plus SHA-256 rather than trusting mutable branch archives. Format-v1 archive primitives now produce byte-identical gzip-compressed POSIX tar source archives with sorted entries, epoch mtimes and normalized ownership, exclude root build/VCS outputs, reject symlinks rather than archiving host-dependent targets, and return a compiler-owned SHA-256 digest implemented without an external hashing dependency. Immutable release metadata drives cache-first HTTPS asset fetching; downloaded bytes are admitted only after SHA-256 verification, and extraction rejects traversal/link entries, requires a root manifest, and installs through a temporary directory. End-to-end registry resolver/CLI fetch/vendor plus configured normal package analysis consume these verified archives. Revision-pinned Git dependencies archive the resolved commit through the same canonical `.fluxpkg` path before lock/cache admission, so mutable Git refs cannot bypass the reproducibility/hash contract. `flux publish package` now produces that canonical archive, uploads `{name}-{version}.fluxpkg` to the package repository's `v{version}` GitHub Release, and accepts an existing release on retry only when the downloaded asset has the identical SHA-256.
- [x] Create a small public GitHub-backed Flux registry index containing package name, owner/source repository, available versions, Flux compatibility constraints, release-asset location, SHA-256, dependency metadata, and yank state; resolution is local/cacheable and must not require an always-on Flux database/API server. Compiler-owned registry metadata format v1 strictly models exactly those release fields plus dependency requirements, selects the highest compatible non-yanked release through the existing SemVer solver, and supports deterministic directory and static HTTPS providers. The public `brandonp2412/flux-registry` repository now hosts the canonical static index and validates pull requests with Flux's own index checker. Ordinary registry resolution defaults to its raw GitHub content while `FLUX_REGISTRY_URL` and `FLUX_REGISTRY_DIR` remain explicit mirror/private/test overrides; package publishing likewise defaults registry pull requests to that repository while retaining `--registry` and `FLUX_REGISTRY_GITHUB_REPO` overrides. Static metadata remains cacheable for strict offline use, and exact registry locks replay independently of whichever provider produced them.
- [x] Implement `flux publish` for the bootstrap registry using the publisher's GitHub authentication: `flux publish package` validates the lock and manifest, checks and tests the package, creates the canonical `.fluxpkg`, uploads it to the package repository's versioned GitHub Release through authenticated `gh`, clones or forks the configured GitHub registry repository, writes deterministic release/version/ownership metadata, and submits the corresponding pull request without requiring package authors or Flux maintainers to operate separate storage infrastructure.
- [x] Make published package versions immutable; release retries verify that an already-published GitHub asset has the exact expected SHA-256, and registry writes accept an existing version only when its metadata is byte-identical. A broken version remains yanked through registry metadata so new resolution excludes it without deleting/replacing bytes needed by existing lockfiles. Package-name ownership lives in deterministic `<package>/owner.txt` registry metadata; publishing under a different owner is rejected until an explicit registry ownership transfer changes that metadata.
- [x] Package fetch/add/publish validation never executes dependency-controlled lifecycle hooks, install scripts, build scripts, or arbitrary shell commands. Registry metadata remains strict enough that hook-like unknown fields are rejected rather than ignored; publishing permits only registry SemVer dependencies and rejects native plugin/library/search-path declarations, while fetch/materialization only performs compiler-owned transport/archive operations. Bootstrap registry packages therefore contain Flux source plus declared resources only; arbitrary native libraries/JVM glue/platform bridges remain behind a future explicit audited native-package ABI rather than being smuggled through installation.
- [x] Add workspace support with one root dependency resolution/`flux.lock`, local workspace-member package identities, and workspace-wide check/test/build commands, while preventing undeclared local paths from silently shadowing registry packages. Workspace roots validate explicit relative members with unique package identities; member dependency graphs are emitted under member-qualified IDs in the single root lock, member targets replay that lock, and root `check`/`analyze`/`build`/`test` aggregate the root plus all members. Members remain non-importable unless declared as dependencies, so registry names cannot be shadowed implicitly.
- [x] Keep the registry transport/provider abstract behind Flux-owned metadata and lockfile semantics: resolution consumes the `RegistryProvider` contract rather than GitHub-specific APIs, directory/check-out and static HTTPS/file-mirror providers produce identical resolved release identities from the same metadata, and exact `flux.lock` entries remain provider-independent. Regression coverage also proves transitive fetch/vendor works through the provider contract and strict offline materialization uses only SHA-256-verified content-addressed cache entries, so a future static mirror or non-GitHub registry backend does not require changing `flux.toml`, Flux source imports, or existing lockfile package identities.

- [x] Reproducible builds: same-host byte reproducibility is covered for debug/profile/release native artifacts and deterministic package archives, while cross-machine verification uses explicit build metadata and `flux verify-reproducibility` to compare all normalized inputs and artifact hashes.
  - [x] Same-host/same-toolchain native reproducibility for current debug/profile/release modes: generated C is streamed to Clang over stdin so PID-specific temporary source paths cannot leak into DWARF, and cache-isolated builds of identical Flux source are regression-tested as byte-identical in every mode.
  - [x] Cross-machine/toolchain reproducibility contract with pinned compiler/SDK/runtime identities, normalized environment inputs, dependency lockfiles, and documented verification metadata. `flux build --reproducibility <path>` emits generated-source, manifest/root-lock, target/sysroot, explicit compiler/SDK/runtime, and normalized environment fingerprints; `flux verify-reproducibility <metadata-a> <metadata-b>` rejects any metadata or artifact-hash mismatch for CI verification.
- [x] CI-friendly machine output through `fluxc check --json`, preserving a clean stdout JSON contract with no human diagnostic text on stderr; `flux check --json` / `flux analyze --json` now emit the same explicitly versioned diagnostics envelope.

# Milestone 27 — Distribution

- [x] Android signing/store publishing guidance and automation hooks: `docs/android-release.md` documents upload-key configuration, Play App Signing boundaries, current target-API policy, secret handling, deterministic `-o` artifacts, and CI usage; `flux publish android ... --json` emits a clean machine-readable success object only after release AAB construction plus bundle/signature verification, while failures retain non-zero process status. Verification deliberately avoids `jarsigner -strict`, whose CA-chain policy rejects the self-signed upload keys used by ordinary Android signing.
- [ ] iOS signing/App Store workflow.
- [x] Windows packaging/store workflow: `flux package --format msix` emits deterministic MSIX bundles with compiler-generated AppX metadata and block maps, optional PFX signing verifies through Windows `signtool`, and `flux publish windows ... --json` is the CI-friendly release entry point that produces a signed, verified release artifact. Windows CI also requires the SDK `MakeAppx` unpacker to accept generated packages. Publisher-account/certificate provisioning, Partner Center identity/listing/certification, rollout, and Store-side release remain intentionally external release-owner operations rather than compiler responsibilities.
- [x] Verified MSIX packaging follow-up: generated Windows packages now include deterministic archive ordering, AppX content-type metadata, and a SHA-256 64 KiB block map, with regression coverage for metadata shape and hash encoding. Windows package payloads now use the expected .exe name, core content types declare the staged manifest/block map/package manifest, and windows-latest CI builds an MSIX then requires the Windows SDK MakeAppx unpacker to accept it. Certificate provisioning, Microsoft Store identity/listing validation, and Store submission remain external release-owner steps.
- [ ] macOS signing/notarization/store workflow.
- [x] Linux packaging guidance for the current GTK4 backend is documented in `docs/linux-packaging.md`, covering release bundles, runtime dependencies, conventional filesystem/desktop integration, icons, archives/distro packages, reproducibility expectations, and the boundary between current compiler automation and downstream packaging.
  - [x] Bootstrap host bundle from `flux package` provides the first runnable Linux distribution shape without claiming dependency-free/self-contained GTK packaging.
- [x] Web production deployment for the current direct-DOM backend: `flux package web <file.flux|package-dir|flux.toml> [-o directory]` emits an immutable static deployment root containing the same self-contained production `index.html` as `flux build web`, with no development reload endpoint/runtime. Manifest-backed packages default to a versioned `dist/<name>-<version>-web` artifact, explicit outputs refuse overwrite, and the resulting directory can be uploaded as-is to ordinary static hosting/CDN infrastructure without a Flux-operated deployment service.
- [x] Debug symbol separation/upload for current native Linux artifacts: `flux symbols split <binary> [-o directory]` leaves the input untouched and emits a stripped executable plus detached GNU `.debug` file with a debug link, providing an upload/retention-ready artifact for external crash services. The workflow is covered by a real Flux debug-binary E2E rather than only command construction.
