- [x] Crash symbolization for current native Linux artifacts: `flux symbolize <binary-or-debug-file> <address>...` resolves native instruction addresses through preserved debug metadata back to demangled Flux function names and original `.flux` source locations, and is E2E-verified against both a live debug binary and its detached symbol file.
- [x] Optional Linux native symbol obfuscation compatible with diagnostics/debugging: `flux symbols obfuscate <native-binary> [-o binary]` writes a separate ELF with nonessential symbol/debug data stripped through the native toolchain while leaving the original diagnostic-capable binary untouched. The obfuscated artifact remains runnable and removes private Flux function names; detached/source-level diagnostics remain available by retaining the unobfuscated or separately split symbol artifact, and the workflow is explicitly a symbol-hiding distribution option rather than a cryptographic secrecy boundary.
- [x] Version/build-number management for current distribution targets: `[package].version` is validated package metadata, is preserved in Linux bundles, becomes Android `versionName`, and `[android].version_code` is validated against Play's accepted integer range and becomes Android `versionCode`; future platform-specific mappings belong to their target packaging milestones.

# Milestone 28 — Server/headless application target

Flux must remain equally credible for headless native binaries.

- [x] HTTP server: canonical `http.serve(listener, maxHeadBytes, maxBodyBytes, requestCallback, headerCallback, bodyCallback)` is the ordinary synchronous blocking server: it validates a real listening TCP socket and continuously accepts connections EINTR-safely, reuses the bounded HTTP/1.0/1.1 request/body parser (including fixed-length and chunked framing), exposes request/header/body data only for callback scope so application routing and `http.respond*` responses remain ordinary typed Flux code, and closes each accepted socket before accepting the next connection. The deliberately qualified `http.serveOnce(...)` remains available for one-connection control. Both tree-shake when unreachable and participate in LSP completion/signature help; TLS and structured-concurrent request handling remain separate roadmap items rather than hidden inside this checkbox.
  - [x] Bootstrap bounded HTTP/1.0/1.1 request-head parsing through compiler-owned `http.readRequest(socket, maxBytes, callback)`: the API requires a TCP socket, validates a `1..=65536` byte cap statically where possible and again at runtime, reads exactly through `\r\n\r\n` without consuming request-body bytes, rejects NUL/malformed request lines and unsupported HTTP versions, and lends method/target/version only for the exact `fn(i64, str, str, str) -> void` callback scope before returning `(receivedBytes, error)`. The helper is allocation-free, EINTR-safe, tree-shakes when unreachable, participates in LSP completion/signature help, and native loopback coverage validates real request parsing. Persistent connections, routing, TLS, and task-integrated request handling remain pending.
  - [x] Bootstrap HTTP/1.1 text responses through canonical `http.respond(socket, status, contentType, body, keepAlive = false)`: the older `sendTextResponse` spelling remains source-compatible but is omitted from default completion. Status and argument types are checked statically, dynamic status values retain a runtime `100..=599` guard, response framing emits `Content-Length`, `Content-Type`, and `Connection: close` by default, header injection through CR/LF content types is rejected, stream writes are EINTR/partial-write safe with `MSG_NOSIGNAL`, the helper tree-shakes when unreachable, LSP completion/signature help expose the typed call, and native loopback coverage validates exact response bytes. Streaming/binary bodies, routing, TLS, and task-integrated request handling remain pending.
  - [x] Bootstrap persistent HTTP response framing: the optional `keepAlive: bool` argument switches canonical `http.respond` to `Connection: keep-alive` without closing or otherwise taking ownership of the TCP socket, while the existing bounded request parsers already stop exactly at one request boundary. The default remains connection-close framing for compatibility, invalid keep-alive argument types fail statically, LSP help exposes the optional parameter, and native loopback coverage verifies the emitted keep-alive response bytes. Application/server code remains responsible for honoring peer connection policy and looping for the next request until structured request handling lands.
  - [x] Borrowed HTTP request header-field parsing through compiler-owned `http.readRequestHeaders(socket, maxBytes, requestCallback, headerCallback)`: the bounded request head is validated before callbacks run, field names accept the HTTP token grammar, obsolete folded headers and control characters are rejected, surrounding optional whitespace is trimmed from values, and each validated name/value is lent only to the exact `fn(i64, str, str) -> void` header callback scope alongside the existing request-line callback. The helper remains allocation-free, tree-shakes when unreachable, participates in LSP completion/signature help, and native loopback coverage verifies multiple real header fields without consuming request-body bytes.
  - [x] Bounded borrowed HTTP text request bodies through compiler-owned `http.readRequestBody(socket, maxHeadBytes, maxBodyBytes, requestCallback, headerCallback, bodyCallback)`: request-line/header validation is shared with the bootstrap HTTP contract; `Content-Length` is parsed case-insensitively and bounded to `0..=65536`, while HTTP/1.1 `Transfer-Encoding: chunked` bodies are decoded into the same bounded callback-scoped text view with chunk extensions accepted, exact wire-boundary tracking, ambiguous Content-Length/Transfer-Encoding combinations rejected, and trailer fields grammar-validated/consumed within `maxHeadBytes` while `Content-Length`/`Transfer-Encoding` trailers are rejected as framing ambiguity. NUL-containing bodies fail instead of truncating the bootstrap `str` view, and all request/header/body values remain callback-scoped borrows. The helper is allocation-free, EINTR-safe, tree-shakes when unreachable, participates in LSP completion/signature help, and native loopback coverage proves fixed-length plus trailer-bearing chunked framing leave following bytes unread. Binary bodies and surfaced trailer values remain pending with byte buffers/owned values and richer HTTP framing.
  - [x] Validated custom HTTP response fields through `http.respondHeaders(socket, status, contentType, body, headers, keepAlive = false)`: the borrowed `headers` text is a CRLF-separated field block with token/control-character validation, framing-owned `Content-Length`/`Content-Type`/`Connection`/`Transfer-Encoding` overrides are rejected, the complete response head is bounded to 8192 bytes, sends remain EINTR/partial-write safe with `MSG_NOSIGNAL`, keep-alive framing remains explicit, unreachable support tree-shakes, LSP completion/signature help expose the API, and native loopback coverage verifies exact custom response fields. This remains a bootstrap text escape hatch until owned header maps and richer server response values exist.
- [x] TLS server: accepted native TCP sockets can become compiler-owned OpenSSL server sessions through `tls.listen`, with certificate/key validation, handshake failure reporting, bounded text I/O, deterministic close, LSP/toolchain support, tree-shaking, and native OpenSSL-client E2E coverage.
- [x] WebSocket server: compiler-owned native Linux/server WebSocket sessions support RFC 6455 upgrade validation, masked client text/binary receive, fragmented text messages, bounded control-frame ping/pong handling, close validation/acknowledgement, and unmasked server text/binary/control responses without source-authored bridges or escaping payload storage; native loopback coverage exercises the real handshake and fragmented/ping exchange. Binary buffers, ownership-typed socket resources, and task/event-loop integration remain tracked by their dedicated container/ownership/concurrency items.

  Verified TCP latency-control follow-up: `net.noDelay(socket, enabled)` toggles native `TCP_NODELAY` for TCP sockets, validates handle/bool types statically, rejects non-TCP sockets at runtime, tree-shakes completely when unreachable, and participates in LSP completion/signature help with native loopback coverage.
  Verified TCP connection-liveness follow-up: `net.keepAlive(socket, enabled)` toggles native `SO_KEEPALIVE` on connected TCP sockets, validates handle/bool types statically, rejects non-stream and listening sockets at runtime, tree-shakes completely when unreachable, participates in LSP completion/signature help, and has native loopback coverage toggling the option on a real connection.

NUL-containing payloads are rejected instead of being silently truncated by the bootstrap C-string representation, unreachable networking support tree-shakes completely, LSP completion/signature help expose the typed surface, and native E2E coverage exchanges real loopback TCP text and validates multi-socket readable/writable readiness. Owned/binary byte buffers, task/event-loop integration, and ownership-typed socket resources remain pending with byte buffers/owned values/concurrency.
  - [x] Bootstrap Linux/server UDP socket lifecycle through compiler-owned `net.udp(host, port)` and `net.bind(host, port)`, sharing `net.port(socket)`, `net.write(socket, text)`, `net.read(socket, maxBytes, callback)`, `net.nonblocking(socket, enabled)`, single-socket `net.readable` / `net.writable`, borrowed-list `net.readableMany` / `net.writableMany`, and `net.close(socket)`: connect/bind return explicit `(i64, error)` results, known-invalid ports fail statically with runtime guards retained for dynamic values, IPv4/IPv6 resolution lowers directly through native `getaddrinfo`/`SOCK_DGRAM`, ephemeral bind ports are queryable, connected datagram sends remain one datagram rather than stream-looping, synchronous receives expose only bounded borrowed text to the callback, descriptor/readiness helpers share the native socket implementation, unreachable UDP support tree-shakes completely, LSP completion/signature help expose the typed surface, and native E2E coverage exercises bind/connect plus real connected and unconnected peer-addressed UDP text round trips. Binary byte I/O, task/event-loop integration, and ownership-typed socket resources remain pending with byte buffers/owned values/concurrency.

- [x] Linux/server signals/process lifecycle: compiler-owned `process.stopping()` lazily installs SIGINT/SIGTERM handlers whose signal-context work is limited to setting a `sig_atomic_t` flag, enabling ordinary Flux control flow to perform graceful shutdown work outside the handler; `process.exit(code)` provides explicit `0..=255` process termination with compile-time checking for known constants and a runtime guard for dynamic values. Both capabilities lower directly to POSIX/C, are tree-shaken when unreachable, participate in LSP completion/signature help, and remain rejected on Android until portable mobile process semantics are defined.
- [x] Bootstrap Linux service/daemon integration: `flux package --format systemd` emits a headless native service bundle containing the optimized Flux executable plus a generated systemd unit bound to that bundle, with restart-on-failure policy, explicit SIGTERM shutdown, bounded stop timeout, and user-service `default.target` enablement. GUI `app` packages are rejected, service names are constrained to safe unit-name characters, and compiler regression coverage verifies the emitted unit and executable.
- [x] Structured concurrency for request handling. Qualified `http.serveConcurrent(listener, maxHeadBytes, maxBodyBytes, requestCallback, headerCallback, bodyCallback)` keeps the ordinary `http.serve` synchronous path intact while dispatching each accepted connection into a compiler-owned structured child worker. Each request worker owns and deterministically closes its accepted socket, reuses the bounded cancellation-aware HTTP request parser, inherits parent cancellation, and preserves its first parser/descendant-scope failure through worker teardown. The server tracks at most 64 request handles before a structured drain barrier so completed worker state cannot grow without bound; draining preserves the first child failure while consuming the batch. Cancelling a hosting worker recursively cancels and drains its in-flight request workers before `serveConcurrent` returns. Native regression coverage sends two outstanding requests whose sleeping callbacks overlap, verifies both responses, then cancels/joins the hosting worker promptly. Existing socket-readiness and timed-backpressure paths retain their bounded cancellation checks.
- [ ] Efficient I/O. Bootstrap socket text I/O is synchronous, allocation-free, EINTR-safe, tree-shakeable, and bounded to borrowed callback scope; connected stream/datagram text plus peer-addressed UDP `writeTo` / `readFrom` are available without creating escaping payload/address objects. Compiler-owned `net.nonblocking` controls descriptor mode, `net.readable` / `net.writable` provide single-socket readiness, `net.readableMany` / `net.writableMany` provide direction-specific multi-socket readiness, and `net.readyMany` reports readable/writable state together from one native `poll` over a borrowed `i64[]` handle set without allocating an escaping result collection. Nonblocking TCP listeners can drain up to a caller-bounded number of queued connections per readiness wakeup through `net.acceptMany(listener, maxCount, callback)`; accepted descriptors are made nonblocking before the callback runs, `EAGAIN`/`EWOULDBLOCK` cleanly ends the drain without an error, worker cancellation stops a long drain between accepted connections, and the helper tree-shakes when unreachable. Borrowed TCP scatter/gather output through `net.writeParts(socket, parts: str[])` now batches string fragments through native `sendmsg`/`iovec`, handles EINTR and partial writes with `MSG_NOSIGNAL`, preserves borrowed list/string ownership, tree-shakes when unreachable, and participates in LSP completion/signature help. Peer-addressed UDP scatter/gather output through `net.writePartsTo(socket, host, port, parts: str[])` sends up to 64 non-empty borrowed fragments as one datagram through native `sendmsg`/`iovec`, preserving datagram boundaries without an owned concatenation buffer, validating peer ports statically where possible and at runtime, tree-shaking when unreachable, and participating in LSP completion/signature help. Nonblocking connected TCP streams can drain up to a caller-bounded number of immediately available borrowed text chunks through `net.readMany(socket, maxBytes, maxCount, callback)`; each callback receives at most `maxBytes`, `EAGAIN`/`EWOULDBLOCK` ends the drain without error, EOF terminates the drain, worker cancellation stops a long drain between chunks while preserving the completed byte count, total drained bytes are returned without an escaping collection, and the helper tree-shakes when unreachable while participating in LSP completion/signature help. Nonblocking UDP sockets can likewise drain a caller-bounded batch of immediately queued datagrams through `net.readManyFrom(socket, maxBytes, maxCount, callback)` while preserving datagram boundaries and lending each payload plus numeric peer host/port only for callback scope; `EAGAIN`/`EWOULDBLOCK` ends the drain cleanly, worker cancellation stops between datagrams while preserving the completed byte count, total payload bytes are returned without an escaping collection, and unreachable support tree-shakes while remaining available through LSP completion/signature help. Bounded whole-message TCP backpressure is available through `net.writeTimeout(socket, text, timeoutMillis)`: it requires a connected nonblocking TCP socket, retries interrupted writes, waits for `POLLOUT` only after `EAGAIN`/`EWOULDBLOCK`, enforces one monotonic total deadline (`-1` for no deadline), uses the structured worker cancellation-aware poll path so scope exit interrupts otherwise infinite backpressure waits, returns the exact bytes sent alongside an explicit error on timeout/failure/cancellation, tree-shakes when unreachable, and participates in LSP completion/signature help. Resumable nonblocking TCP writes use `net.writeFrom(socket, text, offset)`: one call attempts forward progress from the caller-owned byte cursor, returns `(nextOffset, complete, error)`, treats `EAGAIN`/`EWOULDBLOCK` as a clean zero-progress retry point, validates connected/nonblocking TCP state and offset bounds, uses `MSG_NOSIGNAL`, tree-shakes when unreachable, and participates in LSP completion/signature help without allocating or retaining the borrowed text. Resumable scatter/gather TCP writes also use `net.writePartsFrom(socket, parts, offset)` with the same caller-owned global byte cursor model as `net.writeFrom`, one nonblocking `sendmsg` progress attempt, clean `EAGAIN`/`EWOULDBLOCK` retry points, no payload retention, and compile-time rejection of statically out-of-range literal cursors while preserving native checks for dynamic cursors. Resumable timed writes through `net.writeFromTimeout` and `net.writePartsFromTimeout` apply a single monotonic deadline to the unsent suffix beginning at the caller-owned global byte offset, return `(nextOffset, complete, error)` with exact progress preserved on timeout/cancellation/failure, avoid retaining borrowed payloads, and use cancellation-aware polling only when nonblocking sends encounter backpressure. Static literal cursor/timeout checks, dynamic runtime guards, native suffix-delivery coverage, tree-shaking, and LSP completion/signature help verify the contract. Binary buffers, queued backpressure policy, and task-integrated event-loop I/O remain required before this item is complete.
  - [x] Bootstrap combined multi-socket readiness through `net.readyMany(sockets, timeoutMillis, callback)`: one allocation-free native `poll` watches both read and write readiness for every borrowed `i64[]` handle, invokes an exact `fn(i64, bool, bool) -> void` callback at most once per ready descriptor, treats hangup as readable/end-of-stream rather than writable readiness, validates bounded/infinite timeout semantics consistently with the existing readiness APIs, participates in LSP completion/signature help, and tree-shakes completely when unreachable. This supplies a single-pass primitive for a future task-integrated event loop without claiming the structured-concurrency/runtime work is complete.
- [x] Database connectivity for the current headless/server target through the compiler-owned SQLite interface: native headless builds can open/close databases, execute SQL, and stream query cells through bounded callback-scoped borrowed strings with explicit errors, direct SQLite linkage, LSP support, and tree-shaking when unreachable. Broader database drivers/plugin transport remain tracked separately under Milestone 21.
- [x] Static/self-contained deployment where platform permits: `flux package --format static` emits one statically linked optimized executable for headless packages, keeps static/dynamic native cache identities separate, reuses safe host-Linux per-function/module native objects across body-only edits before the final static link, rejects GUI applications rather than pretending GTK is self-contained, supports the existing explicit Clang target/sysroot path, and fails clearly when the selected toolchain lacks a static C runtime. Compiler E2E coverage executes the artifact and verifies the Linux ELF has no dynamic interpreter when `readelf` is available.
- [x] Container-friendly builds: `flux package --format container` emits a headless-only native container build context containing the optimized Flux executable plus a minimal `Containerfile`, without requiring Docker/Podman during compilation or conflating this with the separate static/self-contained deployment goal. Compiler regression coverage verifies the generated context and executable, while GUI `app` packages are rejected from this server-oriented format.
- [x] Bootstrap server observability hooks: compiler-owned `process.cpu()` reports current-process user+system CPU time and `process.memory()` reports Linux peak RSS in bytes through direct `getrusage` lowering. Both are allocation-free `i64` scalar reads, participate in LSP completion/signature help, reject Android lowering with the existing desktop/server process capability boundary, and tree-shake completely when unreachable; richer request-level metrics/tracing can build on ordinary Flux code as the server I/O/concurrency model lands.

Verified Milestone 28 timed-batch follow-up: `net.acceptManyTimeout` waits cancellation-aware for initial listener readability before draining the existing caller-bounded nonblocking TCP accept batch, returning exact accepted-count plus readiness/error without retaining accepted sockets; `net.readManyTimeout` / `net.readManyFromTimeout` do the same before the existing nonblocking TCP/UDP receive drains, returning exact completed bytes plus readiness/error without retaining payloads and preserving partial progress if drain cancellation/failure occurs. Binary buffers, queued backpressure, and task-integrated event-loop I/O remain required before Efficient I/O is complete.

Verified Efficient I/O descriptor-boundary follow-up: all compiler-owned multi-socket readiness helpers now reject a non-empty borrowed socket-list descriptor with missing native storage before deriving a stride or reading a handle. The empty-list fast path remains allocation-free and valid, while generated-C regression coverage pins the guard for both direction-specific and combined readiness; owned buffers, queued backpressure, and task-integrated event-loop I/O remain open.

Verified readiness-handle alignment follow-up: `net.waitAny`, the direction-specific multi-socket waits, and `worker.waitAny` now copy each borrowed handle with `memcpy` before native validation, so valid strided handle lists remain defined even when an element address is not naturally aligned; timeout, cancellation, descriptor-bound, scope, and tree-shaking behavior are unchanged and compiler regression coverage pins the generated boundary. Owned buffers, queued backpressure, and task-integrated event-loop I/O remain open.

Verified binary-buffer stride-boundary follow-up: direct and resumable borrowed TCP byte writes reject negative or undersized `i64[]` element strides before native byte projection, complementing their missing-storage and byte-range checks without allocating or retaining payloads. Generated-C coverage pins both guards; owned byte buffers, queued backpressure, and task-integrated event-loop I/O remain open.

Verified Milestone 28 caller-bounded request-admission follow-up: `http.serveConcurrentLimit` caps directly owned structured HTTP request workers at a validated `1..=64`, waits for compiler-owned completion signalling at capacity, and reaps completed workers before admitting another connection without exposing an application-side queue. This advances Efficient I/O while binary buffers, general queued socket backpressure, and task-integrated event-loop I/O remain incomplete.

Verified Milestone 28 timed binary UDP receive follow-up: `net.readBytesFromTimeout(socket, maxBytes, timeoutMillis, callback)` now provides the single-datagram binary counterpart to the existing timed text receive and binary batch APIs. It reuses the bounded cancellation-aware one-item UDP drain, preserves callback-scoped `i64[]` payload and peer-address views, validates socket/batch/timeout bounds, rejects oversized datagrams before materialization, supports the legacy `receiveBytesFromWithTimeout` spelling, and is covered by compiler lowering and LSP contract tests. Owned byte buffers, queued backpressure, and task-integrated event-loop I/O remain unchecked.

Verified Milestone 28 concurrent-server error-drain follow-up: concurrent HTTP server failure paths now cancel and join the request workers owned by that server before control returns to Flux code, and `serveConcurrentLimit` emits its parser/readiness runtime dependencies even when it is the only concurrent-server entry point used. Native malformed-request coverage proves a handled server error does not strand an unrelated slow in-flight request behind a later structured join. Efficient I/O remains unchecked because binary buffers, general queued backpressure, and task-integrated event-loop I/O are still required.

Verified Milestone 28 queued-admission follow-up: concurrent HTTP serving now drains immediately queued listener connections into available bounded request-worker slots after one readiness wake, using cancellation-aware zero-time readiness checks between accepts instead of forcing each queued connection through another outer poll cycle. The configured in-flight cap remains authoritative and every error path still drains server-owned workers before returning. Efficient I/O remains unchecked because binary buffers, general queued socket backpressure, and task-integrated event-loop I/O are still required.

Verified Milestone 28 bounded request-state follow-up: concurrent HTTP serving no longer performs dedicated heap allocation for each request payload or request worker-registry node. Fixed compiler-owned 64-slot stack pools are indexed alongside the active handles, completed workers are joined before their slots become reusable, configured in-flight limits remain authoritative, and native single-slot coverage verifies queued requests reuse released state safely. Efficient I/O remains unchecked because binary buffers, general queued socket backpressure, and task-integrated event-loop I/O are still required.

Verified Milestone 28 structured pool-consumption follow-up: `worker.joinAny` lets headless/server code wait for and consume whichever direct child finishes first without a separate completion observation plus join sequence. The operation uses the same compiler-owned completion signalling as concurrent HTTP serving, preserves exact child failure propagation and structured-scope ownership, and avoids application-side polling. Efficient I/O remains unchecked because binary buffers, general queued socket backpressure, and task-integrated event-loop I/O are still required.

Verified WebSocket handshake-boundary follow-up: server request-line/header parsing and client response status parsing now search only within the already bounded handshake byte lengths, using compiler-generated length-delimited byte scans rather than unbounded C string searches. Existing NUL, 65536-byte, header, and loopback handshake regressions remain green; queued backpressure and task-integrated event-loop I/O remain required before Efficient I/O is complete.

# Verified WebSocket server follow-up: the native reader accepts RFC 6455 fragmented text messages, validates continuation sequencing and reserved bits, handles masked ping/pong control frames while a message is in flight, preserves one bounded callback-scoped aggregate, and rejects message-size overflow without introducing escaping storage. Binary frame I/O, client handshake/frame support, and close/ping/pong controls are covered separately; task-integrated event-loop scheduling remains pending under Efficient I/O.

# Verified Efficient I/O safety follow-up: `net.sendText`, `net.sendTextWithTimeout`, `net.sendTextProgress`, and `net.sendTextProgressWithTimeout` now reject null or over-65536-byte borrowed payloads before socket inspection or native transmission, eliminating their prior unbounded `strlen` reads. Generated-C and native coverage pin the guards alongside the existing socket regressions; broader binary-buffer, queued-backpressure, and task-integrated event-loop work remains open.

# Verified binary-buffer boundary follow-up: all compiler-owned TCP/UDP binary send paths now reject a non-empty `i64[]` descriptor with missing native storage before socket inspection or byte projection, including direct, timed, resumable, and peer-addressed sends. This keeps malformed borrowed descriptors from becoming native out-of-bounds reads; owned byte buffers, queued backpressure, and task-integrated event-loop I/O remain open.

# Verified UDP batch receive bounds follow-up: native text and binary UDP batch receivers request datagram-length reporting and reject datagrams larger than the caller's `maxBytes` before inspecting or copying payload storage, preventing silent truncation and out-of-bounds binary-list materialization. Generated-C coverage pins the binary guard; owned byte buffers, queued backpressure, and task-integrated event-loop I/O remain open.

# Verified HTTP text-output bounds follow-up: native HTTP text request/response senders now scan borrowed body strings through the shared 65536-byte bounded helper before socket inspection or transmission, returning explicit size errors instead of relying on an unbounded `strlen` read. Generated-C coverage pins the request helper and the HTTP regression suite remains green; owned byte buffers, queued backpressure, and task-integrated event-loop I/O remain open.

# Verified HTTP text-argument safety follow-up: compiler-generated text request/response senders now reject null method, target, host, content-type, body, and custom-header pointers before dereferencing them, while preserving the existing bounded scans and framing validation. Generated-C regression assertions cover the shared guards; owned byte buffers, queued backpressure, and task-integrated event-loop I/O remain open.

# Verified HTTP text-body UTF-8 follow-up: cancellation-aware fixed-length, chunked, and close-delimited HTTP request/response readers validate the complete bounded body as UTF-8 before lending it to Flux's callback-scoped `str`, while preserving NUL rejection, framing boundaries, partial-progress accounting, and binary-body separation. Native generated-code coverage pins the explicit request/response errors; owned byte buffers and binary HTTP bodies remain pending.

# Verified UDP peer-text bounds follow-up: `net.sendTextTo` and `net.sendTextToParts` now validate borrowed hosts, payloads, and fragments with the same 65536-byte bounded scans used by the rest of the text socket surface before DNS resolution or socket transmission. Generated-C coverage pins the peer-host rejection paths, while the existing native loopback tests remain green; owned byte buffers, queued backpressure, and task-integrated event-loop I/O remain open.

# Verified JSON fixed-escape-width follow-up: compiler-generated JSON string, array, and object encoders copy each selected JSON escape literal using its statically known two-byte width instead of rescanning compiler-owned literals with `strlen`; native output and maximum-expansion coverage remain unchanged, while owned dynamic JSON values and escaping results remain open.

Verified Milestone 29 window/layout hot-apply follow-up: Linux development runs now treat literal application `title`, `width`, `height`, `resizable`, `theme`, and `layoutDirection` value edits plus existing root-grid `gap` and `padding` value edits as ABI-stable data-only changes. The save-triggered runner patches the active GTK window/root grid/settings in process with native bounds/type validation instead of rebuilding it; direction changes include returning an explicit LTR/RTL override to locale-driven system direction, and theme changes preserve the existing Linux light/system/dark preference semantics. Adding/removing declarations, unsupported metadata/layout changes, and structural edits retain the controlled rebuild/restart fallback. Automatic save-triggered hot reload is regression-covered for compatible static UI edits, while broader structural/runtime replacement and cross-platform production coverage remain incomplete.

Verified Milestone 29 theme-palette hot-apply follow-up: Linux development runs treat literal application semantic palette overrides (`surfaceColor`, `surfaceRaisedColor`, `textColor`, `textMutedColor`, `accentColor`, `onAccentColor`, `outlineColor`, `dangerColor`, `successColor`, `warningColor`, and `shadowColor`) as ABI-stable data-only changes. Every generated Linux application now owns the same bounded mutable semantic-palette slots behind its GTK theme provider, so adding an override can patch an existing slot and removing one restores that token's compiler-owned GTK semantic fallback without rebuilding widgets or restarting the app. User-provided patch payloads remain hex-validated; restore operations use a compiler-only sentinel that selects the hardcoded per-token fallback, and high-contrast integration remains attached throughout.

Verified Milestone 29 system-theme restoration follow-up: Linux applications snapshot GTK's existing `gtk-application-prefer-dark-theme` value before applying an explicit Flux theme override. Save-triggered `theme` patches can therefore move through `dark`, `light`, and back to `system` without forcing light mode: `system` restores the captured platform preference in process while explicit values continue to override it.

Verified Milestone 29 live-runner hot-apply follow-up: the native Linux development runner now has end-to-end regression coverage that starts a real GTK application under a virtual display, edits a static UI value on disk, observes the `in_process_string` reload status, and proves the application's `onStart` callback is not invoked again. This pins the save watcher, incremental analysis, patch-file dispatch, SIGUSR1 delivery, GTK-side patch application path, and no-restart contract together instead of testing those layers only in isolation.

Verified Milestone 29 validation-only overlay follow-up: `grid overlay` is now excluded from the development ABI and native application-fragment cache identity because it only controls compile-time sibling-overlap validation and is never consumed by native codegen. Toggling it on a layout that remains valid therefore keeps the running process and emitted native code unchanged while reusing the application/runtime fragments; layouts that become invalid when overlay is disabled still fail during analysis as before.

Verified Milestone 29 root-spacing lifecycle follow-up: root `gap` and `padding` are now ABI-stable whether their declarations are present or absent. Development analysis always exposes their effective values (`12` and `20` when omitted) and removes the declaration lines from the masked source comparison, so adding, changing, or removing either declaration can hot-apply in process. Explicit defaults are recognized as a zero-runtime-work source change, while removing non-default values patches the live grid back to the implicit defaults. Native application-fragment identity likewise uses effective spacing values so explicit defaults do not invalidate reusable runtime/application fragments.

Verified Milestone 29 application-metadata lifecycle follow-up: literal `title`, `resizable`, `theme`, and `layoutDirection` declarations now use compiler-owned effective development defaults even when omitted (root-view name, `true`, `system`, and `system` respectively). Adding an explicit default is an ABI-stable in-process no-op, adding a non-default value hot-applies the existing GTK window/settings/root-grid state, and removing the declaration patches the running application back to its implicit default without a restart. Application identity and callback metadata remain structural restart boundaries.

Verified Milestone 29 window-size lifecycle follow-up: literal application `width` and `height` declarations can now be added or removed without crossing the development ABI. When one side of a save transition omits a dimension, development patch comparison derives that side's exact compiler-owned bootstrap dimension from the root grid only for the declaration lifecycle being compared; ordinary edits where both dimensions remain omitted therefore do not acquire surprise resize patches. Explicit dimensions equal to the implicit size are in-process no-ops, non-default additions update the existing GTK window, and removals restore the implicit bootstrap dimension.

Verified Milestone 29 visibility lifecycle follow-up: literal element `visible` declarations are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. Development analysis always models the implicit value as `true`, removes literal visibility declaration lines from masked-source identity, and keeps a native visibility patch handler available even when the original source omitted the property. Adding `visible: true` is an in-process no-op, adding/changing `visible: false` updates the existing widget or transition revealer, and removing the declaration restores visibility without a restart; dynamic/state-driven visibility remains structural and follows the normal rebuild path.

Verified Milestone 29 enabled-state lifecycle follow-up: literal `enabled` declarations on Button, TextInput, Toggle, and Radio are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. Development analysis models the compiler-owned implicit value as `true`, masks literal declaration lines from development source identity, and emits the GTK sensitivity patch path even when the original source omitted `enabled`. Adding `enabled: true` is an in-process no-op, adding/changing `enabled: false` updates the existing native control, and removing the declaration restores sensitivity without a restart; dynamic/state-driven enablement remains structural and follows the controlled rebuild path.

Verified Milestone 29 primary-button lifecycle follow-up: literal Button `primary` declarations are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. The implicit compiler-owned value is `false`, the live GTK suggested-action patch path exists even when `primary` was omitted at launch, explicit `primary: false` is a zero-work source edit, `primary: true` updates the existing button style in process, and removing the declaration restores the ordinary button style. State-driven primary styling remains structural and uses controlled restart.

Verified Milestone 29 accessibility-hidden lifecycle follow-up: literal `accessibilityHidden` declarations are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. Development analysis models the implicit accessible state as `false`, keeps the GTK accessible-state patch path available even when the property was omitted at launch, treats explicit `false` as a no-op, hot-applies `true` to the existing native accessibility object, and restores the implicit visible-to-accessibility state when the declaration is removed. State-driven accessibility visibility remains structural and uses controlled restart.

Verified Milestone 29 TextInput read-only lifecycle follow-up: literal `readOnly` declarations are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted for both single-line and multiline inputs. The compiler-owned default remains `false`; the live GTK editable/accessibility patch path is emitted even when `readOnly` was omitted at launch, explicit `false` is a no-op, `true` updates the existing entry/text view in process, and removing the declaration restores editability. State-driven read-only mode remains structural and uses controlled restart.

Verified Milestone 29 image-shrink lifecycle follow-up: literal Image `canShrink` declarations are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. Development analysis models the compiler-owned implicit value as `false`, keeps the GTK picture shrink patch path available even when the property was omitted at launch, treats explicit `false` as a no-op, hot-applies `true` to the existing native picture, and restores the implicit non-shrinking state when the declaration is removed. State-driven image shrink behavior remains structural and uses controlled restart.

Verified Milestone 29 image-fit lifecycle follow-up: literal Image `fit` declarations are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. The compiler-owned omitted value remains `contain`; the live GTK content-fit patch handler is emitted even when `fit` was absent at launch, explicit `contain` is an in-process no-op, other validated fit modes hot-apply to the existing picture, and removing the declaration restores `contain`. Runtime/state-driven fit expressions remain structural and use controlled restart.

Verified Milestone 29 image-alt lifecycle follow-up: literal Image `alt` declarations are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. The compiler-owned omitted alternative text is empty; the existing GTK accessibility patch path is available from launch, explicit empty alternative text is an in-process no-op, literal descriptions hot-apply to the existing picture, and removing the declaration restores the empty default. State-driven alternative text remains structural and uses controlled restart.

Verified Milestone 29 image-source lifecycle follow-up: literal Image `source` declarations are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. The omitted source is the compiler-owned empty value; the GTK runner keeps the bounded image-source patch path available from launch, loads non-empty literal paths/assets into the existing picture, and clears the live paintable when a source is removed or reset to empty. State-driven image sources remain structural and use controlled restart.

Verified Milestone 29 TextInput validation-state lifecycle follow-up: literal `validationState` declarations are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. The compiler-owned omitted state is `normal`; Linux development runners retain the validation-class patch handler even when validation styling was absent at launch, hot-apply validated error/success/warning states to the existing input, and remove live validation styling when the declaration is deleted. State-driven validation remains structural and uses controlled restart.

Verified Milestone 29 single-line TextInput placeholder lifecycle follow-up: literal `placeholder` declarations on single-line inputs are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. The omitted value is empty text and the GTK entry placeholder setter is retained from launch, so add/change/remove edits hot-apply without rebuilding; state-driven placeholders remain structural. Multiline placeholder add/remove deliberately keeps controlled restart because its compiler-owned overlay and change callback must be constructed at startup.

Verified Milestone 29 single-line TextInput max-length lifecycle follow-up: literal `maxLength` declarations on single-line inputs are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. The omitted/unlimited value is `0` in GTK, so add/change/remove edits hot-apply through the retained entry setter without rebuilding. State-driven limits remain structural, and multiline add/remove deliberately keeps controlled restart because its compiler-owned insert callback and mutable limit storage are only constructed when the property is present at launch.

Verified Milestone 29 default-Text max-width lifecycle follow-up: literal `maxWidthChars` declarations on Text without an explicit semantic `variant` are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. Omitted Text inherits the compiler-owned `body` max width of `72`, the GTK width patch setter is retained from launch, and removing an override restores that semantic default. State-driven widths and add/remove overrides beside an explicit variant remain structural so variant/override precedence cannot drift in the live process.

Verified Milestone 29 Text max-lines lifecycle follow-up: literal `maxLines` declarations are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. Development comparison uses an internal `0` sentinel only for the omitted/unlimited state, while source declarations remain validated at one line or greater; the retained GTK patch handler maps that sentinel back to native unlimited lines on removal. State-driven line limits remain structural and use controlled restart.

Verified Milestone 29 Text ellipsize lifecycle follow-up: literal `ellipsize` declarations are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. The compiler/native omitted state is `none`; the GTK ellipsize patch handler is retained from launch, explicit `none` is an in-process no-op, start/middle/end modes hot-apply to the existing label, and removal restores no ellipsizing. State-driven truncation remains structural and uses controlled restart.

Verified Milestone 29 Text letter-spacing lifecycle follow-up: literal `letterSpacing` declarations are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. Omitted Text has zero additional tracking; the GTK Pango-attribute patch path is retained from launch, explicit zero is an in-process no-op, validated positive or negative spacing hot-applies to the existing label, and removal restores zero tracking. State-driven letter spacing remains structural and uses controlled restart.

Verified Milestone 29 Text color lifecycle follow-up: literal `color` declarations are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. The GTK color provider/foreground-cleanup path is retained from launch; literal semantic/hex colors hot-apply, while declaration removal uses a compiler-private empty lifecycle value to clear the per-element CSS provider and remove stale Pango foreground attributes so the ordinary themed/high-contrast `.flux-text` rules regain control exactly. State-driven color remains a controlled restart boundary.

Verified Milestone 29 background-color lifecycle follow-up: literal `backgroundColor` declarations are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. Each Linux element retains its compiler-owned property-specific CSS provider and patch dispatch path from launch; semantic/hex colors hot-apply, while declaration removal uses a compiler-private empty lifecycle value to clear only that provider so the underlying native/theme styling resumes exactly. State-driven background colors remain a controlled restart boundary.

Verified Milestone 29 tooltip lifecycle follow-up: literal `tooltip` declarations now hot-add, hot-edit, and hot-remove without changing the development ABI when they do not compete with TextInput validation messaging. Linux tooltip dispatch is retained from launch and the compiler-private omitted value clears GTK tooltip state with `NULL`; state-driven tooltips and TextInput tooltip/validation-message precedence changes remain controlled restart boundaries.

Verified Milestone 29 Text font-family lifecycle follow-up: literal `fontFamily` declarations are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. The GTK family patch path is retained from launch; literal families hot-apply through Pango, while declaration removal uses a compiler-private empty lifecycle value to remove the Pango family attribute and restore native/inherited font selection exactly. Empty source literals remain invalid, and state-driven font families retain controlled restart.

Verified Milestone 29 Text variant lifecycle follow-up: literal semantic `variant` declarations without explicit size/bold/line-height/max-width overrides are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. Omission is the compiler-owned `body` variant; the semantic typography patch helper and per-label dispatch path are retained from launch, explicit body is an in-process no-op, other semantic variants hot-apply as one coordinated typography update, and removal restores body defaults. Variant declaration lifecycle beside explicit semantic typography overrides remains a controlled restart boundary so precedence cannot drift.

Verified Milestone 29 default-Text size lifecycle follow-up: literal `size` declarations on Text without an explicit semantic `variant` are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. Omitted Text inherits the body semantic size of 16; the GTK Pango size patch path is retained from launch, explicit 16 is an in-process no-op, validated overrides hot-apply, and removal restores size 16. State-driven values and explicit variant/override precedence remain structural and use controlled restart.

Verified Milestone 29 default-Text line-height lifecycle follow-up: literal `lineHeightPercent` declarations on Text without an explicit semantic `variant` are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. Omitted Text inherits the body semantic line height of 140%; the GTK Pango line-height patch path is retained from launch, explicit 140 is an in-process no-op, validated overrides hot-apply, and removal restores 140%. State-driven values and explicit variant/override precedence remain structural and use controlled restart.

Verified Milestone 29 Text alignment lifecycle follow-up: literal `textAlign` declarations are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. The compiler/native omitted state is left alignment; the GTK justification patch handler is retained from launch, explicit `left` is an in-process no-op, center/right/fill hot-apply to the existing label, and removal restores left alignment. State-driven alignment remains structural and uses controlled restart.

Verified Milestone 29 Text wrap-mode lifecycle follow-up: literal `wrapMode` declarations are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. The compiler/native omitted mode is word wrapping; the GTK wrap-mode patch handler is retained from launch, explicit `word` is an in-process no-op, character and word-character modes hot-apply to the existing label, and removal restores word wrapping. State-driven wrap modes remain structural and use controlled restart.

Verified Milestone 29 selectable-text lifecycle follow-up: literal Text `selectable` declarations are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. Development analysis models the native/compiler-owned omitted state as `false`, emits the GTK selectable patch path even when the property was absent at launch, treats explicit `false` as a no-op, applies `true` to the existing label in process, and restores non-selectable text when the declaration is removed. State-driven selectability remains structural and uses controlled restart.

Verified Milestone 29 text-wrap lifecycle follow-up: literal Text `wrap` declarations are now ABI-stable whether present or omitted. The compiler-owned portable default remains `true`; the GTK wrap patch path is emitted even when the property was absent at launch, explicit `true` is a no-op, `false` updates the existing label in process, and removing the declaration restores wrapping. State-driven wrap behavior remains structural and uses controlled restart.

Verified Milestone 29 shortcut-scope lifecycle follow-up: elements with a compile-time `shortcut` now treat literal `shortcutScope` declarations as ABI-stable whether present or omitted. The compiler-owned omitted scope is `window`; Linux development runners retain the GTK shortcut-controller scope patch path from launch, explicit `window` is an in-process no-op, `focused` hot-applies to the existing controller, and declaration removal restores window scope without replacing the shortcut or restarting the process. Elements without a shortcut remain structural so hot reload cannot synthesize shortcut machinery that was absent at launch.

Verified Milestone 29 focus-scope lifecycle follow-up: literal common `focusScope` declarations now hot-add, hot-edit, and hot-remove without changing the development ABI. Linux development runners retain the compiler-owned native focus-scope metadata patch path from launch; adding a non-negative scope updates the existing control, while declaration removal uses a compiler-private `-1` lifecycle sentinel to clear the native metadata and restore the ordinary unscoped state. Source-level negative scope values remain rejected by normal semantic validation.

Verified Milestone 29 TextInput keyboard-type lifecycle follow-up: literal `keyboardType` declarations now hot-add, hot-edit, and hot-remove on Linux without changing the development ABI. Every native TextInput keeps a lightweight compiler-owned flag tracking whether an explicit keyboard type is active, so removing the declaration restores the true omitted input purpose in process: free-form text normally, or password purpose when a single-line input is currently masked. Subsequent password hot patches consult that live flag instead of stale build-time declaration presence, preserving explicit keyboard-type precedence across repeated saves; source-level keyboard values remain restricted to the portable validated vocabulary.

# Milestone 29 — Flux 1.0 stability

- [x] Stable grammar: the accepted Flux source grammar now has an explicit compatibility version (`GRAMMAR_VERSION = 1`), `flux grammar --version` exposes that version to tooling without human-output scraping, and `docs/grammar.md` defines the version-1 compatibility contract for additive syntax, breaking parse/token/precedence/keyword changes, and separation from formatter/diagnostic/package-format versioning. Regression coverage pins the library constant and user-facing CLI result.
- [x] Stable type/memory model: compatibility version 1 is exposed through `flux memory --version`, and `docs/type-memory-model.md` defines the stable `Copy`/`Send`, move, immutable-borrow, allocation, rejected-shape, and breaking-change contracts without claiming unfinished owned values or escaping closures are safe.
- [x] Stable package format: `flux.toml` now has an explicit compiler-owned package-format compatibility version (`PACKAGE_FORMAT_VERSION = 1`); `flux new` writes `format_version = 1`, existing unversioned manifests remain version-1 compatible, and explicitly unsupported versions fail before build rather than being silently reinterpreted. The versioning/additive-change contract is documented in `docs/package-format.md` and covered by manifest/scaffolding regression tests.
- [x] Stable native/platform ABI policy: `NATIVE_ABI_POLICY_VERSION = 1` is exposed through `flux abi --version`; `docs/native-abi.md` defines the stable manifest-backed C export ABI v2, the legacy direct-source ABI v1 boundary, explicit break/additive versioning rules, ownership/layout exclusions for unfinished value categories, and the rule that compiler-generated platform/runtime glue remains private unless separately documented as a public interoperability contract. Regression coverage pins both the policy version and current package C ABI version so tooling/native consumers can detect incompatible contract changes instead of inferring stability from compiler implementation details.
- [x] Stable UI API compatibility policy: the compiler-owned portable UI surface now has explicit compatibility version `UI_API_VERSION = 1`, exposed through `flux ui --version` for tooling. `docs/ui-compatibility.md` defines the versioned contract across built-in elements/properties/types, events, view state/derived state, layout directives, portable focus/input/accessibility semantics, semantic presentation values, and UI environment bindings; additive source-compatible UI growth may remain within a version, while removals/renames/type/default/callback/portable-behavior changes require a version bump. Native backend rewrites remain free to optimize or change toolkit implementation details without perturbing the portable source API contract.
- [x] Stable formatter: canonical formatting is deterministic/idempotent, the CLI/LSP/library share one implementation, `flux format --check` provides the CI contract, and formatter compatibility is explicitly versioned through public `FORMATTER_VERSION` plus `flux format --version`. Version 1 changes only when already-canonical source intentionally acquires different canonical bytes; the stability contract and editor/CI guidance are documented in `docs/formatting.md` and regression-tested.
- [x] Stable diagnostics contract for tooling: machine analysis output is pinned to documented `schema_version: 1`, exposes one shared versioned envelope for successful and failed `flux check --json` / `flux analyze --json` runs, ships a checked-in JSON Schema, keeps diagnostic/span/label/note/fix field meanings explicit, and defines additive-vs-breaking compatibility rules in `docs/diagnostics.md`. The version constant and envelope serializer are public so compiler-integrating tools do not need to duplicate the wire contract.
- [ ] Android, iOS, Windows, macOS, Linux, web, and headless/server targets meet documented support tiers.
- [x] Production-quality LSP/debugger/profiler. The compiler-native LSP, source-level debugger, and opt-in profiler/devtools workflows are implemented through the user-facing `flux` commands, share the compiler's source/diagnostic/build model, and are covered by the complete Rust/compiler suite plus native debugger/profiler regressions.
- [ ] Automatic save-triggered hot reload is production-ready for development workflows.
- [x] Package ecosystem and platform interoperability are documented and tested in [`docs/package-interoperability.md`](docs/package-interoperability.md), with regression coverage for dependency resolution/lockfiles/cache isolation, native package metadata and C headers, target-selected modules, and direct platform lowering.
- [x] Flux can build substantial real applications without resorting to an OOP architecture.
